- *Not built.* Guide curves, tangency at the ends, a section that is a single point (a tip), profiles with holes (loft the hole on its own and cut it out), and analytic recognition of a smooth loft that happens to be a surface of revolution (it is a B-spline surface).

**Sweep along a path (2026-10-14, M9).** `doc::Sweep { sketch, path }` is the solid the one closed profile of a sketch sweeps out as it is carried along the curves of another sketch, staying square to them (`feature.sweep`; `kernel::sweep`, `regen/src/sweep.rs`). The profile may have holes (a pipe with its bore); the path is in a plane.
- *The path* is every curve of its sketch that is not construction: lines, arcs and splines that meet end to end into one chain, open or closed, or one circle. A spline with corners (a polyline) is the pieces between them. Where two curves meet they are tangent or, two straight lines only, at a corner, which is mitred: both sides end on the plane that bisects the corner. The order and the direction the curves were drawn in do not matter.
- *Kinks* (2026-10-15). Two curves that meet a little off tangent (a path from rounded coordinates) count as tangent when the profile can be turned through the kink within the tolerance; between two lines only within `sweep::SMOOTH` (1e-6 rad), since a mitre is exact. The exact sweep through a corner turns the profile about the path's binormal there, from square to the piece before to square to the piece after. Turning it through a small angle `a` moves each point of it along the path, which is along the faces either piece sweeps, to first order; off those faces it moves by `reach (1 - cos a)` at most (`reach`: how far the profile reaches to the side of the path in its plane). That second-order movement is the test: at most half the linear tolerance (`kink_movement`). The first-order `reach sin a` is not a distance any point of the body is off (the turbofan's tube has a 2.9e-6 rad kink across a profile of radius 5: 1.5e-5 first order, 2e-11 second). How the kink is absorbed, deterministically and with every piece an exact sweep: the profile stays square to the piece before the kink until a line lays it square again. A line before a kink ends square to the piece after it (its end ring is the profile slid along the line onto that plane and normalised, `reach (1 - cos)` in towards the path), so the next piece starts square. A line after a kink slides the profile as it arrives, which is an exact prism. An arc after a kink carries the profile round its own axis (the true centre, not one taken from the arriving station) turned as it arrives: its faces are surfaces of revolution of the turned curves, and a point `q` towards the axis is then `radius q (1 - cos) / (radius - q)` off the square sweep (`tilted_turn`; B-spline surfaces of revolution are that exactly, tori, cylinders and cones are put through the turned curve's centre or ends); its rails start at each vertex's own angle. Kinks before arcs add up until a line squares the profile; that sum is checked against half the tolerance at each arc and at each line's end. A spline after a kink starts from the turned profile and is held to half the tolerance of the true sweep by its own measure. A path that ends on an arc still turned by a kink is refused unless its end cap is within half the tolerance of square (`reach sin a`, first order, since the cap is a face of its own). A closed path whose last piece is a curve and whose first is a line starts with the profile square to the last piece, so it closes on itself. Beyond the tolerance a kink is a corner as before: mitred between two lines, refused otherwise, with the angle and the curves. *Measured against the exact sweep* (2026-10-15, `crates/kernel/tests/sweep_kinks_measured.rs`, independently of the construction): for line→arc, arc→line, arc→arc and arc→spline kinks, round and rectangular profiles, at scales 1e-3, 1 and 1e6, at the largest kink the sweep admits (found by bisection on `sweep` itself: the rule's own for three of them, 0.894 of it for arc→arc, where `tilted_turn` asks for less; a kink 1 % larger is refused), points of the B-rep faces (placed by the mesh, then projected onto the face's own surface and kept on the face's side of its edges) and of every edge are at most half the tolerance from the exact sweep (the profile carried square along the drawn pieces and turned about the vertex at each kink, in closed form: the points within `r` of the path for a circle, within `w` in plan and `h` in height for a rectangle), and every point of the exact sweep's boundary is at most half the tolerance from the body (an upper bound: along the exact normal, the farthest crossing with a face's surface that lies on that face). Both ways 0.500 tol to four digits, at every scale; the measure sees a mitre of 0.02 rad as the `reach (1/cos(a/2) - 1)` it is, to 2 %.
- *Where the profile sits.* The path has to pass through the profile's plane at a right angle: an open path at one of its ends (the one on the plane; of two, the one nearer the centre of the profile), which is where the sweep starts; a closed path anywhere (of several crossings the one nearest the centre of the profile). The profile need not be centred on the path nor contain it: each of its points keeps its place in the frame the path carries (the path's direction, its normal to the left in its plane, the normal of that plane; a path in a plane has no twist to choose). A profile whose plane leans off square is laid into the plane that is square (2026-10-15), by a rigid motion (the sketch's origin to its foot on that plane, its x axis to the direction of its shadow there), when that moves no point of the profile by more than the document's linear tolerance; the movement is measured exactly at the corners of the profile's bounding box, which bound it because the motion is affine. The body is then the exact sweep of the laid-in profile, valid as any other, and since every station carries the profile rigidly no point of it is farther than that movement from the sweep of the profile as drawn. A larger lean is refused with the angle and the movement (at the default tolerance of 1e-6 a profile of radius 5 may lean about 2e-7 rad). A lean whose cause is an arc of a `sketch.path` with an end off its circle no longer arises when that end is given as coordinates within 1 % of the radius: the command puts it on the circle (`snapped`); otherwise it reports it (`warnings`).
- *Pieces.* One body, not a union: along a line the profile slides, along an arc it turns about the arc's axis, along a spline it follows the spline's frames, and the pieces share the profile's edges where they meet. A line of the profile sweeps a plane along a line of the path, and a cylinder (square to the path's plane) or a cone (slanted) round an arc; an arc or a circle sweeps a cylinder and a torus (a rational surface of revolution when its circle reaches the arc's axis: a torus would be a spindle); a spline sweeps a B-spline surface along a line and a rational B-spline surface of revolution round an arc. All of that is exact. **A line of the profile that is parallel to the path's plane sweeps one plane along the whole path, whatever the path does: that is one face** (with two loops on a closed path), not a face per piece. A profile that is one circle has no seam along lines and arcs: its faces are bounded by the circles (ellipses at a mitre) between the pieces alone, as an extruded cylinder is. A circle that is the whole path is two half turns, and a spline that closes on itself two pieces, so that no face goes all the way round and meets itself.
- *Along a spline* the swept surface is not a B-spline surface, and it is approximated within the document's tolerance. Each polynomial span of the path (the true surface is only once differentiable where spans meet) is followed in one or more parts; along each part the surface is one polynomial of degree 5, 7 or 9 through the profile placed in the exact frames of the path at the ends of the part and at the Chebyshev points between them (`geom::refine::interpolation` and the loft's skinning). Degrees and parts are raised until every surface is within **half the linear tolerance** of the true sweep, measured at three places between each two stations in a row: a point of the surface there is compared with the point of the profile at the same parameter, placed in the exact frame of the path whose plane contains it. What was reached is on the feature (`FeatureResult::sweep`: `deviation`, `stations`, `length`, `closed`; `document.inspect` and the result of `feature.sweep` show it under `path`). The ends of a spline piece are exact frames, so caps and neighbouring pieces are exact. A whole circle of the profile is four quarter arcs when the path has a spline in it. (The first attempt was a cubic through evenly spaced stations, which is what a loft is: its faces had a knot line per station, and `topo::mass_properties` gave up on some of them, "a boundary integral that does not settle". The S-shaped spline of the tests, three spans, is followed through 43 stations at a tolerance of a millionth of its size: two parts of degree 7 per span.)
- *Soundness.* Refused in the kernel before anything is built, with the curves of the path named as the sketch names them: a bend whose axis the profile reaches (`the bend e6 is too tight for the profile: its radius is …, and the profile reaches … towards its centre`); a spline whose radius of curvature comes within 2 % of the profile's reach towards the centre of curvature (sampled at 64 places per span; the message has the place and the radius); a mitre so sharp on a line so short that the mitres at its two ends would cross within the profile (the corner is named); a corner at an arc or a spline; a path that turns straight back; a path that branches or is in several parts; a path that does not start on the profile's plane or crosses it at a slant; a closed path that does not close when it is followed. Then everything `topo::check` knows. A sweep whose path comes back near itself passes through itself without folding anywhere: regeneration runs the same self-intersection check as for a loft (`regen/src/selfcheck.rs`, shared by both) and fails the feature with the two faces that cross.
- *Names.* A side is `F9:side(e3,e12)`: the curve of the profile, then the curve of the path. The one face of a line that is parallel to the path's plane is `F9:side(e3)`. The ends of an open path are `F9:startCap(…)` and `F9:endCap(…)` after the curves of the profile's outer loop. Where the same two curves give several faces (a circle that is the whole path, a polyline's pieces, a quartered circle along a spline, a spline cut at its corners) the later ones are numbered in the order they are made, piece of the path by piece and round the profile within one: `F9:piece(1)/F9:side(e3,e12)`. Edges and vertices are named by the faces they lie on, as for a loft.
- *Failures.* A profile's sketch that is missing, suppressed, later in the history or failed fails the sweep with `sketch: …`, the path's with `path: …`, each with `causedBy`; so does a sketch with no closed profile or with several. `feature.edit` takes `sketch` and `path`.
- *Not built.* A path that is not in one plane other than a helix (below; a 3D polyline or spline needs a rule for how the profile turns about the path: rotation-minimising frames, [`decisions.md`](decisions.md)), a profile that is not square to the path, several profiles at once, twist and scale along the path, guide curves, a corner at an arc (it would not be flat), and recognising a spline that happens to be a line or an arc.

**Helix, and the sweep along it (2026-10-14, M9).** `doc::DatumHelix { axis, start, pitch, extent, left_handed }` is a datum curve (`datum.helix`; `kernel::helix`, `regen/src/datum.rs`): about an axis (`AxisRef`: a world axis, an edge between two faces, a datum axis), starting at a radius level with the axis's own point (towards the first of the world's x, y and z axes that is least along the axis) or at a point off the axis (`HelixStart`), advancing `pitch` per turn for `turns` or a `height` (`HelixExtent`), counter-clockwise seen from the tip of the axis unless left-handed. Its result is `Datum::Helix(kernel::helix::Helix)` (origin on the axis level with the start, unit axis, unit start direction, radius, pitch, turns, handedness); `datum.helix` and `document.inspect` show it with its closed-form length `turns · √((2πr)² + p²)`. The viewport draws it as a curve, as a spline of a sketch (`scene.rs` › `scene_helix`, chords within the scene's tolerance). A helix is not a rational curve (a rational curve is algebraic, and a whole helix meets a plane through its axis infinitely often), and `geom` has no helix kind: the kinds are the boolean's, the tessellator's and STEP's to know, and none of them needs one. Where a curve is needed (`Helix::curve`) it is a polynomial B-spline in parts of at most a quarter turn, each a polynomial of degree 5, 7 or 9 through the helix at the part's ends and at the Chebyshev points between; the advance along the axis is linear in the angle and reproduced exactly, so the distance to the helix is the radius times the same polynomial's distance to the unit circle (`helix::circle_error`, measured once per plan: 2.0e-5 for quarter turns of degree 5, 5.5e-8 of degree 7, 9.5e-11 of degree 9), and the plan is the cheapest that keeps that within a quarter of the tolerance.
- *The sweep along a helix* is `feature.sweep` with the helix as its `path` (`doc::Sweep` unchanged: `path` is a sketch or a datum helix; `kernel::helix::sweep_helix`). **The profile is carried by the helix's screw motion:** it turns about the axis and advances along it, a pitch per turn, as a rigid body. That is the helix's own frame that keeps one axis pointing at the helix's axis (the Frenet frame of a helix does, and is carried by the same motion), not a rotation-minimising frame, which turns against it by the torsion times the length: a thread's tooth drawn in a plane through the axis would leave that plane and the thread would not be the same after a turn. The profile lies in any plane through the start of the helix that the motion crosses everywhere: through the axis (a thread), square to the helix (a spring's wire), square to the axis (a flight). Every turn is the first moved along the axis by the pitch.
- *Faces.* Each curve of the profile sweeps one face per half turn (a whole circle of the profile is four quarters first), the later ones numbered as pieces in the order they are made (`F9:side(e3)`, `F9:piece(1)/F9:side(e3)`, …); the ends are the profile at the start and where the motion leaves it, both flat (`F9:startCap(…)`, `F9:endCap(…)`). A line of the profile parallel to the axis sweeps a cylinder, exactly. Every other face is a B-spline surface through the profile placed at stations along the half turn by the screw motion, with the band of the sweep along a spline (`sweep::part_band`): two or more parts per half turn, of degree 5, 7 or 9, starting from the plan the profile's reach needs and raised until the surfaces lie within **half the linear tolerance** of the true sweep, measured at three places between each two stations and nine across the profile against the point the screw motion puts there. Since the motion is rigid and every column curve has the same knots and weights, the distance is the reach from the axis times the plan's error on the unit circle, so it is predicted and then checked: the spring of the tests (wire 1 on radius 10) is 1.0e-9 off at a tolerance of 1e-6, with degree 9 in quarter turns (91 stations over two and a half turns), and its volume is the closed form to 4e-13. `FeatureResult::sweep` has the deviation, the length of the helix and the stations.
- *Run-out.* `kernel::helix::RunOut { angle, rise }` carries the profile on past the end of the helix while it moves away from the axis by a rise that grows with the square of the angle (it leaves the helix tangentially); used by the thread. A negative rise moves it towards the axis (an internal thread's groove running out into the hole, 2026-10-15); one that would bring the profile to the axis is refused.
- *Soundness.* Refused in the kernel before anything is built: a profile that reaches the axis, or round it (more than a quarter turn from the start, seen along the axis: a profile across the axis or behind it); a profile whose plane the motion runs along somewhere (the speed of the motion across the plane is affine in the point, so its sign is checked on the profile's boundary; the sweep would fold); a pitch not longer than the profile is along the axis as the motion sees it (the extent of `z − (p/2π) φ` over the profile: one turn would run into the next); a helix that does not start on the profile's plane (within the tolerance). Then `topo::check`, and regeneration's self-intersection check as for every sweep.
- *Volume.* The swept volume is `θ · A · n·v(c)`: the speed of the motion across the profile's plane is affine in the point, so its mean over the profile is its value at the centre of area. For a profile in a plane through the axis that is Pappus (`A · 2π x̄ · turns`), for one square to the helix with its centre on it the area times the helix's length, exactly and for any size of wire as long as the sweep does not pass through itself. These are the tests' closed forms.

**Thread (2026-10-14, M9).** `doc::Thread { face, start, size: Option<ThreadSize>, pitch: Option<Expr>, length, left_handed, modeled }` puts an ISO metric thread on the cylindrical face `face` (external on a shank; internal in a hole since 2026-10-15, below) of the body of an earlier feature, which goes into the thread: cosmetic by default (the face is marked with it), cut into the body when `modeled` (since 2026-10-14; before, a thread was cut unless `cosmetic`) (`feature.thread`; `kernel::thread`, `regen/src/thread.rs`).
- *Where.* `face` and `start` may be named as an earlier body had them and are followed to its newest form (*References that follow a body*). On the axis of the face, from where the axis passes through the flat face `start` (square to the axis at an end of the cylinder: the end of a shank, with nothing of the body beyond it) along the cylinder for `length` at full depth, then a run-out of a quarter turn.
- *Size and pitch, found at every regeneration* (`regen/src/thread.rs` › `resolve`), so that a thread on a shank made from a variable follows it. `size` is one of three: `ThreadSize::Designation` (`M10`: ISO 261, `M` and the nominal diameter in millimetres, whatever the document's unit); `ThreadSize::Diameter` (the nominal diameter as an expression of a length, `d`; named by the table's designation when it is one of the table's sizes within the tolerance, else `M` and its millimetres, `M9`); or `None`, the size of the table (`kernel::thread::iso_261_sizes`, every size of ISO 261 from M1 to M68, those with fine pitches only included) that would take the face if it were given (the margins below), the nearest when two would; a face no size takes fails naming its diameter and the sizes of the table on either side (`size: F2:face(0) is 9 mm across, which is the nominal diameter of no ISO metric size of the table (the nearest: M8 (8 mm), M10 (10 mm)); give `size` …`). `pitch` is the expression given, which stays when the size changes, or `None`: the coarse pitch of the table for the size, looked up again each time (a size without one, `M15`, fails asking for `pitch` and naming its fine pitches, `kernel::thread::fine_pitches`; the coarse table, `iso_sizes`, has every size of ISO 261 that has a coarse pitch, M1.1, M2.2, M4.5, M7, M9, M11, … since 2026-10-15). The command reads `size` as a designation when the text is one (`M` and a number), refuses a text that starts like one but is none (`M10x1.25`), and takes any other text as an expression; a number is refused (it would be in the document's unit, a designation is in millimetres). `ThreadReport` has the designation found and `pitch_given`. The dependencies are the usual ones: the face's body, and the variables of `size` and `pitch` when they are expressions (`FeatureKind::exprs`).
- *The profile* is the basic one of ISO 68-1 for the size: 60° flanks, `H = √3/2 P`, the crest flat `P/8` at the major diameter `D`, the root flat `P/4` at the minor diameter `D − 5H/4 = D − 1.0825 P`. Its groove (the trapezoid between two teeth) is carried on past the major diameter by `H/16`, so that it breaks through the shank's surface and stays narrower than the pitch (`15P/16`). The face has to be of the nominal diameter, within half the tooth's depth below it and `H/32` above it.
- *How.* The groove is swept along a helix at the minor radius by the screw motion, starting a pitch before the start plane (outside the material) and lifting out of the material over the run-out (the root rises `5H/8 + H/8`), and subtracted from the body by the boolean. The root is a cylinder; the flanks are B-spline surfaces within half the tolerance; the boolean meets them with the shank's cylinder, the lead chamfer's cone and the end face. An M10 shank of 20 with 12 of thread: 55 faces, 4 s for the boolean.
- *Cosmetic, the default.* Without `modeled` the body is the source body, every face, edge and vertex a copy; the thread is only said: `FeatureResult::thread` (`ThreadReport`: size, major and minor diameter, pitch, whether the pitch was given, length, handedness, cosmetic, the groove's deviation), shown by `feature.thread`, `feature.edit` and `document.inspect` under `thread`. It is not exported to STEP (AP214 has no clean entity for it that FreeCAD reads; a property on the face would be a convention of our own).
- *Names.* The faces left of the body are copies, instance 0 (`F9:copy(0)/F2:side(e7)`); the groove's faces are the thread's own, by the new name role `thread`: `F9:thread(0)` the root, `F9:thread(1)` the flank that faces the start, `F9:thread(2)` the flank that faces away, one per half turn, the later ones pieces (`F9:piece(3)/F9:thread(1)`). The groove starts outside the material, so its first pieces are not left (an M10 × 12 thread starts at `F9:piece(1)/F9:thread(2)` and `F9:piece(2)/F9:thread(0)`). Edges and vertices are named by their faces, as after a boolean.
- *Checked.* The removed volume is the closed form to 8e-7 mm³ in 164 mm³, the minor diameter by a section to the bit; a screw whose sizes all follow a variable `d` is driven through d = 6, 8, 10, 12 mm with its thread following (`crates/engine/tests/thread_follows.rs`).
- *Failures.* A face that is not a cylinder, not of the size's diameter, a start that is not flat, not square to the axis or not at an end of the cylinder, a thread whose length and run-out are longer than the cylinder, a pitch of 0 or of the diameter or more, a size that is no designation and no length, a face of no size of the table when no size is given, a size without a coarse pitch when no pitch is given: each fails the feature with the parameter. `feature.edit` takes `size`, `pitch`, `length`, `leftHanded` and `modeled` (or `cosmetic`, its opposite, the name of before; both given and disagreeing is refused); `size: null` goes back to the face's size, `pitch: null` to the coarse pitch.
- *Internal threads* (2026-10-15). A cylindrical face whose material is outside it (the face's normal, with its `reversed`, points at the axis) is a hole, and its thread internal: no field of its own, so no format change. A hole takes a size from the minor diameter less half the groove's overshoot up to the tap drill's diameter `D − P` and an eighth of a pitch (M10: 8.33 to 8.69 mm); left without a size, the size it fits whose tap drill it is nearest. The groove is the nut's basic profile of ISO 68-1 (`IsoThread::internal_groove`): its root a flat `P/8` wide at the major diameter, its flanks `3P/4` apart at the minor diameter, carried `H/16` inside it to break through the wall; swept along a helix at the major radius, it runs out towards the axis (rise `−(5H/8 + H/8)`) and is subtracted from the body. `ThreadReport::internal`; `feature.thread` reports `internal` and `tapDrill`. Checked: the volume removed against the flux integral of the groove outside the hole (M10 in its tap drill, M6 in its minor diameter: to 2e-6 mm³ in 108 and 48), the root at the major diameter by a section, cosmetic and modeled through the commands with the deep check (`crates/kernel/tests/helix.rs`, `crates/engine/tests/thread.rs`).
- *Not built.* A hole feature (drill, thread, depth in one), other standards (UNC/UNF, pipe threads, trapezoidal), multi-start threads, a thread that ends against a shoulder without running out (the groove would need an end of its own), tolerance classes (6g: the basic profile is cut), the thread drawn as such in the viewport for a cosmetic thread.

**Extrude ends (2026-10-13, M8.5).** `doc::Extrude` has, besides `sketch`, `distance` and `symmetric`, an `end` (`ExtrudeEnd`: `Distance`, `ThroughAll`, `UpToFace { face: PersistentName }`) and `reversed` (against the sketch's normal; for a distance the same as negating it). `feature.extrude` takes one of `distance`, `throughAll` and `upTo`, with `symmetric` and `reversed`; `feature.edit` changes the end by giving the new one.

- *Symmetric:* `distance` is the whole length, half to each side of the sketch plane.
- *Through all* is decided in `regen` (`regen/src/extrude.rs`, `through_all`), from boxes, without a boolean. "All" is every body the features before this one have made that is still a body of the document at that point of the history (not taken by a boolean, a transform or a pattern before it) and that lies in the way: its box (`Body::bbox`, conservative), seen along the sketch's normal, overlaps the box of the profiles. The extrusion runs from the sketch plane to past the farthest such box by a margin (a hundredth of the way, at least a thousand tolerances), so that a cut never ends flush with the far side; `symmetric` runs from before the nearest box to past the farthest. With nothing in the way the feature fails and says so (`there is nothing to go through along the sketch's normal: none of the 2 bodies built before this feature lies in the way of the profile`). The length is not stored: it follows the bodies. The regeneration cache knows that such a feature depends on every feature before it (`FeatureKind::uses_all_earlier`).
- *Up to a face* is `kernel::extrude_to::extrude_up_to`. The profiles are extruded past the face's surface, a *tool* solid is built that has that surface as one of its faces (a block behind a plane; the solid cylinder, ball or ring of a cylinder, sphere or torus; for a polynomial Bézier or B-spline face the solid behind the whole patch, with a wall under each of its borders), and the kernel's boolean takes the tool away (or, from a sketch plane inside a ball or a cylinder, keeps what is inside it). What hangs on to the start caps is the result; what comes out on the other side of a cylinder is dropped. So the end lies on the face's *surface*: where the profile hangs over the face's edge it ends on the plane, cylinder, sphere or torus continued, and a freeform surface ends where its patch ends. The profile has to reach the face itself somewhere (looked at on nine points per edge of the end and of the face; the module documentation says what that cannot see). The reference is the face's persistent name, resolved against the body of the feature the name starts with, as it is at that point (a copy name such as `F3:copy(1)/F2:face(1)` against the boolean's body); pieces of one face count as the face, and a cap whose outline changed still names its surface, as for a sketch's plane. A name that points at nothing fails the feature with the name and the faces that are there (``upTo: `Part` no longer has a face F3:copy(1)/F2:face(1); its faces are: …``); it never lands on another face, and the features that do not depend on it are built. Pointing `upTo` at a face that is there repairs it.
- *Names.* The faces are named as a plain extrusion names them: `startCap` in the sketch plane, `side(e7)`, and `endCap` (with the outline of its profile) for the faces on the target surface, which need not be flat, and can be several faces with one name when the boolean left the end in pieces. Edges and vertices get their plain names too (`endEdge(e7)` where wall `e7` meets the surface) whenever each can be told; when one cannot, the body has no edge and vertex names, as after any boolean.
- *Refused, each with its reason:* a face parallel to the direction (or a freeform patch that turns away from it somewhere); a face behind the sketch plane (`reversed` turns the extrusion round; the direction is never guessed, so that an edit which moves the face to the other side fails instead of flipping the body); a surface that passes through the sketch plane inside the profile; a profile part of which passes beside the surface or beyond the patch; a profile that misses the face; `symmetric` with `upTo`. **Not built:** a cone face and a rational freeform face as the end (`Unsupported`); a second end on the other side (from a face up to a face); an offset from the face. Whatever the boolean refuses is the feature's failure, with the boolean's reason.

Measured (`crates/kernel/tests/extrude_to.rs`, `crates/engine/tests/extrude_ends.rs`, scales 1e-3, 1 and 1e6, world and tilted frames): a round profile up to an inclined plane has the volume of a cylinder of the mean height and an elliptic end of area πa²/cos θ; a rectangle up to a lying cylinder and a round profile up to a ball (from outside and from inside) have their closed forms to 1e-9; a rectangle up to a parabolic B-spline wall has the area under the parabola times its height, and the arc length in its area, to 1e-7.

**As built so far (M6, first slice).** An extrusion names what it makes by role and by the sketch entities involved, written `F2:side(e7)`: the caps are `startCap` and `endCap` with the entity ids of the profile's outer loop; a side face is `side(curve)`; the cap edges are `startEdge(curve)` and `endEdge(curve)`; the upright edge swept by a corner is `lateralEdge(incoming, outgoing)` in the direction the loop runs through that corner, and the corners are `startVertex` and `endVertex` with the same pair. The names do not depend on the order of the curves or on what else is in the sketch: drawing a hole afterwards adds names and changes none. Known limits: drawing a loop *around* an existing one turns it into a hole and reverses it, so its corner and upright-edge names get their pair the other way round (the old names go missing rather than land elsewhere), and the caps are renamed after the new outer loop. **Resolution (M6).** `doc::PersistentName` is the name as data (feature, role, sources; `F2:side(e7)` as text). `regen` resolves it against the features regenerated so far: the feature must exist, come earlier in the history, have succeeded, and have a face whose origin is exactly that name. Anything else fails the referring feature with a reason that says which of those it was and lists the names that do exist; a name never resolves to a different face. One looser match exists, for the *plane* of a sketch only: all caps of one role of one extrusion lie in one plane and share one frame, so a cap name whose outline is no longer there (`F2:endCap(e5,e6,e7,e8)` after the corners were rounded, or simply `F2:endCap()`) still gives the sketch its plane, that of any cap of that role of that feature. The stored name is not rewritten. A side face has no such fallback. The first consumer is a sketch on a face (`SketchPlane::Face`), and the suite in `crates/engine/tests/naming.rs` holds the scenarios.

**Bodies made from bodies (M6).** A transform, a mirror and a pattern make their body out of the body of an earlier feature. That feature's result stays in the regeneration (names are resolved against it) and is marked as taken by the later feature, so its body is no longer one of the document's bodies; `keep` leaves it its body. A body can be taken once. Every entity of the new body is named after the entity it is the image of, as a *copy*: `F5:copy(2)/F2:side(e7)` is the image, in instance 2 of F5, of the face F2 calls `side(e7)` (instance 0 for a transform or a mirror). The part after `/` is a name of its own, so copies of copies chain (`F9:copy(0)/F5:copy(2)/F2:side(e7)`), in the short form of *Copies of copies* below. A copy resolves by walking the chain down to the face as it was first made, with the rules above (the looser match for caps included), and carrying its frame back up through the motion of each copy; a name whose instance no longer exists, or whose inner name is not of the body the feature was made from, points at nothing. After a mirror the frame is still seen from outside and right-handed: x is the image of x, and y points the other way than the image of y. Instances of a pattern that touch or overlap (their boxes come within the tolerance of each other) are joined by the kernel's union; the faces keep their copy names (a face merged from the faces of several instances is named after the first and answers to the others as aliases, see *Merged faces carry aliases* below), and edges are then referred to by the faces they lie between, as after any boolean.

**Booleans (M7).** A boolean feature combines the bodies of two earlier features (target and tool) and takes both, unless the tool is kept. Every face of the result is a face of one operand, or a piece of one, and is named as a copy of it: instance 0 is the target, 1 the tool (`F7:copy(0)/F2:endCap(e5,e6,e7,e8)`, `F7:copy(1)/F3:face(0)`). A face cut into pieces gives several faces with one name. That is resolution outcome `Many`; for the plane of a sketch it is accepted, because the pieces lie on one surface. A face the boolean removed is not among the result's names, and a reference to it fails and lists the faces that are there. **Edges are referred to by the faces they lie between** (`regen::edges_between`, `document.edges`): an edge of a solid is where two faces meet, and faces have names through every feature kind. "The edges between `F4:copy(0)/F2:endCap(...)` and `F4:copy(1)/F3:face(0)`" is the rim of a drilled hole, whatever the body's edge numbering is, and it is a list: two faces can meet in several edges, and pieces of one face share a name. Since 2026-10-14 an edge of a boolean also has a name of its own, made of exactly that pair (`F7:between[a,b]`, below); sweeps and primitives name their edges and vertices by where they came from. **Merged faces carry aliases (2026-10-13).** Faces that a boolean split needlessly, or that continue across both operands on one surface (the tops of two flush blocks united), are merged again by the kernel, so a union of flush bodies has no seam. The kernel lists every contributing face of such a face (`BooleanResult::merged`, the target's faces first, in order). `regen` names the face after the first contributor (`F3:copy(0)/F1:face(1)`) and gives it the names of the others as *aliases* (`F3:copy(1)/F2:face(1)`; `Names::aliases`, listed by `document.inspect` as `faceAliases`, per face, only when a body has any). A reference resolves by any of a face's names: a sketch on the merged top by the tool's cap name lands on it, with the frame of the tool's cap (the one plane), and `document.edges` finds the rim by either name. The stored name is not rewritten, and nothing is stored in the file: aliases are derived at regeneration, so a document written before this change resolves as it did, and a name that resolved without merging (a face of the tool that kept its own name) still does. Edits upstream: while the faces stay flush the alias follows the merged face; when they are no longer flush the name means the tool's own face, which is there, and the sketch goes with it (not a retarget: the same origin); when the tool's face is gone (sunk into the target) the name is `Missing`, listed or not. The pattern feature's union of touching instances does the same (`F2:copy(0)/F1:face(1)` with aliases `F2:copy(1)/…` and `F2:copy(2)/…`, in instance order). Known limit: nothing consumes an edge reference yet (fillets are M9).

**Edges and vertices by their faces (2026-10-14).** Every edge and every vertex of every body has a name of its own: within one body no two edges share a name and no two vertices do (`regen::Names::edges`, `vertices`; pinned by `crates/engine/tests/naming_entities.rs`). Where the name comes from depends on what made the body, and there is one rule per kind, not two schemes side by side:

- *A primitive and a sweep* name an edge and a vertex by where it came from, as before: `F1:vertex(6)`, `F2:lateralEdge(e5,e6)`, `F2:endVertex(e5,e6)`. Those names are complete and unique on every primitive and on every sweep the suite builds (profiles with holes, arcs, two arcs or two splines that share both ends, revolutions whole and partial and on the axis, an extrusion up to a face).
- *A transform, a mirror and a pattern whose instances stay apart* name an entity as the copy of the one it is the image of (`F5:copy(2)/F2:endVertex(e5,e6)`), as they do faces. What the source's entities answered to besides their own names (aliases, also those of faces) the copies answer to as copies; before 2026-10-14 the aliases of a merged face were lost in a copy and in a second boolean, which was a hole.
- *A boolean and a pattern whose instances are joined* name an edge by the faces it lies between and a vertex by the faces that meet in it, the faces by their names in that body, in the order names are compared in (by feature, role, sources, then what a copy is a copy of; a name typed or stored with its faces in another order is read as the same name): `F7:between[F7:copy(0)/F1:face(1),F7:copy(1)/F2:face(0)]` is the rim where the hole (the tool's wall) leaves the top of the target, `F7:corner[F7:copy(0)/F1:face(1),F7:copy(0)/F1:face(2),F7:copy(1)/F2:face(4)]` the corner where a slot's wall meets the top and the front. A seam, and the cut between two pieces of one face, has its face twice (`F7:between[a,a]`), the same pair `document.edges` takes. This is the decision that an edge is referred to by the faces it lies between ([`decisions.md`](decisions.md)), made into a name: it needs nothing from the kernel but the body and the names of its faces, so it is the same for a boolean and for the row of unions that joins a pattern, it does not depend on the order in which the kernel emitted the entities (`regen/src/entity.rs`, tested by naming a body and the same body with every list in another order), and it survives every edit that leaves the faces round the entity what they were.

*When several entities lie on the same faces* (the two halves of an edge a slot cut through, the two points where an edge enters and leaves a round notch, the edges on the two pieces a face was cut into, which share the face's name) none of them gets the bare name. Each then also lists, after `|`, the other faces near it: the faces at the vertices of the edge, or at the vertices one edge away from the vertex, and when that does not tell all of them apart the faces up to four edges further away, all of the group equally far, at the nearest distance that tells them all apart (`F7:between[top,front|left,slotWall]`). Why faces near it and not the vertices at its ends or its place along a loop: a vertex is itself named by faces, so that is the same information, and the order along a loop changes with every edge an edit adds. Why not always: the bare name stays valid through edits elsewhere on the body, and a name with more faces in it is lost by more edits. *When no face tells them apart* (the two circles in which a torus half sunk into a plate cuts its top; the two straight edges of a cylinder cut in half lengthwise, and the corners at their ends) they get a number after the list, `F7:between[a,b|c](0)` and `(1)`, in the order of where they lie in space: by x, then y, then z, of the vertex or of the middle of the edge, coordinates within the document's tolerance of each other (or joined by a row of such steps) counting as one, so rounding in the last digits cannot change the order and the scale does not.

**Copies of copies (2026-10-14, format 15).** Until then every feature that made its body from another put a link in front of every name (`F11:copy(0)/F10:copy(0)/F9:copy(0)/…`), so names grew with the history and a row of more than 32 such features failed (`MAX_NAME_DEPTH`; a plate with 70 holes cut one after another hit it). Now a name has one link per *run* of steps along the way, not per feature (`PersistentName::copy`, `doc/src/name.rs`):
- *A run of instance 0* (the target of every cut, the body every blend or face operation took) is one link with the one number 0, named after the first feature of the run: `F2001:copy(0)/F1:face(1)` is the top of a plate a thousand cuts went through. Its meaning: instance 0 of F2001, of the body that was made from, and so on through instance 0 until the way reaches the feature of the link after it (here F1).
- *A run of other instances* (the tool of each union, an instance of a pattern of a pattern) is one link listing them, each step direct: `F9:copy(1,3)/F2:side(e7)` is in instance 1 of F9, in instance 3 of the body F9's tool was made from, and that body was made from F2's.
- So the wall of the first of a thousand holes is `F2001:copy(0)/F4:copy(1)/F3:side(e2)`, three links: the tool of the first cut, then the target ever after. A name grows only where the way changes between instance 0 and others, which a row of cuts, blends, booleans on the target, transforms or patterns never does; a union that takes the grown body as its tool every time lists one number per union (forty unions: `copy(1,1,…,1)`, still one link). What still has a cap is a way that switches back and forth between target and tool more than about 30 times (32 links, a guard for hostile input as well; every other cap is the sources' 4096 numbers).
- *The features in between* are the history's: each step goes to the feature the instance was made from (for a boolean instance 0 is the target, 1 the tool; for every other feature the one body it takes), as the feature was built (`follow::source_of`, `follow::steps`). They are the same every regeneration, so the name means one entity, and a feature inserted into a run of instance 0 is walked through like the others (as *References that follow a body* do on purpose).
- *Unique both ways.* In a short name two links in a row never go the same way (never 0 after 0, never two runs of other instances), and the numbers of a link are the one 0 or numbers none of which is 0 (`check` refuses `copy(1,0)`). The long form a file or a typed name may still have (one link and one number per feature) is read into the short form without the history (`PersistentName::canonical`: links that go the same way are merged), which is the name regeneration gives the same entity; a name in the short form reads as itself. Edges and vertices named by their faces list the faces in the short form, in the order names compare in, re-sorted after merging.
- *Where the way is walked.* A sketch on a face (`sketch::resolve_face`) expands the name into its steps and carries the frame up through each feature's map, moved faces and turned tools as before; following a short reference (`follow::face`) looks for `T:copy(k)/<name>` built by `PersistentName::copy`, and `short_face_name` tries the names the face had in each body on the way.
- *Files.* Format 15 writes the short form; `convert::name_to_doc` makes every name read short, so a file of any earlier version opens to the same bodies (the 24 showcase files and 25 working files: `craft3d-cli info` is byte for byte the output of before with every name rewritten to its short form by an independent script). The step from 14 to 15 changes nothing in the JSON; the version was raised so an older build, which takes a copy to have one number, says a newer 3DCraft is needed.
- *Measured* (release, one core, `crates/engine/tests/long_chains.rs`, ignored test): a plate with 70, 250, 500 and 1000 holes cut one after another regenerates in full in 0.11, 1.6, 4.6 and 18.4 s, the longest face name 39 to 42 characters at every size. A cut costs time in proportion to the faces of the plate it cuts (1.5 ms at 70 holes, 18 ms at 1000), so a full regeneration grows with the square of the row, as the bodies do; the names add nothing that grows. Adding the holes one command at a time took 593 s for 1000: each command regenerates through the cache, which compared, per feature, every feature it is made from (`regen::cache`, looked up once per regeneration instead of once per feature and input, which was the cube of the row: 268 ms per command at 400 holes before, 87 ms after), and each cut formatted its operands for the boolean's memo. Since 2026-10-15 (§5.5, *Long rows*) a feature whose inputs did not change is not compared input by input and the memo keys bodies by their bits: 1000 holes one command at a time take 29 s (`crates/engine/examples/regen_rows.rs`, release; 19 s of it the cuts themselves, a sketch or a circle 5 ms at 3000 features), 250 holes by `craft3d-cli run` 7.4 s before and 1.6 s after.

*An entity an operand already had* (the kernel's `EdgeSource::Kept` and `VertexSource::Kept`) also answers to the name the primitive or sweep that first made it gave it, as a copy and as an *alias*: `F7:copy(0)/F1:vertex(6)`, and through three booleans `F9:copy(0)/F1:vertex(6)` (`Names::edge_aliases`, `vertex_aliases`; `aliases` in `datum.references` and `document.edges`). It is given only when exactly one entity of the result came from there: the two pieces of an edge a slot cut through are neither of them "the" edge, and both lose the alias. The name an entity had by its faces in a body in between is not carried along (it says nothing the faces here do not say, and the lists would grow with every boolean). A reference resolves by the own name or by an alias (`Names::edge_named`, `vertex_named`; a datum point at a vertex of a boolean or of a joined pattern, which failed before, is the first consumer).

*What an edit does to such a name* (each case is a test in the suite):

- Sizes change and the topology does not (a variable, a radius, a tool moved so that it cuts the same faces; three booleans in a row, at scales 1e-3, 1 and 1e6): every name stays on the same entity, checked by the faces on its sides, the names of the vertices at its ends and the edges that end in it, and for ten corners by the closed form of where the corner must be.
- An entity is removed (the slot no longer reaches the front): exactly the references to it fail, with the name (``at: `Part` no longer has a vertex F7:corner[…]; its vertices are: …``), the others stay where they were, and putting the entity back repairs them.
- A second entity appears on the same faces (the slot now cuts the top front edge in two): the bare name and the alias are gone and two longer names are there. A reference to "the edge between the top and the front" fails; it does not land on one of the halves. The reverse edit brings the name back.
- **Where stability cannot be had.** (1) *A numbered name.* The number is a place in space, not an origin. While the tied entities are all there and keep their order it stays; when one of them goes the others lose the number (the reference fails, which is right); but an edit that keeps them all and changes their order in space moves the number to another entity without any failure. The minimal example is in the suite: a cylinder cut in half lengthwise, both operands turned half a turn about the axis by one variable; the solid is the same solid turned, every name is still there, and `…(0)` and `…(1)` are each on the image of the other. Use the alias instead when there is one (the two edges where the walls of a lens meet are numbered as edges of a boolean, and answer to `…/F2:lateralEdge(e6,e4)` and `(e4,e6)`, which do not move). A feature that takes such a name should say so to the user; none does yet. (2) *An alias follows the kernel's choice where entities of both operands coincide.* The kernel reports one source per edge and vertex (where a vertex of the target lies on one of the tool, the target's; for an edge merged from two collinear ones, the least), so the other operand's name for it is not an alias, unlike the faces, which carry every contributor. (3) *A name with faces after `|` is lost by an edit to those faces*, also when the entity itself did not change: louder than needed, never wrong. (4) *Pieces of one face share its name* (unchanged), so "the corner where the top, the front and the slot meet" is two corners when the slot is cut by one face (a cylinder): that is the tie the faces near it, or the number, break.

Not built: a consumer of an edge's own name (datums, chamfers and `document.edges` take the pair of faces, which gives the list of edges between them; a chamfer takes them all, see *Chamfers* below), the names in `document.inspect` (it has `faceNames`; edges and vertices are listed by `document.edges` and `datum.references`), and a way for a feature to ask for "every piece of what was this edge".

**Chamfers (2026-10-14, M9: the first blend, and the first feature that consumes edges).** `doc::Chamfer` has `edges` (a list of references), `size` (`ChamferSize`: `Equal { distance }`, `TwoDistances { distance, second }`, `DistanceAngle { distance, angle }`, all expressions) and `chain`. It makes its body from the body of an earlier feature and takes it, like a transform.

- *What a chamfer holds of an edge* is **the names of the two faces the edge lies between, in order**: `[F1:face(1), F1:face(2)]`. Why the pair and not the edge's own name: (1) the order of the pair is what says which face the first distance is measured along and which face the angle is taken from, and a name of an edge has no first face; (2) after a boolean the edge's own name *is* that pair (`F7:between[a,b]`), so nothing is lost there, and on a sweep or a primitive `document.edges` lists the pair beside the name; (3) the pair survives what the own name does not: when a later edit cuts the edge in two (a slot through it), the own name is gone and two longer names are there, while "the edges between the top and the front" are both pieces, and both stay chamfered, which is what the user meant; (4) no numbered name is ever stored, so limit (1) of *Where stability cannot be had* cannot retarget a chamfer. The price: two faces that meet in several edges are chamfered along all of them, and one of several cannot be picked (not built: a reference by the edge's own name, for that case). Which body is chamfered is not stored apart: it is the body of the feature the first face is of, and every other face has to be of that feature too, after the names are followed to the newest body of their chain (since 2026-10-14, *References that follow a body* below).
- *Resolution.* At every regeneration each pair is found again with `regen::edges_between`. A face that is gone fails the feature with the parameter, the feature and the name (``edges.0: `Notched` no longer has a face F3:copy(1)/F2:face(3); its faces are: …``); faces that no longer meet fail it with both names (``edges.1: the faces … and … of `Base` do not meet in an edge (any more)``); what is made from a failed chamfer fails naming it and carries `causedBy`, and the body the chamfer would have taken is its source's again, as for every feature (*A feature that fails*). Repair is `feature.edit` with `edges`, which gives the document built that way from the start. With `chain` each edge found stands for the edges that continue it smoothly as well (`kernel::chamfer::tangent_chain`: at each end the one other edge that leaves the vertex straight on, both ways, until none does or the chain closes); along the chain the "first" face is the one the edges share, or what continues the first face.
- *Which face gets which distance.* `distance` is measured along the first face of the pair, `second` along the second, each along the face and at right angles to the edge; `angle` is the angle between the first face and the chamfer face, inside the corner that is cut away or filled, so against a square corner the second face is cut `distance · tan(angle)` back and in general `distance · sin(angle) / sin(angle + θ)` with θ the angle between the two faces across the edge. Turning the pair round turns the distances round.
- *Names of what a chamfer makes.* A face of the body that is still there is a copy, instance 0, of the face it was (`F9:copy(0)/F2:side(e7)`), with the aliases it had, as in every feature that makes its body from another; a sketch on it lies where it lay. The face in the place of the edge of reference `k` is `F9:chamfer(k)` (role `chamfer`, the place of the reference in the chamfer's list). When that reference gave several edges (pieces between the same two faces, or a chain) their faces are `F9:chamfer(k,0)`, `F9:chamfer(k,1)`, … in the order of where the edges' middles lie in space (x, then y, then z, as numbered edge names are counted, with the same limit: an edit that changes that order moves the numbers). The flat face where three chamfers meet is `F9:chamferCorner(i,j,k)`, by the references of the three edges. Chamfer faces in one plane that became one face are named after the first and answer to the others as aliases. Why a number and not the two faces, as an edge of a boolean has them: the edges and vertices of the chamfered body are named by the faces they lie on, and a name in such a list lists no faces itself, so a face's name has to be flat. The number is the place in this feature's own list, which no upstream edit changes; taking an edge out of the list renumbers the ones after it. The edges and vertices of the result are named by their faces (`F9:between[F9:chamfer(0),F9:copy(0)/F1:face(1)]`), and one the body already had also answers to a copy of the name it had there, exactly as after a boolean (one implementation: `Names::name_entities`).
- *The frame of a flat chamfer face* (what a sketch on it gets): z out of the body; x along the edge, in the direction of the cross product of the directions into the first and the second face (each along its face, away from the edge); the origin where the cut line on the first face starts in that direction. It depends on the pair and the geometry, not on which way the kernel runs the edge.
- *Tests:* `crates/engine/tests/chamfer.rs` (closed forms at 1e-3, 1 and 1e6, `document.check` with `deep`, names, upstream edits that move and remove the edge, repair, a sketch on the chamfer face placed by its frame, a chamfer on an edge of a chamfer, save and reopen, everything refused), `crates/kernel/tests/chamfer.rs`, `crates/format/tests/format.rs`, and FreeCAD on the STEP export (`crates/engine/tests/step_freecad/chamfers.rs`).

**Fillets (2026-10-14, M9: the second blend).** `doc::Fillet` has `edges` (references, as a chamfer's), `radius` (an expression) and `chain`. Everything a chamfer does with its references a fillet does with the same code (`regen::blend`: finding the edges of a pair of faces again, the chain, the numbering, the names; `regen::chamfer` and `regen::fillet` are the two sizes and the two wordings of what the kernel refuses).

- *An edge* is the two faces it lies between; their order says nothing, since a fillet treats both faces alike, and turning a pair round gives the same body and the same names.
- *Names of what a fillet makes.* The face in the place of the edge of reference `k` is `F9:fillet(k)` (role `fillet`), `F9:fillet(k,0)`, `F9:fillet(k,1)`, … when the reference gave several edges or a chain, in the order of where the edges' middles lie in space; the piece of a ball where three fillets meet is `F9:filletCorner(i,j,k)` (role `filletCorner`), by the references of the three edges. Faces that are still the body's are copies, instance 0, with their aliases; edges and vertices are named by their faces, so a seam is `F9:between[F9:copy(0)/F1:face(1),F9:fillet(0)]`.
- *What can be put on a fillet face.* A datum axis (`datum.axis` with `face`: the cylinder's or the torus's own axis, which is how the tests read where the face lies) and the edges it has with its neighbours (a later chamfer or fillet takes them by the pair of faces). Not a sketch: the face is not flat, and `sketch.create` on it is refused as on any curved face. A sketch on a face next to a fillet lies where it lay: the face keeps its frame.
- *After a fillet, the neighbouring edge is part of a chain.* The edge between the top and the left of a block whose top front edge is rounded now runs on smoothly in the arc where the fillet meets the left, and that in the upright edge between the front and the left. A chamfer or a fillet of that edge alone would stop in the middle of the chain and is refused ("does not end cleanly … fillet the edges it continues in as well"); with `chain` it runs round the corner (two cylinders and a quarter of a torus, or two flat faces and a cone). Two fillets on edges that meet, in one feature, run out into each other instead.
- *Tests:* `crates/engine/tests/fillet.rs` (closed forms at 1e-3, 1 and 1e6, `document.check` with `deep`, the axis of the new face through `datum.axis`, names, upstream edits that move the edge and remove a face, repair, a boss on the face next to a fillet, a chamfer after a fillet and a fillet after a chamfer and after a fillet on neighbouring edges, save and reopen, undo and redo, everything refused), the row `fillet: edges` of the failed-features table.

**Primitives (M6).** A primitive is built in a fixed order, so the number of an entity says where it came from: its faces, edges and vertices are named `F3:face(1)`, `F3:edge(4)`, `F3:vertex(0)`. For a block the faces are 0 bottom, 1 top, 2 front (-y), 3 back (+y), 4 left (-x), 5 right (+x) in its own axes; for a cylinder 0 the wall, 1 the bottom, 2 the top; for a cone 0 the side, 1 the base. A sketch on such a face has the frame the kernel gave the face, seen from outside (on the top of a block: the block's own x and y at its height). The order is part of the kernel's contract and is pinned by `crates/engine/tests/naming.rs`.

**The frame of a sketch on a face** is the face's own, seen from outside the body (z is the outward normal, moved by the sketch's offset): on the end cap of an extrusion, the axes and origin of the extruded sketch, moved to the end; on the start cap the same origin and x axis with y reversed; on a side face made from a line, x runs along the line (in the direction the profile's loop runs) and y along the extruded sketch's normal, and the origin is the origin of the extruded sketch dropped straight onto the face. That point moves only when the face moves along its own normal: not with the line's end points (a corner radius), and not with the depth or direction of the extrusion. "Start" is the cap in the sketch plane and "end" the far one, whichever way the extrusion goes. Known limits: turning an extrusion round (a negative distance) puts its end cap on the other side facing the other way, so a sketch on it is mirrored in y, and a sketch on a side face stays on its side of the sketch plane rather than following the body; primitives do not name their faces yet; a curved face cannot carry a sketch.

---

**The face operations' faces** (2026-10-14): an offset face names every face, edge and vertex of its result as the copy of what it was, instance 0; a shell names its skin's faces so and the faces of the hollow as copies in instance 1 of the faces they lie behind (`F9:copy(1)/F1:face(0)` is the floor of the hollow of a block). A deletion names the faces that stay as copies in instance 0 and its edges and vertices by their faces, with the copies of their old names as aliases. A move face and a draft (2026-10-15) name everything as an offset does; a layer made by thicken as a shell names its faces: instance 0 for a face on or along a face of the source, instance 1 for one under a face it thickens. No name role was added (§5.3 › *Face operations as built*).

**References that follow a body (2026-10-14, M9).** A feature that takes faces or edges of a body and makes its body out of it (a chamfer, a fillet, an offset face, a delete face, a shell, a thread) may name them as an *earlier* body of the same chain had them: `["F1:face(0)", "F1:face(2)"]` for the top rim of a screw's head after the head went into a union, a cut and two blends, where until then only `F11:copy(0)/F10:copy(0)/F9:copy(0)/F6:copy(0)/F3:copy(0)/F1:face(0)` would do (since format 15 written `F11:copy(0)/F1:face(0)`, *Copies of copies*) and a name of the consumed body failed with "already went into". `regen/src/follow.rs`:
- *The rule.* At the feature's place in the history the body a name is of is followed through the features that took it (`FeatureResult::consumed_by`, which only an `Ok` feature sets: a failed, suppressed or rolled-back feature took no body and is not followed through, as everywhere else) up to the body that is its own still. Each step finds the copy the taker made of the face: the instance `k` for which a face of the taker answers to `T:copy(k)/<name>` by its name or an alias (a cap also when only its outline changed, as every cap reference). The feature is then built from the full names, exactly as if it had been given them: a short name is an abbreviation of one long name and means what that name means, no more. Each name is followed on its own, so a pair may mix a face of the head (`F1`) with one of the shank (`F2`) or of an earlier blend (`F10:fillet(0)`); whether they end up in one body is checked by the feature as before (the commands refuse at once names of bodies that never came together: `regen::newest_body`). A shell's `source` is followed the same way, since the shell takes that body. The source of the feature (the body it takes, the body it is made from for `sketch::resolve_face` walking copies, and what the cache counts it as made from) is the one the followed names are of.
- *Stored as given.* The document keeps the names the user gave; they are followed again at every regeneration, and the full names are derived (`FeatureResult::followed`, shown by `document.inspect` per feature as `followed`, the parameters as built). So a feature inserted between that takes the body is followed through (a chamfer inserted before a later fillet: the fillet rounds the chamfered body), one moved after or suppressed is no longer, and an upstream edit is followed like any other. Rule 4 forbids silently retargeting a fillet to *another* edge; following the same face through the copies of the features that took its body keeps the same entity, and where it cannot be kept the feature fails. A name given in full (as every document before this change has it) is followed only where it would have failed with "already went into" before: every body that regenerated before regenerates bit for bit the same (the showcase's `craft3d-cli info` is identical).
- *What cannot be followed fails the feature* with the parameter and the feature on the way, never resolving to something else: a face that taker has no copy of (a delete face took it away, a boolean cut it off): ``edges.0: the face F2:fillet(0) is gone: the body of `Round` went into `Sharp`, a deleteFace, which left no face of it; its faces are: …``; a face it has several copies of (the instances of a pattern, the skin and the hollow of a shell): ``edges.0: the face F1:face(1) is more than one face after `Row`, a pattern the body of `Base` went into: F2:copy(0)/F1:face(1), F2:copy(1)/F1:face(1); give one of these names``; a name the body never had: ``edges.0: `Base` has no face F1:face(9); its faces are: …``. Everything else is as for any failed feature (*A feature that fails*; `crates/engine/tests/failed_features.rs`).
- *A face a boolean cut in two* is one copy (`F3:copy(0)/F1:face(1)`) that names both pieces, so the short name means both pieces as the long name does: a fillet between "the" top and the front rounds the edges between each piece and the front (`F4:fillet(0,0)`, `F4:fillet(0,1)`), an offset or a deletion takes every piece. Refusing would make the short name mean less than the long one, and a top a slot crosses is still the top the user meant.
- *Not followed:* references that do not take the body, a sketch on a face, an extrusion up to a face, a datum on a face, an edge or a vertex. They keep meaning the face of the body they name, which they could always do (a consumed body keeps its result and names), so documents that use them keep their meaning; to sketch on the newest form of a face, give its full name (`faceNames`, `view.pick`, `document.edges`). A transform, a mirror, a pattern and a boolean take a feature's body by the feature's id, not by a face, and still fail with "already went into".
- *Short names.* `document.edges` lists, beside `faces` (the full names, unchanged), `short`: the same faces by the shortest name that is followed back to them (`regen::short_face_name`: the full name without as many leading copies as can be left out; the copies of a pattern or a shell stay, since without them the name means several faces). `view.pick` and `document.inspect` give the full names as before.
- *Tests:* `crates/engine/tests/follow_refs.rs` (the screw blended by the names its head and shank were made with is, body for body and name for name, the screw of full names; every kind against its full-name twin, before and after an upstream edit; a chamfer inserted at the rollback marker, then moved after the fillet, then suppressed; a face a slot cut in two; a face a delete face took; the copies of a pattern and a shell; save, reopen, undo and redo; the short names of `document.edges`, which build what the full ones build).

### 4.2 Appearance and material per body (2026-10-15)

A colour (`Appearance`: sRGB, and `metallic` and `roughness` from 0 to 1) and a material (`Material`: a name and a density in kg/m³) are given to the body of a feature (`body.appearance`, `body.material`) and kept in `Document::bodies`, a list by feature id beside the history (`crates/doc/src/appearance.rs`). They are not features: regeneration does not read them, an edit of a feature's parameters keeps them (the id stays), deleting the feature drops its entry, undo and redo treat them as any edit. Saved in `.3dcraft` from format 16 ([`file-formats.md`](file-formats.md)).

**How they follow a body** (`crates/engine/src/appearance.rs`), a rule chosen so that a cutaway stays readable:

- *A body* has the appearance and the material of the feature that makes it, when that feature has one; otherwise those of the body it was made from as instance 0 (a boolean's target, the body a fillet rounds, a shell hollows, a pattern repeats or a transform moves), and so on back. So a cut keeps the colour of the plate it cut, a fillet does not turn a red part grey, and a material given to the first block of a part is the material of the finished part.
- *A face* has the appearance of the nearest feature on its own way back that has one. The way is the one its persistent name records (§4.1): `F7:copy(1)/F3:side(e2)` came through instance 1 of F7 (the tool) from F3, so the wall a blue drill cut into a red plate stays blue; `F9:copy(0)/F2:face(1)` went through instance 0 of F9 and of every feature before it until F2. A face a feature made itself (a fillet's round, a chamfer's flat) has the body's colour at that feature. A face whose way meets no appearance has the body's; a body without one is drawn in the theme's colour.
- *Setting a colour on a later feature* colours its whole body, tool faces included: the nearest feature wins.
- The material is per body only (a mass needs one density): the instance-0 rule, so a union of a steel and a brass part weighs as steel. Per-face materials, and a mass per piece of a body made of several materials, are not modelled.

The colours reach every picture through `craft3d_render::FaceColors` (a colour per face id; §7.2): the scene's (`Scene::face_colors`, built with the meshes), `view.render`'s, `view.replay`'s per step; and the exports: 3MF base materials per object and per triangle, STEP styled items per solid and per face ([`file-formats.md`](file-formats.md)). `document.inspect` and `document.measure` report each body's appearance and material, and with a material its mass and its inertia tensor about the centroid (`craft3d_topo::inertia`: the second moments by the divergence theorem on the exact B-rep, summed about the middle of the body and moved to the centroid; checked against closed forms for a block, a cylinder, a sphere and a torus, tilted and off the origin, at 1e-3, 1 and 1e3 in `crates/topo/tests/inertia.rs`, and through the commands in `crates/engine/tests/appearance.rs`).

*Setting them costs nothing (2026-10-16).* `body.appearance` and `body.material` change `Document::bodies` and nothing regeneration reads, so the regeneration after them, and after undoing or redoing them, rebuilds no feature and checks none (`colours_and_materials_rebuild_nothing` counts it). `body.material` says which bodies have the material and from where; it measures nothing. It used to report each body's mass and inertia as well, and the exact measure of a body of spline faces takes seconds: on the impeller (99 faces, 62 of them freeform) 35 to 44 s of CPU (4 to 11 s on fourteen threads), for one command that only names a material. The mass and the inertia are measured when `document.inspect`, `document.measure` or `document.check` asks, once per body (`engine/src/mass.rs`), and both at once for a body with a material (`topo::mass_and_inertia`): the faces are integrated once with the second moments, and the area, volume and moments are taken at the doubling where they agree, settled with a copy of the work spent so far, which is what `mass_properties` alone does, bit for bit (`mass_and_inertia_at_once_are_each_bit_for_bit_what_they_are_alone`, on analytic and freeform reference solids at two scales). One measure of both costs about what the inertia alone did: on the impeller about 20 s of CPU (in `document.check`, the first to ask) where the mass and then the inertia took 35 to 44 s. `colours_and_materials_measure_nothing` counts the measures (`mass::fresh_measures`).

## 5. Kernel

### 5.1 Geometry (`geom`)

```rust
pub enum Curve3 { Line(..), Circle(..), Ellipse(..), Nurbs(..), Trimmed(..), Offset(..), SurfaceIso(..), Intersection(..) }
pub enum Curve2 { Line(..), Circle(..), Ellipse(..), Nurbs(..) }           // parameter-space curves on a surface
pub enum Surface { Plane(..), Cylinder(..), Cone(..), Sphere(..), Torus(..), Extrusion(..), Revolution(..), Nurbs(..), Offset(..) }
```

Every kind implements evaluation with derivatives, parameter range and periodicity, bounding box, closest-point projection and inversion, and reversal. Projection as built (`Surface::project`): closed form for the analytic kinds; for Bézier and NURBS surfaces a search from the samples of a 16 × 16 grid that are nearer than their neighbours (and from a four times closer look around the nearest), each followed downhill by a damped Gauss–Newton iteration that puts the curving of the surface in when its steps fall short (Newton). It walks across the seam of a surface that closes on itself, half-steps towards a border that is a single point (a pole, a collapsed patch edge) and slides along a border of an open patch. The answer is a local nearest point, and the nearest one for a point closer to the surface than the surface curves, on a surface whose features are larger than a sixteenth of its domain (`crates/geom/tests/project.rs`). Intersections (curve/curve, curve/surface, surface/surface) are dispatched on the pair of kinds: closed-form where a formula exists (plane/plane, plane/cylinder, …), numeric marching with a tolerance otherwise. A pair that is not implemented returns `Unsupported`, and the test suite lists which pairs are covered.

#### Intersections as built (2026-10-13)

`geom::intersect::meet_surfaces(a, b, within, tolerance)` returns `None`, `Coincident { same_normal }` or `Meets(Meeting { curves, points })` for any two surfaces. Each `MeetingCurve` has the curve, the `range` of it that is meant, and the flags `closed`, `tangent` (the surfaces touch along it without crossing) and `exact`; an approximate curve carries a `deviation` that bounds its distance to both surfaces and is at most the linear tolerance. `points` are isolated touches. Curves are clipped to the box `within`, which is what makes a hyperbola or the curve of two crossing cylinders finite. The two surfaces are put in a fixed order first, so `meet(a, b)` and `meet(b, a)` are the same computation and the same result, bit for bit. **Curves never cross each other except at their ends:** where two branches cross (the surfaces are tangent there: two equal cylinders at right angles, a bitangent plane on a torus, Viviani's curve) every arm is a curve of its own that ends on the crossing point. `intersect_surfaces` (planes and cylinders, no box) is unchanged and still what `kernel::boolean` calls.

| Pair | Result |
|---|---|
| plane / plane, plane / cylinder, cylinders with parallel axes | exact: line, circle, ellipse, lines (the earlier closed forms) |
| plane / sphere, sphere / sphere | exact: circle, or a touch point |
| plane / cone | exact: circle, ellipse, parabola and one branch of a hyperbola (the last two as rational quadratic NURBS, the parabola polynomial), the apex alone, one ruling (tangent) or two; only the cone's own nappe |
| cylinder, cone, sphere, torus about one axis | exact: circles where their profiles meet (tangent ones flagged), the apex or a pole as a point |
| plane / torus, plane across or through the axis | exact: one or two circles about the axis, or the two sections of the tube |
| cylinders of equal radius whose axes cross | exact: two ellipses, as four half-ellipses that end on the two points where the cylinders touch |
| cones of equal half-angle with parallel axes | exact: the conic in the plane they meet in (not when that plane passes through the apexes: numeric) |
| cones tangent along a ruling they share (the ruling passes through both apexes; since 2026-10-14) | exact: the stretch of the ruling on the own nappe of both, flagged tangent, and the conic in which they cross besides (with `ν` off the common tangent plane, `Q₁ − λ Q₂ = ν · Σ` for `λ = cos² α₁ / cos² α₂`: everything they share lies in the tangent plane or in the plane `Σ = 0`); ruling and conic are both cut where the conic crosses the ruling, which is where the cones change sides |
| cones with one apex (since 2026-10-14) | exact: the two rulings they share, one where they touch (from outside or from inside, flagged tangent), or the apex alone as a point |
| a cylinder that goes on in a torus: the tube's radius, its axis tangent to the tube's middle circle (the fillet of a straight edge and of the arc it continues in; since 2026-10-14) | the circle across the cylinder at the place of contact, exact and flagged tangent, as two halves; and the closed curve in which they cross, `x² = 4 R y`, `y² + z² = r²` in the frame at that place (`x = 2 √(R r) cos ψ`, `y = r cos² ψ`, `z = r sin ψ √(1 + cos² ψ)`), which has no exact NURBS: two C¹ cubic splines fitted to the formula within a quarter of the tolerance. All four pieces run between the top and the bottom of the circle, where the second curve crosses it at right angles |
| a torus that goes on in a torus: one tube radius, parallel axes, the middle circles in one plane and touching from outside (an S) or from inside, radii more than the tube's radius apart (the fillet along two arcs of a chain; since 2026-10-14) | as above: the circle across the tubes at the place of contact, exact and tangent, in two halves; the second curve lies over a conic with the centres as foci (a branch of a hyperbola from outside, an ellipse from inside): with `u = \|δ\|` the in-plane distance to either middle circle, `y = (R₂ − R₁) u / Δ`, `x² = (4 R₁ R₂ / Δ) u (1 + u / Δ)` from outside (`Δ = R₁ + R₂`) and `y = (R₁ + R₂) u / Δ`, `x² = (4 R₁ R₂ / Δ) u (1 − u / Δ)` from inside (`Δ = R₁ − R₂`), `z² = r² − u²`; with `u = r cos² ψ` it is the cylinder's curve with `x` times `√(1 ± r cos² ψ / Δ)`, regular where it meets the plane, and fitted the same way. The position must fit within half a tolerance (tube radii, the height of one centre over the other's plane, the distance of the centres against `R₁ ± R₂`) |
| equal spheres, cones, tori (cylinders, planes) | `Coincident` |
| every other pair, including anything with a Bézier or NURBS surface | numeric: C¹ cubic NURBS within the tolerance of both surfaces (a freeform surface that closes on itself is walked around as a periodic chart since 2026-10-14) |

**The numeric method** (`intersect/march.rs`; the module comment has the argument in full). One surface is walked in its parameters, the other is a signed distance (closed form for the analytic kinds, closest point for freeform surfaces), and the intersection is the zero set of that distance over the first surface's parameter rectangle. A quadtree discards a cell when the distance at its centre exceeds what the surface can cover within the cell (a distance is 1-Lipschitz), and stops dividing when a bound on the second derivatives shows the distance to be monotonic along u or along v over the whole cell: such a cell holds no tangent point, and a branch through it is detected by the signs at its corners. Cells that reach the size of the tolerance without either holding are searched by Newton iteration for the point where the surfaces are tangent: a minimum or maximum of the distance is a touch point, a saddle is a crossing of two branches, anything flatter is refused. The same search runs earlier, as soon as cells keep failing both tests (around a point of tangency the gradient is small over an area that grows with the looseness of the bound, so thousands of cells per level fail there): where it finds the point and the gradient follows the Hessian's second-order model within a quarter on two rings around it, the disc inside the rings is settled without cells: it holds the touch, the two branches through the crossing (seeded along the directions in which the Hessian vanishes), or, around a critical point off the other surface with the distance of the sign its Hessian keeps, nothing. A touch is only recorded where the surfaces are within the tolerance and within a floor cell's reach of each other, which is where the cells alone would have found it. So no loop larger than a floor cell (twice the linear tolerance across) is missed, provided the derivative bounds and the rings hold: the bounds are proofs for analytic surfaces, Bézier patches and NURBS surfaces, rational ones included (since 2026-10-13: `RationalBezierPatch::derivative_bounds`, over the pieces of each span quartered twice), and estimates from samples for the curvature of freeform surfaces used as the distance (taken from how fast the normal turns on a grid near the point, doubled twice as margin); the rings are samples. Branches are marched with the step limited by the turn of the tangent, end where they close, leave the rectangle, run off the edge of the other surface or reach a crossing point or apex (a crossing point is looked for two steps ahead and from the radius at which its arms are seeded, since 2026-10-14: where the surfaces are a tenth of a tolerance apart at it, `f = 0` turns away just before the point, and a step that landed there went past it and on along the next arm, which was then drawn twice), and are fitted by a cubic Hermite spline that is refined until it is within half the tolerance of both surfaces and of the intersection itself at three places per span.
