A contact where the Hessian of the distance is degenerate (the surfaces touch along a curve, or to more than second order) is a touch when the distance keeps one sign on three rings of samples around it: the surfaces do not cross there, so nothing is divided. Where such a contact has a closed form it never reaches the marcher: cones along a ruling, and a cylinder that goes on in a torus or a torus in a torus, where the distance does change sign along the circle of contact (at the two points where the second curve crosses it), are in the table above. **Refused, as errors:** such a contact where the sign changes on every ring (`Unsupported`); surfaces nearly tangent or coincident over an area (more than a million cells of the quadtree; 400,000 until 2026-10-14, when two pairs of a flat face on the tangent plane of a gently curved roof needed 401,264 and 440,000), a curve that needs more points or evaluations than the caps allow, a fit that does not reach the tolerance, a tolerance finer than rounding allows at the size of the box (`NoConvergence` or `InvalidParameter`). Nothing partial is returned.

`intersect_curve_surface(curve, surface, tolerance)` covers every curve kind against every surface kind. Closed form: a line with a plane, cylinder, sphere or cone; a circle or ellipse with a plane and with a cylinder it is a section of a parallel cylinder to; a circle with a sphere, and with a surface of revolution about its own axis. Everything else is root finding on the signed distance along the curve with the same two certificates in one dimension (`intersect/curves.rs`); a hit where the curve stays within the tolerance of the surface without crossing by more is `tangent`, and a curve within the tolerance everywhere is `OnSurface`.

### 5.2 Topology (`topo`)

```text
Body ── Shell ── Face ── Loop ── Coedge ── Edge ── Vertex
                  │                 │        │        │
               Surface           Curve2    Curve3   Point     (+ orientation flags, tolerances, persistent names)
```

- A **body** is a set of shells: a *solid* (closed, oriented shells), a *sheet* (open faces) or a *wire* (edges only).
- A **face** is a surface trimmed by loops; a **coedge** is the use of an edge by a face, with a direction and a parameter-space curve.
- Storage is arena-based with typed ids, and persistent: an operation builds a new body that shares unchanged parts with its input.
- **Genus** (`topo::genus`): from Euler–Poincaré, `V − E + F − R − 2H = 2 (S − G)`. `R` is, per face, its loops beyond the first (−1 for a face without loops), which counts every face as a disc with holes. A face on a torus that still has the torus's handle (the whole torus, or the torus less the discs inside its loops) is not one and takes 2 off (`H`). A torus face with a loop around the axis or around the tube is a band, and one inside its loops is a patch: neither counts. The hole a drill leaves in a torus and the plug it takes out have the same loops walked the other way, so which of the two a face is, is read off the area its loops enclose in parameter space. A spline surface that closes on itself is not known to.
- **Validity checker** (`topo::check`): Euler–Poincaré, every edge of a solid used by exactly two coedges with opposite directions, loops closed, coedge curves on their surface within tolerance, vertices on their edges, consistent orientation, no self-intersection (the expensive checks behind a level flag). Every kernel operation is tested to return bodies that pass it.
- **Self-intersection, as built (2026-10-13):** `topo::check` does not test it. The test is `tess::body_self_intersections(&Body, &SelfCheckParams) -> SelfCheck`, in `tess` because it works on the mesh and so stays independent of the kernel it checks: it tessellates the body (chordal tolerance 1/1000 of its diagonal), finds the triangles that meet without being neighbours (§6), and reports crossings and overlaps between faces, each with a depth and with the margin `deviation(f) + deviation(g) + 2 × tolerance` that the approximation alone could explain (`deviation` is the chordal tolerance, or 0 for a plane bounded by straight edges, whose mesh is the face). A finding deeper than its margin is *certain*; one within it is reported as such and proves nothing (tangent faces, a ball resting on a plane). Findings within the margin between curved faces are looked at again with a mesh four times finer, twice by default. Flat faces within twice the modelling tolerance of one plane that share area are an overlap. Shells that do not cross are checked for where they lie, by the winding number of the other shells at a point of each: an outward shell must be in no material, a cavity in material. It is a test oracle (`crates/testkit/tests/self_intersection.rs`, [`testing.md`](testing.md) oracle 9, which also lists what it cannot see) and, since 2026-10-15, a step of regenerating every operation that can make such a body, near what the operation changed (§5.5, *Never through itself*); it is not a level of `check`, and the kernel operations do not run it themselves (`kernel` and `tess` share a layer). It also reports where a shell touches itself, on the B-rep (§5.5). Run over the boolean results of the corpus and the random runs (2026-10-13) it found nothing in 39,834 of them.
- **Mass properties:** volume, surface area, centroid and inertia tensor, computed from the exact B-rep (surface integrals), independent of the tessellator. Volume and centroid are taken about the middle of the body, not the origin of the model (2026-10-13): a small body far from the origin is then measured as well as the same body at the origin. These are the backbone of the test oracles. As built (`topo::mass_properties`: area, volume, centroid): each face is a boundary integral in its surface's parameters, and the result does not depend on how the surface is parametrised. A sphere face is measured in a frame chosen for its boundary, not in the surface's own (one pole in the middle of the boundary, the other as far from it as it gets); on a cone the integral runs from the apex whenever the boundary comes near it. Every boundary integral is computed with half the panels its edges need, with all of them, and with twice as many until two sums in a row agree to 1e-12; a face for which they never do is an error (`Unsupported`), as is a face that lies outside its own loops (`UnboundedFace`). Only plane faces bounded by lines, circles and ellipses skip that check: their sums are exact. On a spline surface every panel, across the face and along its boundary, ends at the surface's knot lines (and an edge's panels at its own knots too), and the panels across the face are doubled along with the boundary's; a face that holds a pole of a spline surface of revolution is integrated from that pole. **A boundary through a pole of a freeform surface** (a border of a Bézier or NURBS patch that is one point: every control point of it the same, to 1e-9 of the net's size; `geom::pole`) is measured since 2026-10-14. Such a border is a whole side of the parameter range that no edge of the body stands for, as at a cone's apex: with the inner integral running from the pole the integral is zero all along it (the integrand is smooth up to the pole; the normal's length goes to zero there, which is what makes the area element integrable), and an edge that passes through the pole on its way is cut there. A boundary through the poles at *both* ends (the face a spline sweeps that starts and ends on the axis runs from pole to pole between two meridians and has no other edge) has no inner integral that vanishes at both: the face is divided along a line of constant parameter between the poles, each side is integrated from its own pole, and the two are joined by the integral over the rectangle between the places where the boundary crosses that line (found by bisection along the edges). Poles on borders of constant u exchange the roles of u and v. Held to closed forms (`crates/topo/tests/mass_poles.rs`: wedges, octants, caps and halves of a spline ball, cones with a ruled spline side, with the poles either way, to 2e-15 at 1e-3, 1 and 1e6 in two frames), to Pappus for the dome and the drop (`crates/kernel/tests/spline_sweeps.rs`, 1e-9), and all 32 patches of the teapot to their double integral (24 before). A face that passes through no pole is measured exactly as before (the printed worst errors of every suite of `topo` are the same to the last digit). Declined by name: a boundary through two poles that also crosses the seam of a surface that closes on itself. **A spline surface that closes on itself** (its control net ends on the row it starts with, on clamped knots; `geom` gives it no period, so the measure finds it from the net) is followed since 2026-10-14, closed in u, in v, both ways or to a point, with or without seam edges. A seam line of constant u adds nothing to `−∮ G du`; one of constant v does, so where the boundary jumps over the seam in v the inner integral runs along u instead (`∮ H dv`), and where it jumps both ways the loops are unrolled as on a torus and the area settles the whole number of surfaces. Loops that only walk seam edges there and back make the face its whole surface (also on a sphere and a torus, which were refused), and loops that run the wrong way round are the rest of a surface that closes all around. Until then a face that closes in v was measured wrong without an error. Faces that do not go over a seam are measured to the same bits as before. Held to the closed forms of a NURBS cylinder, a vase (Pappus), an egg and a NURBS torus with windows across its seams, to 1e-12 at 1e-3, 1 and 1e6, and to the mesh (`crates/tess/tests/closed_freeform.rs`; `crates/topo/tests/mass_seams.rs`). On a freeform surface the nodes along one piece of an edge are projected each from where the last one landed (`Surface::project_from`, a few evaluations instead of a search over the whole surface), and the search is run only where the short way does not land on a foot of the node: within 1e-5 of the body's size and square to the surface, which is the same point the search finds. A spline edge of eight knot spans or more (the curve in which two surfaces meet, as the kernel fits it) is summed first with the eight-node rule on one panel per span and then with the sixteen-node one, before its panels double.
- **Moving, mirroring, merging** (`topo::transform`): `Body::transformed` maps a body by an isometry exactly (no curve or surface changes kind; scaling and shear are errors) and keeps every entity id. Curves keep their parametrisation, so edges are untouched. A mirror turns right-handed things left-handed: frames become `(Tx, Ty, −Tz)`, every loop is walked the other way round, and faces on planes and freeform surfaces toggle `reversed`, while cylinders, cones (half-angle negated), spheres and tori keep their outward normal and get `v` negated. `Body::bbox` is a conservative box from the exact geometry (tight for planes, cylinders and cones with analytic edges; the whole surface for spheres and tori, the control net for freeform). `merge` puts the shells of several bodies into one and reports the id offsets; it does not check that they are disjoint. These are what the transform, mirror and pattern features build on.

### 5.3 Operations (`kernel`)

Each operation is a pure function: bodies and parameters in, `Result<OpResult, KernelError>` out, where `OpResult` holds the new body and the **origin map** (for every result entity: which input entities and which role produced it), which feeds persistent naming.

| Group | Operations | Milestone |
|---|---|---|
| Primitives | box, cylinder, cone, sphere, torus | M3 |
| From sketches | profile detection (closed regions from sketch entities), extrude, revolve | M6 |
| Loft | a solid through a row of closed profiles: ruled, smooth, closed into a ring (`kernel::loft`, ✅ 2026-10-14, see *Loft*) | M9 |
| Booleans | union, subtract, intersect on solids; imprint, split | M7 |
| Local | chamfer (✅ 2026-10-14, see *Chamfers as built*), fillet with a constant radius (✅ 2026-10-14, see *Fillets as built*), offset face, shell and delete face (✅ 2026-10-14, see *Face operations as built*), move face, thicken and draft (✅ 2026-10-15, the same), replace face | M9 |
| Sweeps | a closed profile carried along a path in a plane: lines, arcs, mitred corners, splines; open or closed (`kernel::sweep`, ✅ 2026-10-14, see *Sweep along a path*); along a helix by its screw motion, and the ISO metric thread cut by it (`kernel::helix`, `kernel::thread`, ✅ 2026-10-14, see *Helix, and the sweep along it* and *Thread*) | M9 |
| Arrangement | transform, mirror, linear and circular pattern | M6–M9 |

**Rules for every operation:**
- **Signature:** `fn(inputs, params: &P, tol: &Tolerance, cancel: &CancelToken) -> Result<OpResult, KernelError>`; `P: Serialize + Deserialize`.
- **Never an invalid body.** The result passes `topo::check` or the operation returns `Err`. Errors name the entities and the reason ("faces F3/cap and F5/side are tangent along a curve; tangent booleans are not supported yet").
- **Deterministic:** no dependence on thread count or hash order.
- **Not registered by itself:** `engine` registers commands; `kernel` stays engine-agnostic.

#### Chamfers as built (M9, 2026-10-14)

`kernel::chamfer::chamfer(body, edges, tolerance)` takes, per edge, the edge, which of its two faces is the first, and a size (two distances, or a distance and an angle), and returns the body with a source for every face (`Kept` faces of the body, `Chamfer` of which requested edges, `Corner` at which vertex), edge and vertex.

**Route: a tool solid per edge and the boolean, not a local operation.** Two routes were open. (a) A local operation: cut the two neighbours at the offset curves, put in the new face, sew. It is what fillets will need in the end, and it keeps every untouched entity as it is; but it needs its own handling of every way an edge can end (the neighbours' other edges, a vertex of any valence, a mix of convex and concave edges, seams, a chamfer that eats a whole neighbour), and each of those is a place to produce a body that is valid in its counts and wrong in space. (b) A tool whose cross-section is the triangle between the edge and the two cut lines, subtracted from the body at a convex edge and added at a concave one. It leaves all of that to a kernel that is measured against a reference kernel (11549 of 11988 corpus outcomes agree, none wrong), whose result is checked or refused, and that names every entity of its result. Its price is the contact it produces by design: two faces of every tool lie in the two neighbours (coincident faces, same normals in a subtraction and opposite in a union), tools of edges that meet overlap each other, a chain's tools end against each other, and a corner plane goes through three existing vertices. All of that is what the boolean kernel handles today (`Contact without overlap`, the coincident rows of its table, merging): of the cases built for this feature it refused none. (b) was taken for the first version, with what it cannot do stated below; (a) remains the route for chamfers on edges the boolean route cannot make a tool for. (Fillets, built the same day, took (b) as well: the boolean built their tangent tools; see *Fillets as built*, also for the one small local step they added.)

**The tool.** *A straight edge between two planes:* a prism, `kernel::extrude` of the triangle (edge, edge + d₁ along the first face, edge + d₂ along the second) along the edge. *A circular edge between a plane, a cylinder or a cone about the circle's axis* (the end of a rod, the mouth of a hole, the foot of a boss, the base of a cone): the same triangle in the half-plane through the axis, turned by `kernel::revolve` through the whole circle or through the arc; the chamfer face is a cone. Convex or concave is read from the solid, never from the direction of the edge: with the faces to the left of their loops seen from outside, an edge is convex when the direction into one face points behind the other. Every tool is made from the body as it was given, so the order of the edges does not change the solid.

**Where an edge ends** (the part a local operation would have to do topologically). When every other edge at the end vertex is convex like the chamfered one (or every one concave), the body near the vertex is what its faces leave of space and the chamfer is one more such face: the tool is made longer than the edge (past where the chamfer plane cuts the other edges there, with a margin; by an angle for an arc), and what sticks out meets nothing, or only material when it is added. Otherwise the tool ends at the vertex, across the edge. That is right when the face the chamfer runs into lies in that plane (the top edge of a step that ends against a wall: the wall grows by the triangle and the boolean merges it into one face), and when the edge continues smoothly in another chamfered edge (a chain: the two tools end against each other and the cut between them leaves no trace). In every other case the end of the tool would stay in the result as a face nobody asked for, which the final check finds.

**Corners.** Two chamfered edges that meet: each chamfer is the whole half-space of its face near the vertex, so their faces meet in the line the two planes share (a mitre), for equal and unequal distances alike. Three chamfered edges of one kind (all convex or all concave) that meet in a vertex with nothing else: the corner is **the flat face through the three points in which the cut lines cross on the three faces**, a triangle; three points are always in a plane, so the rule is the same for unequal distances, as long as the three points are points of the chamfered body: when one chamfer is larger than the other two reach together (on a square corner: d₁ ≥ d₂ + d₃) it takes the corner whole, the other two never meet, and there is no corner face. It is what OpenCascade gives (measured through FreeCAD: all twelve edges of a 4 × 3 × 2 block by 0.25 have its volume, area and 26/48/24 counts to the last digits; three edges, and two, likewise), and what Fusion calls the corner type "chamfer"; the pointed corner where the three planes meet (a "mitre" corner) is not offered. It is made by one more boolean, with a block lying against that plane on the vertex's side. Where more than three edges meet, or convex and concave chamfers meet, there is no corner face: the chamfers are their half-spaces.

**Never a wrong solid.** (1) Before anything is built, a distance for which a cut line would leave its face is refused with the edge, the face and the largest distance that fits (`KernelError::ChamferTooLarge`): the measure is how far the rest of the face's boundary is from the edge along the face (for a straight edge the part of the boundary beside it; for a circle the part in its sector, and the axis), less the distance of another chamfer that cuts the face from the other side. (2) After the last boolean the result is held against what a chamfer is: every chamfer face is there, in one piece, borders both neighbours of its edge, and borders nothing but those, the faces at the ends of its edge, and the chamfers and corners of edges that end where it does; no other face of a tool is left; no chamfer face has become part of a face of the body. Anything else is `KernelError::Chamfer` with the edge (and the face), so a case the estimate in (1) lets through is still refused, with a less exact message. The boolean's own refusals come back as `KernelError::ChamferBoolean` with the edge.

**Supported and not** (tests: `crates/kernel/tests/chamfer.rs`, closed forms at three scales and in tilted frames):

| Configuration | Result |
|---|---|
| a straight edge between two planes at any angle, convex or concave; ending in corners of the part, against a wall across it, or in the next edge of a chain | built |
| a circle or an arc between a plane, a cylinder and a cone about its axis, convex or concave (rod, hole, blind hole, boss, cone, half a rod) | built: the face is a cone |
| one distance, two distances, a distance and an angle | built |
| several edges; two that meet (mitre); three that meet (flat corner); all twelve of a block | built |
| a chain of tangent lines and arcs (the rim of a slot) | built, edge by edge (with one distance: one joined tool, as a fillet's chain; 2026-10-15); one edge of it alone is refused ("does not end cleanly") |
| a straight edge between a plane and a cylinder along it, or two such cylinders (the cut edges of a halved rod or tube, the tip of a lens of two arcs; 2026-10-14) | built: the face is a plane (*Blends next to a cylinder* below) |
| any other edge: a straight edge next to a cone or a spline wall, an elliptic or freeform edge, a circle between other faces (2026-10-14) | built as a ruled spline surface when each end is a corner of three faces (or the edge is closed) and no other blended edge ends there (*Blends along freeform edges* below); otherwise `Chamfer`, with the reason |
| an edge that ends against a plane that is neither square to it nor convex like it (a ledge that runs into a slanted wall, a root that ends on a chamfer; 2026-10-15) | built: the chamfer ends in the plane of that face (*Blend findings of the engine block* below); against a face that is not a plane `Chamfer`: "does not end cleanly" |
| an outside and an inside chamfer that meet in a vertex (the inside corner of an L together with the ledge that ends in it) | `Chamfer`: "does not end cleanly" (the web that is added would have to be cut by the other chamfer; each of the two alone is built) |
| two faces tangent along the edge; a seam; a sheet | `Chamfer`, with the reason |
| a distance too large for a neighbour; chamfers that meet in the middle of a face | `ChamferTooLarge`, or `Chamfer` from the final check |

**Cost.** One boolean of the body with a five-face tool per edge, and one per corner: the twelve edges and eight corners of a block take 20 booleans, a few milliseconds together in a debug build. On a body with thousands of faces each of those booleans looks at the whole body; a local operation would not. Not measured on a large body yet.

#### Fillets as built (M9, 2026-10-14)

`kernel::fillet::fillet(body, edges, tolerance)` takes, per edge, the edge and a radius, and returns the body with a source for every face (`Kept` faces of the body, `Fillet` of which requested edges, `Corner` at which vertex), edge and vertex. The face in the place of an edge is what a ball of the radius touches as it rolls along the edge against both faces: tangent to both.

**Route: the chamfer's, a tool solid and the boolean, with one addition.** The plain case was tried first: a block less the prism whose section is the corner between the two tangent lines and the arc. The boolean builds it (the tool's curved face is tangent to both neighbours along lines that are edges of the tool, and two of its faces lie in the neighbours), and it built every single-edge and corner case asked of it. So fillets are made by the code chamfers are made by (`kernel::blend`, of which `kernel::chamfer` and `kernel::fillet` are the two front ends): how an edge is read from the solid, convex or concave, the path (a line, a circle, an arc), where the tool ends, the estimate of what fits, the booleans, the bookkeeping of where every face came from, and the check at the end. What differs is the section's third side and the corner. **The one contact the boolean refused** (it takes it since 2026-10-14, see below) was a cylinder that goes on in a torus across the border they share, as the fillets of a line and of the arc it continues in do along the rim of a slot: the two surfaces are tangent along that circle and cross in a second curve that leaves it, a pair `meet_surfaces` had no closed form for ("surfaces that touch along a curve … without a closed form"). A fillet does not ask it: the tools of edges that continue each other are **joined into one tool** first (`blend::join`: the two ends that lie against each other are taken out and their three edges and three vertices made one, a change of topology only; the joined tool is checked), so the cylinder and the torus are neighbours in one operand and the boolean never intersects them. That is the first local operation in the kernel, and the only one. **Since 2026-10-14 the boolean takes the contact** (§5.1: the circle of contact and the second curve in closed form; `a_cylinder_that_goes_on_in_a_torus_touches_it_along_their_border` in `crates/kernel/tests/fillet.rs`, which was the ignored reproducer, and `a_rod_that_goes_on_in_a_ring` in `boolean_surfaces.rs`), so the join can go; it has not been taken out yet. Measured once with the join switched off (an experiment, not in the tree): the twenty tests of `crates/kernel/tests/fillet.rs` pass with a separate tool per edge, the rim of the slot included; the engine's fillet tests were not run that way. A torus that goes on in another torus (two arcs of a rim that continue each other) is the same contact and has no closed form yet: the join still covers it.

**The section and the tool.** Across the edge the two faces leave it in two directions with the angle θ between them. The ball touches each face `r / tan(θ/2)` from the edge, and its centre lies on the bisector, `r / sin(θ/2)` from the edge. The tool's section is the kite between the edge and the two tangent points, less the sector of the ball: two lines and an arc, which `kernel::extrude` takes along a straight edge (the face is a **cylinder** of radius r, its axis parallel to the edge) and `kernel::revolve` about the axis of a circular edge (a **torus** whose tube has radius r), for an edge between a plane, a cylinder or a cone about that axis. The tool is taken out at a convex edge and added at a concave one.

**Where an edge ends** is the chamfer's rule, unchanged: longer than the edge where every other edge at the vertex is convex like it (or concave like it), ending at the vertex otherwise, which is right against a wall across the edge and where the next tool of a chain begins.

**Corners.** *Two fillets that meet, the other edges at the vertex sharp:* each tool is its whole section near the vertex, so the two faces run out into each other in the curve their surfaces share (two cylinders of one radius at a square corner: an arc of an ellipse), for equal and unequal radii. It is what OpenCascade gives (same volume to 1e-11, same counts). *Three straight filleted edges of one kind and one radius that meet in a vertex with nothing else:* the ball comes to rest in the corner touching all three faces, each of the three cylinders has its axis through the ball's centre, and the corner is **the piece of that ball between the three planes through its centre across the three edges**: an eighth of a sphere on a block, a spherical triangle on any other corner (the top corners of a hexagonal prism). It is made by one more boolean with the tool "the cell those three planes cut out on the vertex's side, less the ball"; the ball is tangent to each cylinder along the great circle in which the cylinder ends. That this is the surface a rolling ball leaves (the union of all balls of the radius that fit behind the three faces) is argued in `kernel::blend` and measured: see the tests. *Refused, with the reason:* three or more filleted edges at a vertex in any other arrangement (different radii, which a kernel call can ask for and a feature with its one radius cannot; a circular edge among them; a fourth edge) need a vertex blend that is not built; an outside and an inside fillet that meet end against each other and are refused by the rule for ends.

**Never a wrong solid.** The chamfer's two checks, and a third. (1) A radius for which a tangent line would leave its face is refused before anything is built, with the edge, the face and the largest radius that fits (`KernelError::FilletTooLarge`): the same measure as for a chamfer's distance, turned into a radius by `tan(θ/2)`; the ball that does not fit in a hole is the tangent circle that runs into the axis. (2) The result is held against what a blend is, as a chamfer's. (3) **Tangency:** at five points along every edge a fillet face shares with a neighbour of its edge, and with the ball of a corner, the two outward normals have to agree within the angular tolerance (and never closer than 1e-9, which is what rounding leaves); anything else is `KernelError::Fillet` and no body.

**Supported and not** (tests: `crates/kernel/tests/fillet.rs`, closed forms at three scales and in tilted frames):

| Configuration | Result |
|---|---|
| a straight edge between two planes at any angle (60°, 90°, 120°), convex or concave; ending in corners of the part, against a wall across it, or in the next edge of a chain | built: a cylinder |
| a circle or an arc between a plane, a cylinder and a cone about its axis, convex or concave (rod, hole, blind hole, boss, cone, half a rod) | built: a torus |
| several edges; two that meet (they run out into each other); three straight ones of one radius that meet (a piece of a ball), also at corners that are not square, convex or concave; all edges of a block or a hexagonal prism | built |
| a chain of tangent lines and arcs (the rim of a slot; round a corner an earlier fillet rounded) | built, with one joined tool |
| the rim of a rod with a radius of half the rod's or more | `Fillet`: the torus would reach its own axis (`geom::Torus` has no spindle form); a radius that reaches the axis is `FilletTooLarge` |
| three or more fillets at a vertex with different radii, a circular edge or a fourth edge | `Fillet`: "the vertex blend … is not built yet" |
| an edge that ends against a plane at a slant (a ledge into a slanted wall, a flange root that ends on a chamfer or at a corner of the outline; 2026-10-15) | built: the fillet ends in the plane of that face (*Blend findings of the engine block* below) |
| an outside and an inside fillet that meet; an edge that ends against a face at a slant that is not a plane; one edge out of the middle of a chain | `Fillet`: "does not end cleanly" |
| a straight edge between a plane and a cylinder along it, or two such cylinders (the cut edges of a halved rod or tube, the leading edge of a lens of two arcs; 2026-10-14) | built: a cylinder (*Blends next to a cylinder* below) |
| both rims of the end of a lens of two arcs (two tori that run out into each other at its sharp tips; 2026-10-14) | built since two surgical changes to the boolean (below) |
| any other edge: a straight edge next to a cone or a spline wall, the edge where two lofted faces meet, an elliptic or freeform edge, a circle between other faces (2026-10-14) | built as a spline surface within the tolerance when each end is a corner of three faces (or the edge is closed) (*Blends along freeform edges* below), goes on smoothly into another blended edge (a chain), or meets another such fillet at a vertex whose third edge stays sharp (2026-10-15, *Chains and meets of freeform blends*); otherwise `Fillet`, with the reason |
| two faces tangent along the edge; a seam; a sheet | `Fillet`, with the reason |
| a radius too large for a neighbour; fillets that meet in the middle of a face | `FilletTooLarge`, or `Fillet` from the final check |

**Limits that are the shared code's.** The estimate of what fits takes an edge that leaves from the end of the blended edge as the end of the face there (followed on round tangent arcs since 2026-10-15). That is right for the faces of a block and too careful where that edge is short and the face goes on behind it (the top front edge of a block whose top left edge is chamfered by less than the radius: refused as too large, though it would fit). Both blends have it; it refuses, it never builds wrong.

**Cost.** One boolean of the body per edge or per chain, and four per corner (two to cut the cell out of three blocks, one to take the ball out of it, one with the body): the twelve edges and eight corners of a block take 44 booleans, measured at about 0.16 s in the test profile (the chamfers of the same block: a few milliseconds; where the time goes was not profiled). As for chamfers, each boolean looks at the whole body.

#### Blends next to a cylinder (M9, 2026-10-14)

A straight edge on a cylinder is one of its rulings, parallel to its axis, and a plane through that line holds the axis' direction: the two faces look the same in every plane across the edge, and so does the blend (`kernel::blend::section`). In that plane each face is a line or a circle. A fillet is the circle of the radius tangent to both: its centre is where the two curves moved by the radius towards the ball cross (line and line, line and circle, circle and circle, in closed form; of two crossings the one nearest where the ball would sit between the tangent planes). A chamfer is the chord between the points the distances away along each curve (along the arc on a cylinder); with a distance and an angle the chord leaves the first point at the angle to the first curve. The tool is then the extrusion of the section (two sides along the faces, a line or an arc each, and the fillet's arc or the chord), exactly as between two planes, and goes through the boolean as every other tool: the fillet face is a cylinder, the chamfer face a plane. How far a face reaches across the edge, which the estimate of what fits measures, is measured along its circle. Corners of three such blends are not made (a fillet refuses them, a chamfer leaves the chamfers to meet). Tests: `crates/kernel/tests/blend_cylinders.rs` (a halved rod with one and both cut edges rounded, also from a boolean as in the turbofan's finding F5, a lens's tip, the foot of a half-round boss, a halved rod chamfered along the diameter and the arc; closed forms by Green's theorem over the section, at three scales and in a tilted frame).

**Two tip fillets of a lens (finding F1).** Rounding both rims of the end of an extruded lens of two arcs makes two tori that run out into each other at the sharp tips. The tools are right; the boolean refused at the edge of tolerance twice, and was changed in two places (`kernel/src/boolean/section.rs`): (1) a stretch of an intersection curve that is out of either face at its middle is dropped before it is held against the other face's boundary (a curve that leaves a face's edge tangentially, where a fillet's face meets the other side of the sharp edge, runs within the tolerance of that edge for a while, beyond the other face too, and was refused as "partly on and partly off a face boundary"); (2) an approximated (spline) edge that leaves a surface tangentially at one of its ends, staying within the tolerance of it up to a point where it crosses it again, meets the surface at its end and not there (it split the edge where the re-marched curve of the same two surfaces was not split: "an imprinted curve that does not fit the edges it should coincide with"). An exact edge that lies on the surface up to a point (a ruling of a cone up to its apex) is not touched by (2). The boolean corpus and every other boolean test give the same outcomes. Test: `both_tip_edges_of_a_cambered_blade` (radii 0.05 to 0.3 on the compressor blade's section, both orders, three scales, tilted).

#### Blends along freeform edges as built (M9, 2026-10-14)

`kernel::blend::rolling`: every edge the closed forms above do not cover (a straight edge next to a cone or a spline wall, the edge where two lofted faces meet, an elliptic or a spline edge, a circle between other faces). **Route: a local operation, not a tool and the boolean**: the fillet face is tangent to both neighbours along curves that have no closed form, and a boolean between a spline face and a face it is tangent to along such a curve is what the boolean refuses ("surfaces that touch along a curve … without a closed form"). Built directly, the borders of the blend face are edges of both faces from the start and nothing has to be found where two surfaces touch.

- **The face.** A fillet is the surface a ball of the radius sweeps rolling along the edge against both faces. Each ball is found in a plane across the edge (through a point of it, square to it; past the ends of the edge on the edge's curve continued, a spline's end spans as the polynomials they are): its centre moves to where the two tangent planes at the nearest points of the surfaces, moved by the radius, cross the plane, until it stops moving (in a few steps); then it has to be the radius from both surfaces, along their normals, on the right side. The face meets the ball in the short arc between the two points where it touches the faces, a rational quadratic, exact. Which planes pick the balls does not change the surface: the balls of the radius that touch both faces are one family. A chamfer is, in each plane, the line between the points of the faces the distances away from the edge's point (straight distances in the plane; given by an angle it is refused).
- **The approximation.** The arcs (or lines) of a row of planes, evenly spaced along the edge and past its ends, are joined into one B-spline surface by interpolating their homogeneous control points with a cubic, so the surface passes through every sample exactly. Between two samples it is held against the exact section there, point for point in the same parameter (which bounds its distance from the true surface from above), to a quarter of the linear tolerance; and a fillet's normal along its two borders against the faces' normals, to the angular tolerance or the angle the linear tolerance makes over the radius, whichever is more. Samples are added until both hold (at most 256). The borders are the surface's own iso-curves, so the blend face and the faces beside it share their edges exactly.
- **Where it ends.** Each end of the edge has to be a vertex of exactly three faces: the blend is continued past it until a whole section lies outside the third face, and cut where it crosses that face's surface, along a cubic fitted to points found exactly (each by bisection along the surface), held to a quarter of the tolerance between them. The two other edges at the vertex are shortened to where the borders reach them, the third face gets the cut in place of its corner, and the edge becomes the blend face; every other entity keeps its number. That is the cut edge of a conical casing ending at its end faces, the leading or trailing edge of a lofted blade ending at the root and the tip. **A closed edge** (one vertex that no other edge passes through: the rim of a slanted cut of a rod, the mouth of a slanted hole, a closed spline root) has no ends: the sections go evenly round it, joined by the closed cubic with continuous curvature through them (`refine::periodic_tangents`, one Bézier piece per pair), refined the same way; the edge becomes the first border, the second border is new, and the blend face is the ring between the two loops.
- **Never a wrong solid.** A ball that does not settle or does not touch both faces, an arc of half a turn, a border that leaves the edges at an end, a cut that is not found, or an approximation that does not converge is refused with the edge and the reason; the body passes `topo::check`, and the fillet's tangency is measured again along its borders (to ten times the bound above). What the kernel cannot see, a large blend running into a face across the body, regeneration looks for on a mesh (`tess::body_self_intersections`, as for a loft) before the body is handed on.
- **Refused, with the reason** (work owed): an edge that ends at a vertex of four or more faces; a closed edge whose vertex other edges pass through; a chamfer by distance and angle; a ball that does not fit (a face curves more tightly than the radius under it, as the fan blade's suction side does at its leading edge, radius 0.049 mm); a border that runs off a face beside the edge along the way (checked on the body: the border may not come within the tolerance of another edge of its face; `Reason::FreeRunsOver`). Chains of tangent edges, closed-form edges in the same feature and two fillets that meet at a vertex are built since 2026-10-15 (*Chains and meets of freeform blends* below).
- **Tests** (`crates/kernel/tests/blend_freeform.rs`): a straight edge between a plane and a cone, held point by point against the definition of a rolling-ball fillet (a point of the body is kept exactly when a ball of the radius behind both faces holds it: the distance to the set of such centres, an intersection of two convex sets, by Dykstra's projections), at three scales, with the borders tangent to within the angle the tolerance makes over the radius; the rim of a slanted cut of a rod (a whole ellipse, a ring) by the same definition; the same edge chamfered (every point of the borders the distance from the edge's line); a straight edge between two spline walls (an extruded blade section), whose removed volume is the 2D fillet of the two Béziers worked out on its own (Green's theorem with Gauss–Legendre) times the height. In the engine (`crates/engine/tests/blend_freeform.rs`, `chamfer.rs`): the leading and trailing edges of the turbofan's lofted fan blade, a conical casing's cut edge, the rings of a slanted cut and a slanted hole, a spline wall chamfered against an independent integral over the strip it cuts (to 1e-7).

#### Blend findings of the engine block (M9, 2026-10-15)

An engine-block showcase found eight refusals of blends a part like it needs (the reproducers in its findings, E1–E8). The causes and what changed, all in `kernel::blend` except the last:

- **The estimate of what fits** (`fits`, which refuses a size whose cut or tangent line would leave a face before anything is built) read the face's boundary too narrowly. (1) An edge that leaves an end of the blended edge bounded the strip "up to its own far end": where the boundary runs on smoothly (a tangent sketch arc that turns by 38.7° next to a rim, "below 2.63" on a 176 mm cap, E5; a circle split at the cylinder's seam, so that a boss's root fillet depended on where the seam lay, E2), the far end of that one piece was taken as the end of the face. The boundary is now followed along the edges that go on straight from it (`smooth_run`) up to a corner, where the face can turn back. (2) Along an arc, an edge that goes on from its end along the circle's tangent lies beyond the arc's sector and bounds nothing of it (a short ledge after an inside round of the bellhousing's rail, E3). (3) A cut line that runs exactly into the corner where that boundary turns fits: a second chamfer of the same distance next to an earlier one (E7) is the solid the three make in one feature. (4) The other way round: a boundary edge that leaves an end the way the blended edge does (a rib's foot whose arc touches the line where the wall goes over into a rail's round, E8) means the face pinches to nothing there; that is refused as too large with nothing that fits, where the boolean used to refuse it ("an imprinted curve that does not fit"). What a blend would need there, running over the tangent edge onto the round, is not built.
- **Where a blend ends against a plane at a slant** (a flange root that ends on the deck's chamfer, E4, or at a corner of the block's outline where the next root is not rounded, E3): the tool used to end square across the edge, which leaves a face nobody asked for. When exactly one other face meets the end vertex and it is a plane, the tool is now made long enough to reach that plane and cut there by one more boolean (`end_plane`, `trim`): its end lies in the plane of the face, and the boolean merges it into that face, as modellers end a blend on the face it runs into. Both ways the plane can lean, convex and concave edges; the ledge that runs into a slanted wall, which the tables above listed as refused, is built the same way. A face that is not a plane there is still refused ("does not end cleanly").
- **Chamfer chains** with one distance are joined into one tool as fillet chains are (`joints`): the plane of a line's chamfer and the cone of the arc it goes on in touch along their common ruling, which the boolean refused at the finding's tolerance ("two points closer together than the tolerance can tell apart", E1).
- **The boolean** (`boolean/section.rs`, `edge_cuts`): a hit of an edge on a surface within twice the tolerance of an end of the edge that lies on the surface is taken as that end. Where an ellipse or a circle runs onto a face at a shallow angle the hit is found only to a fraction of the tolerance, while the end, which the other edges there share, is exact; two such hits of edges that meet there were two places closer together than the tolerance can tell apart (a ball corner of the crankcase bay where the outline turns by 51°, at the document's tolerance, E5b). The boolean corpus gives the same outcomes (11573 agreeing, none wrong).
- **Still refused:** all edges of a prism rounded in two steps with one radius (the rims after the long edges): along the earlier round's arc the rim's torus would reach its own axis, a sphere the closed forms do not make (one feature with ball corners builds it).
- **Tests:** `crates/kernel/tests/fillet.rs`, `chamfer.rs`, `blend_cylinders.rs`: closed forms (Pappus over the outline offset by the section's centroid, prisms between oblique planes, convex solids from their planes), against the same chamfers in one feature, against the definition of a rolling ball near a corner, at three scales and in a tilted frame, several at the finding's finer tolerance; the boss root with the seam at 0, 45, 90, 180 and 270° gives one volume. The engine block's full script builds with the same volumes as before; FreeCAD reads the reproducers' STEP as valid solids with our volumes to 2e-10.

#### Chains and meets of freeform blends (M9, 2026-10-15)

`kernel::blend::rolling::chain` and `chain::meet`. **Grouping.** The requests of one feature are grouped by their vertices (`chain::group`): where two requested edges end in one vertex and go on into each other within the angular tolerance they are a *joint* of a chain (walked from either end, or round to a closed chain); where they meet at an angle they are a *meet*; three or more at a vertex are refused. A closed-form edge that touches a freeform one (a line between two planes that continues a cone's cut edge) joins it and is built by `rolling` too; closed-form edges apart from every freeform one are blended after them by the tools and the boolean, on the body the freeform blends left (every entity of which keeps its number), and the sources are carried back (`blend::mixed`).

- **One family of balls, a face per edge.** A fillet chain is followed along the path of the balls' centres rather than in planes across the edges (the edge's planes pick balls badly where the blend lies far from its edge, as at a concave foot that crosses a fillet's tangent line at a shallow angle, nine millimetres from the vertex): from a ball, a step ahead along the path's direction (square to both normals at the feet, n₁ × n₂), the next ball in the plane square to it there, by a damped Newton step on the two distances (never more than half the radius at once); the step halves where the ball cannot be found or the path turns by more than a quarter radian, grows to a 24th of the edge where it runs straight. Each edge's face then uses the planes square to a clamped cubic spline with continuous curvature through all its balls (exact tangents at its ends), turned at the joints into the joint ball's own plane by a correction that fades out over half the edge (flat to the third derivative at both ends; a plane family that is only C1, as with a smoothstep or a Hermite path, stalls the refinement), so the faces on both sides of a joint end in exactly the same arc, which is their shared edge. A chamfer chain (no face may change at its joints) keeps the planes across the edges, which its lines depend on.
- **Joints.** Both faces go on (a vertex of the two edges only), or one goes on and the other changes into a face tangent to it across a smooth edge that leaves the vertex (cone into torus into plane along the casing's cut edge; flare, root fillet, boss along E6's boss foot), or a seam of one face passes through the vertex (crossed as a smooth edge between the face and itself). Where a face changes, the shared ball is the one whose foot on the changing side lies on the smooth edge (bisection along that edge for the point whose ball, the radius in along the common normal, is the radius from the face that goes on); the smooth edge is shortened to it, the vertex moves to the other foot, and a new vertex and the arc are added. Tangency across every joint is measured (ten times the refinement's bound).
- **Meets** (two fillets of one radius that end in one vertex at an angle; the vertex has three edges and three faces, the third edge stays sharp, all three convex or all concave). What is kept near the vertex is what both fillets keep: each fillet's face where it lies inside the other fillet's rounded corner and inside its far face (the other fillet's own face). Both faces run on past the vertex (until wholly beyond the far face, outside it for a convex meet, inside it for a concave one) and are trimmed per point across them by bisection on the larger of the two distances (beyond the far face; inside the other's corner: beyond its surface where the nearest point of it lies within its borders, beyond the face past its border on the shared face otherwise, and not the other's corner at all past its border on its own face). The two run out into each other in the curve they share, from where their borders on the shared face cross; of the two, the one whose border on its own face reaches the far face first is also cut by the far face, up to where the other fillet's border on that face meets it (taken on the other fillet's border, which crosses it, rather than along the trim, whose two pieces touch there); the third edge is shortened to where its border crosses it. Both reaching it in one point gives a vertex of four edges. Where the two run into each other there is a crease, as between two closed-form fillets that meet (§ *Fillets as built*).
- **Tests** (`crates/kernel/tests/blend_chains.rs`): the rims of the cap of an extruded smooth outline of a line, a half circle and a Bézier, closed and open (square ends), against the swept volume (Pappus-Guldinus along a plane curve: the corner's area times the length less its first moment times the turning) to 0.05 of the tolerance times the fillet's area, three scales and tilted; a casing's cut edge through its root fillet, every point of the three faces' surfaces against the rolling-ball definition (the centre the radius from the cut plane and from the wall in the meridian), eight tangent seams; a chain with a closed-form edge apart in one fillet (the volume of the two blends apart); E6's boss foot (five faces, fifteen tangent seams, the definition in the cross-section of the base); a half cone cut by a tilted plane, both top edges meeting at the cut edges, and the same as a pocket in a block (concave), every point of a grid held against the definition (kept exactly when a ball behind the top and the cone and one behind the top and the cut plane hold it, by Dykstra's projections onto the eroded convex sets), self-intersection check; a lofted blade's tip rounded all round, every mesh point of the fillets against the definition, three scales. In the engine (`crates/engine/tests/blend_chains.rs`): the turbofan's core casing cut by its block, both cut faces' chains in one feature with `chain`, refilleted after an edit; the lofted blade's tip; E6 refused at 3 mm (border off the flare's strip) and built at 1 mm.
- **Not built** (refused with the reason): a fillet whose border runs over a smooth edge of a face beside it in the middle of an edge (E6 in the full engine block: the boss's foot passes within about a millimetre of the 12 mm waist round's tangent line on the flare); concave fillets that meet round a sharp convex edge (a blade's root loop: the ball would have to roll round the edge's foot, a pinched patch); a chain whose two ends meet at one vertex; chamfers whose face changes at a joint, or that meet. A vertex patch with tangency (a setback, an n-sided patch) in place of the crease where two fillets meet is the next step for meets: the trimmed faces give its boundary.

#### Face operations as built (M9, 2026-10-14)

`feature.offsetFace`, `feature.moveFace`, `feature.draft`, `feature.shell` and `feature.thicken`; `kernel::offset_face`, `kernel::move_face`, `kernel::draft`, `kernel::shell`, `kernel::thicken`, and what they share in `kernel::tweak`; `regen::face_ops`, `regen::offset_face`, `regen::move_face`, `regen::draft`, `regen::shell`, `regen::thicken`; `engine::face_cmds`.

**Route: a local operation, and the boolean only to take the hollow out.** The roadmap's recipe for the face operations is "give a face a new surface, extend the neighbours, intersect again, sew". For the blends a tool solid and the boolean was the better route (above); for moving a face it is not. Inward, the body cut by the half-space behind a moved plane would be exact; outward, the new material is bounded by the neighbours' surfaces continued past their edges, so a tool would have to be built from exactly the computation that closes the body directly, and the boolean would then add a face (a block whose top moves up would have seven faces, the old top flush with a new one, until merged). So `kernel::tweak::resurface` does it directly, and nothing about how the body hangs together changes: every face, edge and vertex of the result is the one with the same number in the body given. (1) Every vertex on a face with a new surface is found again where the surfaces of its faces meet, nearest where it was: Newton's iteration on the signed distances to the surfaces, with the smallest step that puts the point on every surface whose normal adds a direction (one step for planes; a face tangent to another says nothing the other does not, and is held to its distance at the end). (2) Every edge that ends in such a vertex is made again on its two faces' surfaces: a straight edge through its two vertices; a circular edge about the axis of the round one of its faces (a cylinder, a cone, a torus, or a sphere about the line along the old circle's axis), through its first vertex, with its frame's axes kept so its parameters keep their meaning; an elliptic or spline edge only when neither face moved and its ends still lie on it. Each is held at five points against both surfaces. (3) The result is held against what a body is: an edge that shrinks to nothing or turns round (which is how a vanishing neighbour shows), a flat face whose loops change their turning or whose outline crosses itself or loses a hole (`profile::detect_profiles` on the face's loops), a round face whose rims change places along its axis, `topo::check`, and next to every moved edge a point just outside and just inside each face, which the booleans' ray classification must find outside and inside (a face that ran into another part of the body, or into the plane of another face, fails this). What such local checks cannot see, two faces far apart that ran into each other, regeneration finds on the mesh (`tess::body_self_intersections`, as for lofts) before the body is handed on.

**What moves.** A flat face goes to the parallel plane at the distance; a cylinder and a sphere change their radius, a torus its tube's; a cone keeps its axis and angle and its radius changes by the distance over the cosine of the half-angle (its frame moves along the axis when the apex passes it). Positive is outward, away from the material: a hole narrows, a boss thickens. So the neighbours that follow are flat faces at any angle (the slanted sides of a frustum are continued until they meet the moved top: closed form at three scales), round faces about an axis the moved face is square to (a hole through a face that moves gets longer) and the flat faces across an axis when the round face moves (the mouth and the floor of a hole, the foot and the crown of a boss, a ball half sunk in a block).

**Too far.** A failure of the checks in (3), or of a vertex to be found, is a matter of how far when a smaller distance works: the distance is halved (48 times at most, each a whole resurface) for the largest that fits, and the error names the edge (by the two faces it lies between) or the face, the reason and that bound ("what fits is below 3.99999 mm"). When no distance at all works, the geometry is one that is not built and that is said instead, with the faces.

**Not built, and refused with the faces:** a neighbour tangent to the moved face (the top next to a fillet: its continuation does not meet the moved plane where the edge was); a vertex where four or more faces meet that would have to become an edge (a slanted side of a frustum on a plinth); a freeform face next to a moved one outward (it cannot be continued past its edge) and an elliptic or spline edge on a moved face; the offset of a freeform face; a cone whose radius at its frame would turn negative in a way the frame cannot follow. Replace face is not built; see the M9 row of the roadmap. Move face, thicken, draft and delete face are built (below).

**Shell.** `kernel::shell` offsets every face of the solid at once by the thickness, inward, except the open faces, which move outward by the same amount, so that the hollow reaches through them and the opening is as wide as the hollow is there. Since the offset keeps every entity, the hollow has the solid's faces, face for face. The shell is the solid less the hollow (`boolean_with`, the operands trusted since both were just checked); without open faces the hollow lies inside, the boolean leaves it as a cavity, and the result has two shells. After the boolean: every face that keeps a wall must be there as skin and as a face of the hollow, and the hollow must come out through every open face (it is gone, or an edge of the boolean crosses it), else an error with the face. A rounded box gets inner rounds of the radius less the thickness; at a thickness of the radius or more they would vanish into a sharp edge, which needs the hollow's topology to change, and is refused with the round and the largest thickness that fits. Behind an open face between walls that stand round it (the floor of a pocket) the hollow is wider than the opening, and a piece of the open face moved out is the hollow's ceiling there; the result is a valid solid, measured in the tests. A boss that stands on an open face would be cut loose by the hollow coming out round it, leaving a body in two pieces; that is not tried in the tests and not refused yet (open). FreeCAD's `makeThickness` gives the same volume, area and counts as ours on a box, a cup, a tube, a rounded tray (three thicknesses) and an L (with its join set to intersection; its default puts an arc in the hollow's outside corner), and `makeOffsetShape` the same as an offset of every face of a block and of a cylinder (`target/scratch-face-ops/thickness.py`; only the cylinders' seam edges differ in the counts).

**Move face** (`feature.moveFace`, `kernel::move_face`, `regen::move_face`; 2026-10-15). Faces of a solid move together by one rigid motion, given as `feature.transform` gives one (a turn by `angle` about the line through `pivot` along `axis`, then a shift by `translate`). Every moved face gets the image of its surface (`Surface::transformed`, which keeps the parametrisation), and `tweak::resurface_rigid` closes the body: a vertex all of whose faces move and an edge both of whose faces move are the images of what they were, exactly and of any kind (so a boss moved with its top keeps its top rim as it was, and two moved faces that meet in an ellipse still do); everything between a moved face and one that stays is found again as for an offset, with the same checks. So the top of a block shifted up is an offset (a slanted shift counts by its part along the normal), tilted about a line across it is a wedge, the wall of a hole or the wall and top of a boss moved across their face put the hole or the boss elsewhere. **Too far** is the offset's halving on the part of the motion (the same part of the shift and of the turn): the kernel's `value` and `largest` are how far the farthest vertex of a moved face goes and may go, and regeneration says the part and the shift and angle it is (`translate, angle: the move is too far: the edge shrinks to nothing …; what fits is below 0.833333 of it, translate (0, 0, -4.99…) mm`). **Refused, with the faces,** where the topology round a moved face would have to change: an edge between a moved face and one that stays that would no longer be a line or a circle (a cylinder tilted against a flat face meets it in an ellipse: `OFF_SURFACE` at every part of the motion, so not "too far"), and everything the offset refuses (a tangent neighbour that stays, a vertex of four faces, a freeform neighbour). **Names** are an offset's: every face, edge and vertex is the copy of what it was, so a fillet on an edge of a moved face survives edits of the shift and the turn (`crates/engine/tests/move_face.rs`, with the fillet's closed form along an edge that runs across a tilted top). A sketch on a moved face lies on it and is turned with it the shortest way onto its new normal (`sketch::resolve_face`, `turned_onto`): for a turn that tilts the face, which is every turn that changes it, that is the face's own turn. *Tests:* `crates/kernel/tests/move_face.rs` (shifts, a wedge about the middle and about an edge, a hole and a boss moved, a tilted frame, at 1e-3, 1 and 1e6; the part that fits; the tilted hole refused; bad input) and `crates/engine/tests/move_face.rs` (the same through the command, checked deep, the fillet that survives, the sketch that turns, too far and refused in words, bad parameters, `feature.edit`, save and reopen, undo and redo).

**Draft** (`feature.draft`, `kernel::draft`, `regen::draft`; 2026-10-15). Faces of a solid tilted by an angle about a neutral plane so that the part comes out of a mould along the pull direction, the plane's normal. The neutral plane is a world plane, a datum plane or a flat face (`PlaneRef`, as datum planes take it); for a face the pull goes *into* the body the face bounds, so a block drafted with its bottom face as neutral narrows toward its top and with its top face toward its bottom, and `reversed` turns the pull round. A drafted face keeps the line or circle in which it meets the neutral plane and leans in by the angle as it goes along the pull; a negative angle leans out. **Closed forms only:** a flat face whose normal is square to the pull turns about the line it shares with the neutral plane (the rotation about that line, applied to its frame: still a plane); a cylinder whose axis runs along the pull becomes the cone about that axis whose radius at the neutral plane is the cylinder's and whose half-angle is the draft angle, narrowing along the pull for a boss and widening for a hole. Then `tweak::resurface` closes the body as for an offset: the top of a drafted block shrinks, edges between drafted faces are found again where the new surfaces meet, and a flat face tangent to a drafted cylinder stays tangent to its cone, since both are drafted from the line or circle they share at the neutral plane: the vertex where they and the top meet is found where the plane and the top meet, nearest where it was, which is the point of tangency (the "one direction" rule of the iteration), so a rounded box drafted is a rounded frustum, to its closed form. **Too far** is the offset's halving on the angle: an angle at which a face is drawn to nothing (the top of a frustum to a point) fails with the edge and the largest angle that fits. **Refused, with the face and the reason, before anything is built:** a flat face whose normal is not square to the pull (the top of the block, a face drafted already), a cylinder whose axis is not along it, a cone (it has an angle of its own; changing it is not built), a sphere, a torus and a freeform face (no closed form of the kind we build), and round them what the offset refuses (a neighbour tangent to a drafted face that is not drafted itself, a vertex of four faces). **Names** are an offset's: every face, edge and vertex the copy of what it was; a sketch on a drafted face turns with it as on a moved one. *Tests:* `crates/kernel/tests/draft.rs` (a block from its bottom, its top and its middle, one side, a rod and a hole into frusta of cones, a rounded box into a rounded frustum, a tilted frame, at 1e-3, 1 and 1e6; the angle that fits; what is refused) and `crates/engine/tests/draft.rs` (the same through the command with the neutral plane as a face, `xy` and reversed, a datum plane whose distance is a variable with a fillet on the drafted top and a peg on a drafted side through edits of the level and the angle, too steep and refused in words, bad parameters, `feature.edit` of the neutral plane, the angle, the faces and the pull, save and reopen, undo and redo).

**Thicken** (`feature.thicken`, `kernel::thicken`, `regen::thicken`; 2026-10-15). Nothing in the history makes a sheet body yet (`BodyKind::Sheet` exists for imports and tests only), so what is thickened is a set of faces of a solid, into a solid of its own: the layer between those faces and the same faces moved along their normals by the thickness, `outside` (away from the material, the default), `inside` or `both` (half each way). The source keeps its body (`FeatureKind::source` says so, as `keep` does for a transform); the layer is joined to it, or to anything, with a boolean. **Method:** two offsets and the boolean, as the shell: the solid with the faces moved outward by the part outside (or the solid itself) less the solid with them moved inward by the part inside (or the solid itself). So at its rim the layer is bounded by the faces round the set, *continued as an offset continues them*, not by walls along the faces' normals: the top of a block thickened is a plate as wide as the block, the wall of a rod a tube as long as the rod, the wall of a hole a sleeve in it or round it, the top and a side of a block an angle, the slanted top of a frustum thickened inward a cap whose rim lies in the slanted sides. That is the layer a solid's faces have, and it is the one route that needs no new kind of face; a thickness along the normals at a rim that is not square to its neighbours (a frustum's top thickened outward with upright walls) is not built. Every face of the set must come out on both sides of the layer, else it is refused with the face; a thickness at which a face of either offset vanishes is the offset's "too far", in the whole thickness (also for `both`). **Names** are a shell's: a face lying on or along a face of the source is its copy in instance 0 (`F9:copy(0)/F1:face(1)` is the far side of a plate on a block's top, `F9:copy(0)/F1:face(2)` its rim along the front), one lying under a face of the set its copy in instance 1 (the near side); edges and vertices are named by their faces with the copies of their old names as aliases. A sketch on the near side looks into the source, as on the faces of a hollow (`sketch::resolve_face`). *Tests:* `crates/kernel/tests/thicken.rs` (a plate on, under and through a block's top, an angle on two faces, a tube on a rod, sleeves in and round a hole, a tilted frame, at 1e-3, 1 and 1e6; what fits; bad input) and `crates/engine/tests/thicken.rs` (the same through the command, checked deep, the plate joined back into a taller block, the source kept, a fillet on the layer and a pin from its near side through edits of the thickness and the block, `feature.edit` to another face and side, save and reopen, undo and redo, bad parameters).

*Read back by FreeCAD:* `step_freecad/face_ops.rs` has a second set for these three (a wedge, a boss moved with its top, an angle thickened on two faces of a block, a sleeve on both sides of a hole's wall, a drafted frustum and a drafted rounded box, at 1e-3, 1 and 1e6): FreeCAD finds valid solids with our volume and area within 1.2e-15 and our counts (bar the seams it adds to closed faces), 18 files.

**Delete face** (`feature.deleteFace`, `kernel::delete_face`, `regen::delete_face`; 2026-10-14). The faces given fall into groups that hang together by edges, and each group is closed on its own, one after the other; a group that neither way below fits is refused with the faces (and the edge) and the reason, and nothing is built.
- *(a) Holes filled.* A group whose every edge to the rest of the body lies in a hole of a face round it, each such hole bounded by the group alone: the wall of a hole through a plate, the wall and floor of a blind hole, the wall and top of a boss, the walls and floor of a pocket, the faces of a closed cavity (a whole shell with nothing round it). The group goes, those loops go out of their faces, and the faces round it go on across where the holes were on the surfaces they had: purely topological. What is checked: every such loop is a hole of its face (sampled and taken into the face's own parameters, where the normal is ∂u × ∂v, an outer loop turns counter-clockwise seen from outside and a hole clockwise; a loop that goes round a periodic direction, the rim of a cylinder, is no hole; one round a pole of a sphere cannot be told and is refused), a neighbour that shares only part of a loop with the group refuses it, the result passes `topo::check` and encloses a positive volume (deleting the outside of a hollow block leaves the cavity inside out: refused).
- *(b) A strip collapsed.* One face with one loop of four edges (two sides along the faces beside it, an end against a face at each end: a chamfer or a fillet along one edge) or with two loops of one closed edge each (the round of a rod's rim, of a hole's mouth, of a boss's foot). The face goes, its two sides become one edge between the faces beside it, the two vertices of each end edge become one, and `tweak::resurface_at` finds those vertices where the surfaces round them meet and makes every edge ending in them again on its faces' surfaces, with all of `resurface`'s checks: the faces beside the strip are continued until they meet, and the faces at its ends lose the end edge and gain the corner. Which edges are the sides: the two along which the face is tangent to its neighbours when there are such (a fillet), else both pairings are tried, the longer sides first, and the first that closes the body is taken (a chamfer on a block: its ends lie in parallel faces that never meet). Refused, with the faces: an end face tangent to the strip or to a face beside it (the next blend of a chain, the ball in a corner of three fillets), an end vertex where more than three faces meet (two blends in a mitre, a corner of the part), faces beside it that do not meet in a line or a circle when continued (the top of a block or of a thin wall, the wall of a boss alone), the edges they would meet in being elliptic or freeform.
- *Regeneration* refuses the corner where blends meet by its name (`chamferCorner`, `filletCorner`) before the kernel would refuse it in numbers, and meshes the result to find two faces far apart that the continuation ran into (`tess::body_self_intersections`, as for offsets). Faces are copies (`F9:copy(0)/…`), edges and vertices are named by their faces with the copy of their old name as an alias; the edge a strip collapsed into and the vertex its ends became have only the name by their faces. After a block, a fillet on an edge and the fillet face deleted, the block's own names (`F1:face(1)`) do not answer again: the faces answer to `F3:copy(0)/F2:copy(0)/F1:face(1)`, as after every feature made from another, and the restored sharp edge is the edge between two such names (a chamfer on it is built in the tests).
- *Tests:* `crates/kernel/tests/delete_face.rs` (a block filleted or chamfered on an edge, upright or in a tilted frame, inside fillets and chamfers of a step, the round and the bevel of a rod's rim, of a hole's mouth and of a boss's foot, two fillets at once, a hole, a blind hole, a boss, a pocket, a cross hole in a rod and a cavity deleted, each against the body before the blend or the cut: volume, area and counts to 1e-9 at 1e-3, 1 and 1e6; what is refused), `crates/engine/tests/face_ops.rs` (the same through the command with `document.check {"deep": true}`, upstream edits that move the hole, a hole that misses so the face is gone, `causedBy`, `feature.edit`, inserting at the rollback marker, save and reopen, undo and redo, bad parameters), a row of `failed_features.rs`, and `step_freecad/face_ops.rs` (five cases at three scales: FreeCAD finds valid solids of our volume within 1.2e-15).

**References and names** (§4.1): faces by name, all of one body (offset) or of `source` (shell's `open`), resolved at every regeneration as the blends resolve theirs; a cap's name survives a change of its outline as everywhere. The result's faces are copies: `F9:copy(0)/…` for every face of an offset (moved or not; a face that moved is still that face) and for the shell's skin; a face of the hollow is the copy in instance 1 of the face it lies behind, `F9:copy(1)/F2:side(e7)`. After an offset every edge and vertex is the copy of what it was (as after a move); in a shell they are named by their faces with the copy of their old name as an alias. A sketch on a face that an offset moved lies where the face lies now (`sketch::resolve_face` drops the frame's origin onto the face's plane and keeps its axes); a sketch on a face of the hollow looks into the hollow (its normal is the face's own, its x axis that of the face it lies behind, so its y is mirrored, as a wall seen from behind). The same holds for a face a subtraction keeps of its tool, turned inside out (the floor of a pocket, the wall of a hole), since 2026-10-14: a sketch or a datum plane on it looks out of the material, its x axis that of the tool's face and its y mirrored (`sketch::resolve_face`; before, it looked into the material as the tool's face did, and a pin extruded from a pocket's floor went down).

- *Tests:* `crates/kernel/tests/offset_face.rs` and `shell.rs` (closed forms at 1e-3, 1 and 1e6; the largest distance that fits; what is refused), `crates/engine/tests/face_ops.rs` (the same through the commands with `document.check {"deep": true}`, upstream edits, a lost face, `feature.edit`, sketches on a moved face and on a face of the hollow, a chamfer on an edge of a shell, save and reopen, undo and redo, bad parameters), two rows of `failed_features.rs`, and `step_freecad/face_ops.rs` (33 files, FreeCAD finds valid solids of our volume within 3e-15).

#### Booleans as built (M7, generalised 2026-10-13)

`kernel::boolean::boolean(a, b, op, tolerance)` returns a `BooleanResult`: the body and, per face, edge and vertex of it, a source (`FaceSource`, `EdgeSource`, `VertexSource`). `boolean_with` takes `BooleanOptions` (`merge`, on by default). `boolean::contains_points` classifies points against a solid with the same ray casting the booleans use; it does not go through `mass_properties`, so a count of sampled points measures a body independently of it.

**Method** (imprint, classify, select, sew, merge; one file per step in `crates/kernel/src/boolean/`):

1. *Section* (`section.rs`). For each face of A and face of B that can meet (their boxes meet and neither lies wholly to one side of the other's surface), the curves in which their surfaces meet are asked of `meet_surfaces`, with the box where both bodies are. A curve is cut where an edge of either face meets the other face's surface (`hits.rs`: the closed forms of `geom::intersect`, and where there is none the roots of the signed distance along the curve, isolated by the fact that the distance changes no faster than the curve moves), and where the curve itself ends. A stretch between two cuts is kept when its middle is in or on both faces. The boolean does not know which pair of surfaces a curve came from, nor whether it is exact: a vertex is always where an *edge's own curve* meets a surface, and is then found on the stretch within the tolerance.
2. *Imprint.* Edges are split at the vertices found on them. Edge pieces and stretches are registered as arcs; two arcs between the same vertices along the same curve are one arc. An edge of B that lies on a face of A, or along an edge of A, is shared from then on, and nothing is matched by position afterwards.
3. *Regions* (`regions.rs`). A face with arcs across it falls into regions: around each vertex the arcs are sorted by direction in the tangent plane (at the apex of a cone: seen along the axis), arcs that leave in one direction are told apart a little further along, and loops are followed keeping the region on the left. An arc with the same region on both sides divides nothing and is dropped. Loops are grouped in a chart of the surface: a loop that goes around a point just to its left is an outside, any other a hole of the innermost outside around it.
4. *Classify* (`classify.rs`, `chart.rs`). One point well inside a region decides for the region: on a face of the other solid with a coincident surface, or inside or outside the other solid by ray casting (a ray that meets an edge or grazes a face is abandoned for another, twelve at most; a point within twice the tolerance of the other body is no use, and the next of up to four is tried). **Inside or outside a face is a winding number in a chart of its surface**, computed from points in space, so no seam or pole is a special place: a plane is its own chart; a cylinder is mapped conformally (angle and `exp(height / radius)` as polar coordinates, evaluated without the exponential); a cone is dropped along its axis onto a plane, which makes the apex an ordinary point; a sphere is projected stereographically from a point chosen clear of the boundary; a torus is cut open along a circle that the boundary does not cross; a freeform face has its own parameters, drawn as a wedge or a lens where a border of its patch is one point. A face on a sphere or a torus may contain the place its chart leaves out; the winding of its loops about a point just inside it says which. A face without loops is its whole surface.
5. *Select.* See the table below.
6. *Sew* (`sew.rs`). Faces connected through edges form a shell. Where more than two faces meet along an arc, faces and material alternate around it: each face that runs against the arc is paired with the next face counter-clockwise (faces tangent there are put in order a little way off the arc) and every pair gets its own copy of the edge. Where several fans of faces meet at a vertex, every fan gets its own copy of the vertex. The copies are in one place and share nothing, so every result is a manifold. A bounded face on a sphere gets a frame whose poles are clear of its boundary (a sphere is the same surface whichever way its poles point; what reads a face through its parameters is at its best that way).
7. *Merge* (`merge.rs`). Faces that share an edge and lie on one surface facing one way are one face when they are pieces of one face of an operand, or pieces of a face of A and a face of B that are coincident; every edge inside such a group goes and the loops are spliced where it was. A vertex left with two edges that continue each other along one curve goes too, unless its operand had it like that. The face that remains has the source of the lowest-numbered face of A in the group, else of B; a joined edge the lesser of its sources (an edge of A before an edge of B before an intersection). The result passes `topo::check` or the operation fails.
