| Region of | Outside the other | Inside the other | Coincident, same normal | Coincident, opposite normals |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| A, union | kept | | kept (the source is A's face) | |
| A, intersection | | kept | kept (the source is A's face) | |
| A, difference | kept | | | kept |
| B, union | kept | | | |
| B, intersection | | kept | | |
| B, difference | | kept, turned inside out | | |

**Contact without overlap.** Bodies that only touch (face against face along a line, a circle or at a point; an edge against a face or an edge; a vertex against anything) have nothing in common: the union is both bodies as shells that share no entity, the intersection is `EmptyResult`, the difference is the first body with the entities it had. A curve along which two faces touch is imprinted like any other; where the result does not need it (it ends inside a face, or has one face on both sides) it leaves no trace, and where it does (a cylinder that touches all four sides of its block cuts it into four parts) it is an edge. A result whose parts hang together only by an edge or a vertex has that edge or vertex once per part.

**Supported and not** (tests: `crates/kernel/tests/boolean.rs`, `boolean_surfaces.rs`, `boolean_contact.rs`, `boolean_random.rs`, `boolean_corpus.rs`):

| Configuration | Result |
|---|---|
| faces on planes, cylinders, cones, spheres and tori; whole spheres and tori; edges on lines, circles, ellipses and splines | operands like any other |
| coincident faces (same or opposite normals; overlapping, touching or sharing edges), on any of those surfaces | handled by the table above, and merged |
| faces that touch along a curve or at a point; bodies that touch along an edge or at a vertex | handled: see *Contact without overlap* |
| curves that leave a vertex in one direction (a circle tangent to an edge it meets) | handled |
| a boundary through the apex of a cone or a pole of a sphere | handled |
| intersection curves that are approximations (splines within a deviation of both surfaces) | handled: tolerant edges |
| a pair of surfaces whose intersection `meet_surfaces` does not give | `Unsupported`, with its reason |
| a Bézier, B-spline or rational NURBS face in either body (a domed block against a block, a cylinder, a cone, a ball, a torus or another dome; a rational patch that is a piece of a cylinder, a cone, a sphere or a torus, or none of those, against the same) | operands like any other: see *Freeform faces* below; the curves in which its surface meets another are splines within the tolerance |
| a freeform face with a pole (a border of its patch that is one point: a dome or a drop swept by a spline that ends on the axis, an octant of a ball as a rational patch), cut away from the pole, around it or through it | an operand like any other since 2026-10-14: see *Freeform faces with a pole* below |
| a freeform face on a surface that closes on itself (in u, in v, both ways, with a pole at one end or at each; with or without a seam edge), cut across its seam, along it, through its poles or around it | an operand like any other since 2026-10-14: see *Freeform faces that close on themselves* below |
| a freeform face that closes into one point on two adjacent borders (a pole at a corner of its parameters: a lune of a ball by stereographic projection, a cone's apex at a corner), cut away from the pole, around it, through it and at the far corner | an operand like any other since 2026-10-15: see *Poles at a corner of the parameters* below |
| a freeform face whose parametrisation folds, or stalls anywhere but at a border that is one point; one that closes into a point on more than two borders, or on two adjacent ones without leaving it in a fan; one that comes to its point as a cusp or folds back over it; a rational one whose weights differ by more than a factor of a thousand within one span (and, short of that, a hundred apart: see *Rational faces*) | `Unsupported`, with its reason |
| a freeform face that lies on the surface of a face of the other body, where that face is analytic (a rational quarter cylinder against the cylinder it is a piece of, a rational zone of a ball against the ball) | handled since 2026-10-14: `meet_surfaces` finds that the patch lies on the surface; a point of the analytic face beyond the patch is off the freeform face (not placed in the patch's chart), and the two faces are not merged (a face on a patch ends where the patch does; pieces of one patch still are) |
| a freeform face on a face of the other body that is not wholly under it on both sides (the rational quarter rod against its analytic twin moved along the axis), and two freeform faces on one surface (against itself moved, against a wider patch of itself, upright and tilted) | handled since 2026-10-15: see *Coincident freeform faces* below |
| two faces between half and twice the tolerance apart where one is over the other; two faces of different kinds that go through each other by that much; two points likewise; a piece of a face thinner than about eight tolerances | `Unsupported` ("at the edge of tolerance") |
| two faces of different kinds that go through each other by less than half the tolerance | touching: nothing is imprinted |
| a curve in which two faces cross, strictly inside both, that ends up dividing neither | `Unsupported` ("imprint lost"): a consistency check, zero on the corpus and the random runs |
| more than two faces along an edge where two of them are tangent to second order | `Unsupported` |
| a cylinder that goes on in a torus across a border the two faces share (the fillet of a line and of the arc it continues in): tangent along a circle that is not about one axis of both, with a second curve that crosses it; cones tangent along a ruling up to an apex | handled since 2026-10-14 (the closed forms of §5.1); before that `Unsupported` ("surfaces that touch along a curve … without a closed form") |
| a torus that goes on in another torus (tubes of one radius, middle circles tangent in one plane, from outside or from inside) | handled since 2026-10-14 (the closed form of §5.1) |
| any other pair that touches along a curve or to more than second order and crosses through it without a closed form (a cylinder whose axis is square to a torus' axis, inside the tube and touching its top: fourth-order contact, the random runs' seed 2001169) | `Unsupported` ("surfaces that touch along a curve … without a closed form", "marching … stalled", or "two curves leave a point in the same direction and stay together"; pinned in `boolean_contact.rs` › `a_cylinder_touching_the_top_of_a_torus_tube_from_inside_is_refused`). Found 2026-10-15: along its top line the cylinder rises above the tube by `y⁴ / (8 R² r)`, so the surfaces meet in two curves `θ = ±c y²` tangent to each other at the point of contact (a tacnode of `f`, whose Hessian there has rank one). The marcher does not stop there: its stationary search takes no step where the Hessian is flat and the cells' centres are not tangent points, so no singular point is made, and branches pass through the point and run on along either arm; two of them then cover one arm, which the regions refuse. Owed: find the point (Newton across the ridge, then along it), seed its four arms from the ridge (`θ = θ*(y) ± √(h(y) / a)`), end branches there as at a crossing, and keep the corrector on its own arm where the arms are a few tolerances apart. |
| a curve that runs a few tolerances from a cone without a closed form for it (a circle about the cone's axis; a line along a ruling through the apex, on the cone up to the apex) | handled since 2026-10-14 in `hits.rs`: a circle about the axis of a cone, sphere or torus is at one distance all round and is decided by one point (when its tilt and offset from the axis leave no doubt); a line along a ruling through the apex is on the cone on the cone's side and meets it at the apex. Before, the root isolation (which has no bound on a cone's bend) cut such a curve into pieces the size of that distance and refused "a curve that runs along a surface" |
| a touch where a curve dips through a surface by less than half the tolerance between two crossings (a cone's rim a tenth of a tolerance over a face whose edge it touches in the plane: the cone's side, carried on, crosses the edge twice, half a millimetre apart) | handled since 2026-10-14: the touch is put where the curve goes deepest, between the crossings, not on whichever crossing the search for the nearest point happened on (which split the edge where the rim, touching it between them, did not, and was refused as "an imprinted curve that does not fit"); a deeper dip keeps a crossing |

The curves come from `geom::intersect::meet_surfaces` (since 2026-10-13; the stand-in of the first version is gone): exact where there is a closed form, splines within the tolerance otherwise. What it cannot give (surfaces tangent or nearly coincident over an area, a touch along a curve without closed form) is refused with its reason. Curve/surface hits use geometry's closed forms for lines, circles and ellipses against planes and cylinders, and for every other analytic pair the bounded root isolation in `hits.rs`, which takes a piece as given (a hit at its end is a touch) and never samples. `BooleanResult::merged` lists, per face, every face of the operands it has a piece of; `BooleanOptions::trust_operands` skips the validity check of operands the kernel itself returned.

**Tolerances.** Points within half the linear tolerance are one vertex; between that and twice the tolerance the operation refuses. The same holds for faces: two faces on one surface, or on surfaces that never cross, are the same surface within half the tolerance of each other where one is over the other, apart at twice the tolerance or more, and refused in between. Joined faces are not moved onto each other: the volume of such a result is right to the area in contact times the gap. The counterpart for faces of different kinds (a ball that dips into a plate, a torus lying a hair too deep in a block): beside every curve in which two faces cross, `section::passage` walks one surface away from the curve both ways until it is twice the tolerance from the other. Where that happens on opposite sides of the other surface the faces cross, exactly as given. Where it happens on the same side, the surface went through the other and came back: a lens thinner than twice the tolerance, whose thickness is measured; up to half the tolerance the faces touch and nothing is imprinted, above it the operation refuses. A body ten tolerances thick is a body. No decision is taken on the sign of a number that could be rounding: membership is a winding number, a root is isolated by a bound and not by sampling, and what cannot be told is refused.

**Speed (2026-10-13).** Measured by stage on a cylinder through a plate with 1024 holes (1030 faces): classification 73 %, section 24 %, both from asking every boundary piece of a face with a thousand loops about every point. Now each outline keeps a box per boundary piece, sorted along x (`outline::Boxes`), and "is anything nearer than r" looks at the few pieces near the point; in a plane a loop is not followed around a point outside its box; edges are split only near a vertex; a region is classified by one point, the others being looked for only when it does not decide; a whole face away from the other solid's box is outside it. Bit-identical results (a digest of every result of the corpus and the random runs is printed by the tests). 100 holes one by one 371 → 134 ms, 1024 holes 82 → 9.9 s, the cylinder through the 1030-face plate 269 → 47 ms, two plates of 1030 faces each 0.64 to 0.85 s per operation.

**Speed (2026-10-14): what is asked again is answered from memory.** Measured by phase on an aircraft built by commands (not in the repository: a revolved spline fuselage, lofted cubic wings mirrored, a tailplane and a fin, five unions, every face a rational or polynomial NURBS face but the end caps): building the `PatchTree`s of the operands' freeform faces about a fifth of each union (a fuselage half 7848 nodes, about 10 µs a node for its speed bound), classification and the clipping of the curves nearly half, nearly all of it nearest-point searches (a winding number about a point of a freeform face projects every end and every bisection point of the face's boundary into its chart, and the next winding number about another point does it again), `meet_surfaces` a quarter to a third, the operand check and the final check a few percent. Five memos, each a pure function remembered, so that nothing changes but the time:
- *the nearest point per tree* (`PatchTree::nearest`), by the bits of the point asked about: nearly three quarters of the searches of a union were for a point asked about before (up to 16 384 points per tree, then forgotten all at once);
- *the trees*, shared between booleans (`PatchTree::shared`, least recently used out): in a row of unions each operand's faces are on the surfaces the last one had, and the fuselage is indexed once instead of five times. *Since 2026-10-16* up to 1024 trees and 768 MiB (`PatchTree::bytes`: the nodes and the pieces the leaves keep, about 520 bytes a node); it was 32 trees and 131 072 nodes, about ten trees of a lofted blade (12 000 to 16 000 nodes each). The impeller of the showcase (a hub with six blades and six splitters, lofted, filleted and chamfered: 62 freeform faces, some 860 000 nodes) then took its trees round the shelf in the order its booleans asked for them, each pushing out the one asked for next, and every boolean on the finished body built all of them again: 124 trees for a chamfer of the two bore circles (12 s of its 12, CPU), 62 for the bore's subtraction (8 s), 68 for the union with the splitters. A boolean holds the trees of every freeform face of both operands while it works, so the shelf keeps no more than such a body takes anyway (the peak memory of the impeller's run is the same, 0.7 GB). Measured (release, CPU time, a machine other agents loaded; `craft3d-cli run` of `impeller.steps.json`): the chamfer 8 to 17 s before, 1.1 to 1.7 s after; the bore 3.7 to 8 s, 0.9 to 1.1 s; the union with the splitters 18 to 31 s, 12 to 16 s; the fan blades of the turbofan 12 to 16 s, 5 to 9 s. Every output line of both runs is the same text. `the_trees_of_a_body_of_many_freeform_faces_are_built_once` counts the trees built (`trees_built`), not time;
- *the checked bodies* (`boolean::checked`, up to 8 bodies and 32 MB written out): a union's result is checked as a result and again as the first operand of the next; written out, a body is compared in about a twentieth of what checking it costs. Since 2026-10-15 written out as its bits rather than as text (`craft3d_math::Bits`, implemented in `math`, `geom` and `topo` by destructuring every field, so a field added later does not compile until it is written out too; a list starts with its length, an enum with its variant): formatting every number of a plate with a thousand holes was a quarter of a long row of cuts;
- *the projectors* of `geom::intersect` (`Projector::shared`, 32), the sampled grid a freeform field starts its nearest-point search from, made once per surface instead of once or twice per pair of faces;
- *the numeric intersections* (`meet_surfaces`, after the closed forms: 64 of them, 2¹⁸ curve points at most): the union, difference and intersection of the same bodies ask the same questions (the three booleans of a corpus case: its slowest case, a cylinder lying on a spline roof, spent 1.1 s of each of its three booleans marching the same 700 000 cells), and so does a boolean done again on operands an edit did not touch.

A key is the thing written out in full (`{:?}`, which writes every `f64` so that it reads back to the same bits, -0.0 included, or for the checked bodies the bits themselves), never a hash, so two things are one entry only when every number of them is the same. Each memo is a `Mutex`, poisoning ignored, and nothing is computed under a lock. Besides the memos, two scans that looked at every node of a projector's 25 × 25 grid per sample (the nearest node, and the curvature near a point) look only at the nodes in a window along x, from a list of the nodes in order of x: a node whose x alone is further than the best so far is further whatever rounding does, so the answer is the same node, ties to the first in the grid's order included (held to the full scan at three scales, at a pole where nodes coincide, and halfway between nodes); and the leaf of a tree gives its own box to a bound query instead of one made again. On the aircraft the five unions went from 3.8 to 1.2 s (CPU time per union, the least of three runs each, on a loaded machine; the two ran in turn); what is left is `meet_surfaces` (about half: the quadtree that finds every branch, some 27 000 samples of the fuselage's field for the two curves of a wing root), classification (a fifth) and the checks. Bit for bit the same: the verbose corpus output of every one of its 12 097 outcomes, its digest, the long random runs, and `craft3d-cli info` and the STL and OBJ meshes of every showcase document and of the aircraft. Not taken, because they would give other (equally valid) curves: asking `meet_surfaces` about the box where the two faces are rather than where the two bodies are; tighter derivative bounds or a cell budget from curvature in the marcher's quadtree; a step from curvature where it marches; a box tree of the field surface that settles empty cells without a nearest-point search (a third of the samples are empty cells, but nearly all within four times the cell's reach of the surface, where a box bound proves little).

**Freeform faces (2026-10-13, `boolean/freeform.rs`).** A Bézier or B-spline face is indexed by a `PatchTree`: the surface as Bézier pieces (`geom`: `NurbsSurface::bezier_spans` by knot insertion, `RationalBezierPatch` with de Casteljau splitting), each quartered five times, with the box of every piece's control net (the piece lies in it). The tree answers what the booleans ask of a surface: a bound below the distance from a point to the surface that never overestimates (the distance to the nearest box, split further on the spot to a quarter of the tolerance or of the distance), which is what calls a stretch of a curve provably clear of the surface in the root isolation of `hits.rs`; the nearest point, by Newton's iteration (second derivatives from differences of the first, steps held to the domain's sides) from the middle of the nearest piece and from every piece whose box is nearer than the best point found; the signed distance; and a bound on how fast the parameters change per length travelled on the surface (the smallest singular value of the Jacobian, from the Bézier nets of the derivatives and of the normal field), which the chart of the face needs: a freeform face's chart is its own parameter rectangle, reached through the nearest point. A `Sheet` (a surface with its index) is what the helpers of `outline`, `hits`, `section`, `classify`, `regions` and `chart` take; an `Outline` builds the tree for a freeform face once and shares it with the pieces of the face. The hull of a freeform face includes its control net. Where a freeform surface meets another, the surfaces count as crossing (no lens is looked for). A NURBS surface or curve evaluated beyond its domain continues the end span's polynomial, as a Bézier does, because the marcher of the surface intersections needs something to correct onto past a border. Since the evening of 2026-10-13 the root isolation (`hits.rs`, `numeric`) judges a whole stretch of a curve against a freeform face at once wherever the curve's bend is bounded (lines, circles, ellipses and splines, rational ones included, whose second derivative is bounded through the nets of their Bézier pieces: `NurbsCurve::derivative_bounds`): a stretch on the surface is proven to stay within the tolerance from the distances at its ends and a bound on the surface's second derivatives over its domain (from the Bézier nets: the difference between curve and surface along the parameter path between the feet stays within an eighth of the bend, `PatchTree::stretch_within`); a stretch beside it is proven clear by a lower bound on the distance from its chord to the surface (`segment_lower_bound`; the distance from a segment to a box is exact, one convex quadratic per side crossing) less how far the curve can leave the chord. The tree bounds its pieces by boxes in the piece's own frame (as thick as the piece bends, not as it tilts: a tilted piece's axis-aligned box is as thick as the piece is wide, which left a curve at 1.06 tolerances from a face unprovable), takes a threshold above which nothing matters, and the point bound is only asked where it can prove something. The slowest corpus case (a contact of two spline-roofed blocks, 200 s per boolean) takes 0.19 s.

**Rational faces (2026-10-13, late).** A face on a rational Bézier or NURBS surface (weights that are not all one) is an operand like a polynomial one. A rational patch lies in the hull of its control points just the same, so the boxes of the tree, the bound below the distance, the nearest point and the signed distance needed nothing. What was polynomial-only were the three bounds that came from derivative nets, and each now has a proven counterpart in `geom` (`nurbs.rs`; the arguments are in the doc comments):

- *First and second derivatives* (`RationalBezierPatch::derivative_bounds`, `NurbsCurve::derivative_bounds`). With `S = A / w` (weighted points over weight, both in Bernstein form), `S_u = (A_u − S w_u) / w` and `S_uu = (A_uu − 2 w_u S_u − w_uu S) / w`. `A_u − S w_u` is a Bernstein form whose coefficients are `m (w₁ (P₁ − P₀) + (w₁ − w₀) (P₀ − S))` over neighbours, and `S` lies in the hull, so `|P₀ − S|` is at most the reach of `P₀` across the box of the net; `w` is at least the lightest weight; the second derivatives likewise from second differences of `w (P − S)`. For weights that are all one these are the hull bounds of a polynomial patch, to the last bit. They loosen as the weights of a piece differ and tighten as the piece shrinks, so they are taken from small pieces: the leaves of the tree for the bound that `stretch_within` uses, the pieces of a span quartered twice for the marcher's field (which used estimates from samples for a rational surface before), a span halved three times for a curve.
- *The normal* (`RationalBezierPatch::weighted_normal_net`). `w³ (S_u × S_v) = w (A_u × A_v) + w_v (A × A_u) + w_u (A_v × A)` is a polynomial where the normal is not; its Bézier net (degrees `3m − 1` by `3n − 1`, products of Bernstein forms) holds it in its hull, so the normal is at least as long as the distance from the origin to the box of that net over the cube of the heaviest weight. With the bound on the first derivatives that gives the smallest singular value of the Jacobian, and so the parameter speed the chart needs.
- *The bend of a rational edge* (`hits.rs`, `turn_bound`) comes from the curve bound above, so a rational spline is judged a whole stretch at once like any other curve.

Measured on a NURBS cylinder the parameter speed bound is within 20 % of the true value and the second-derivative bound within 50 %; on a bicubic roof with the weights 1, 2, 0.5 and 3 within 36 % and 24 %. The parameter speed is now taken in the surface's own parameters, with the width of the piece: before, it was the piece's own, which is right only for a span one wide, and a B-spline face over a wider knot span got a bound that was too small by that width (found here, fixed for polynomial faces too; `the_parameter_speed_is_in_the_parameters_of_the_surface`).

The oracle (`crates/kernel/tests/boolean_rational.rs`): a rational surface can *be* a piece of a quadric or a torus, so each test solid exists twice, with its curved faces on the analytic surfaces and on the rational patches that equal them (a quarter rod, a quarter ball zone, a quarter frustum, a quarter slice of a ring with an outer and an inner face; patches that end on the trims and patches a fifth larger; circular edges as circles and as rational arcs). The twins pass `check`, measure as the closed forms to 1e-9 and mesh watertight; their three booleans against a block, a cylinder, a ball, a cone and a torus through the rational face agree with the same booleans of the analytic solid within 5.1e-8 in volume and area (360 booleans; held to 1e-6) at the scales 1e-3, 1 and 1e6, in the world's frame and tilted; tangent contact with a plane and a ball and an edge of a block on the face come out as for the analytic twin. A roof that is no quadric (the weights above) is held to a quadrature of its own and to the identities of sets, against a block, a ball, a block over a corner (which cuts the rational roof edges) and a cylinder.

*Refused:* weights more than a factor of a thousand apart within a span. Short of that, weights a hundred apart are refused in the winding numbers ("too close to a face boundary", 2026-10-15, pinned in `boolean.rs` › `what_is_not_supported_says_so`): such a patch moves a hundred times faster with its parameters near its light corners than near its heavy ones, and the chart's one speed for the whole face makes the halving of the loops give up. Tried and taken back: spans with weights more than a thousand apart split into pieces whose weights are within 64 of each other (the tree takes them, the winding numbers refuse the same way), and the chart's speed bounded near each piece of a loop from the tree's pieces within reach (the union of a ball and the plate with weights a hundred apart is then built, but its measure does not settle: `topo::mass_properties`, "a boundary integral that does not settle"). What is owed is a measure and a chart that follow a parametrisation that far from uniform. (A rational face on the surface it equals, a patch or an analytic one, wholly over it or not: built since 2026-10-14 and 2026-10-15, see the table above.) (A patch with a collapsed border was refused here until 2026-10-14: see the next section.) *Cost* (the optimized test profile, four at a time on a quiet machine): a boolean with a rational quadric face takes 35 to 420 ms (medians per pair of solids 45 to 250 ms, a block the slowest tool) where the analytic twin takes 0.4 to 14 ms (closed forms against marched splines and nearest-point searches, as for any freeform face); the weighted roof against a block takes 1.5 s where the polynomial roof takes 0.2 s, because its parameters really move four times as fast and its second derivatives really are twelve times as large, and the chart (one speed for the whole face) and the proofs pay for both. The polynomial roof itself got 15 to 30 % faster by the parameter speed taken in the surface's parameters (0.28 s before).

**Freeform faces with a pole (2026-10-14, `geom/src/pole.rs`, `boolean/freeform.rs`, `chart.rs`).** A border of a patch that is one point is where the parameters stall: the derivative along the border is zero, `∂u × ∂v` has no length, and the parameter rectangle is no chart (a loop through the pole is not closed in it, and nothing bounds how fast the parameter along the border changes nearby). The surface itself is a surface there: smooth when it closes up smoothly (a dome), a cone-like point when it does not (a drop's tip). Every place that read the surface through its parameters is listed here with what it does now; a face without a pole takes the code it took before in each of them.

- *The chart* (`PatchTree::chart`, `chart_speed`). The parameter rectangle is drawn as the surface is. With `s` and `t` the shares of the domain along and across the poles' borders, the point goes to `(ω(t) (s − ½), t)`, with the width `ω = t` for a pole at the start, `1 − t` at the end and `t (1 − t)` for both: a wedge, or a lens between two points. A pole is one ordinary point of it, the map from the surface is one to one and continuous, and it turns the way the parameters do, so the winding number of the loops is what it is on the surface. Its speed is bounded, not sampled: a step `(ds, dt)` moves the chart by the shear `[1 k; 0 1]` of `(ω ds, dt)` with `|k| = |ω′ (s − ½)| ≤ ½` (norm at most 1.2808; 1.29 is used) and the surface by `E ω ds + F dt` with `E = S_s / ω`, `F = S_t`, so the speed is at most 1.29 over the least singular value of `[E F]`, which comes from an upper bound on `|E|² + |F|²` and a lower bound on `|E × F|` as before. `E` and `E × F` are bounded piece by piece of the tree: away from a pole by the piece's nets over the least and the greatest `ω` on it (`ω` is concave); next to a pole, where `S_s` and the normal vanish as `ω` does, by the Bézier nets of `w² S_σ` and `w³ (S_σ × S_τ)` *divided by the piece's own parameter* (`B_j^n(τ) / τ = (n / j) B_{j−1}^{n−1}(τ)`: a net whose border row is zero is the net, one degree lower, of the quotient; `geom`: `RationalBezierPatch::weighted_derivative_nets`, new, and `weighted_normal_net`). A border row counts as zero below 1e-9 of the net's longest vector (a border collapsed up to rounding is collapsed, as the measure and the detection take it). Measured, the bound is 1.4 to 2.5 times the fastest the chart is seen to move (an octant, a lune, a cone, a drop, either way round; `the_chart_of_a_surface_with_poles_moves_no_faster_than_its_speed`, which also steps to within 1e-9 of the poles).
- *The side of the surface a point is on* (`PatchTree::signed_distance`, and the marcher's field for a freeform surface, `field.rs`). The sign is that of `(p − foot) · normal`, and at a pole the formula has no normal. For a point whose nearest point is the pole the normal is taken along the meridian that leaves the pole nearest to the direction of the point (`Pole::normal_towards`): a point a little further on, whose foot is an ordinary point near the pole, has that foot on that meridian and is on the side its normal says, and the sign cannot change on the way, since it would have to pass through zero, which takes a point straight behind that meridian, for which it is the farthest in direction and not the nearest. Where the surface closes up smoothly every meridian gives the same normal. This is an argument by continuity, not a bound; it is held against the distance to a ball (straight above and below the pole of an octant, to 1e-9) and against a cone's two sides behind its apex.
- *The directions around a pole* (`outline::outward_normal`, which `regions` sorts the curves at a vertex by, `sew` and `section`). At a pole it gives the direction the surface closes around (`freeform::pole_axes`): the mean of the unit normals the surface reaches the pole with, which is its normal there when it closes up smoothly and stands in for the axis, as at a cone's apex, when it comes to a point. Seen along it the meridians leave the pole in the order they have on the surface as long as every limit normal is on the axis's side (the direction of a meridian dropped onto the plane across the axis turns at a rate with the sign of axis · normal). That is proven, not sampled: the limit normals are, up to a positive factor, the divided normal net's border row, whose vectors hold them in their hull, and each must make an angle with the axis whose cosine is above 0.05.
- *The marcher* (`geom/src/intersect/field.rs`, `short_of_poles`). The chart of a freeform surface stops a quarter of the tolerance short of a pole, as a cone's does of its apex: the border is moved in by `tolerance / (4 L)` with `L` the proven bound on the speed across it, so everything left out is within a quarter of the tolerance of the pole and a curve that runs into the pole ends within that of it, where the vertex rule joins it to the pole. The bounds the cells are judged by are upper bounds on derivatives and hold at a pole as anywhere. The grid normals of a freeform field are the limit normals at a pole.
- *Unchanged, and why:* the boxes of the tree, the bound below the distance and the nearest point need no regular parameters; `stretch_within` compares along any path in the parameters, so its proof holds (it is only less often conclusive next to a pole).

*Handled:* a pole where the surface closes up smoothly and one where it comes to a cone-like point; one pole or one at each end of the same parameter; cuts away from the pole, around it (the pole inside the tool) and through it (the pole on a face, an edge or the curved side of the tool). *Refused by name:* poles on more than two borders across both parameters (`POLES_BOTH_WAYS`; two adjacent ones are a corner since 2026-10-15, below), a point the limit normals do not all lean one way around, or a cusp (`POLE_SHAPE`, or `IRREGULAR` when the divided nets give no bound). *Held to* (`crates/kernel/tests/boolean_poles.rs`): the octant of a ball as a rational patch against the same booleans of the analytic octant, with a block, a cylinder, a ball, a cone and a torus away from the pole, around it and through it (the pole on a block's face, on a block's edge, on the side of the others), in the world's frame and tilted, at 1e-3, 1 and 1e6: 288 booleans, within 3.7e-7 in volume and area (held to 1e-6), none refused since 2026-10-15 (the torus through the pole, tilted, at 1e6 was refused "imprint lost": see *The helmet's findings* below); the dome and the drop of `kernel::revolve` against the same kinds of tools by the identities of sets (1e-6) and, for planes across the axis and through it (through both poles, through the drop's point), by closed forms from the profile (1e-7). Every result passes `check` and is measured; every result is meshed, except eight kinds of cut through a pole that the tessellator declines ("segments per side") or leaves with a gap, pinned in the test's `UNMESHED` with a reproducer for `crates/tess` (meshed since the same night, §6 *Poles of spline surfaces*; the list is empty). *Cost:* 75 to 280 ms per boolean with the octant's patch (the test profile), as for any rational face.

**Poles at a corner of the parameters (2026-10-15, `boolean/freeform.rs` › `Corner`, `geom/src/pole.rs`, `geom/src/intersect`, `tess/src/face.rs` › `CornerPole`).** A patch whose borders `u = 0` and `v = 0` (or any border across u and one across v) are both one point closes into that point along an L of its parameter rectangle; the two other borders meet at an ordinary corner. A lune of a ball between two meridians is such a patch when it is the inverse stereographic projection from its north pole of the quadrant `X = (1 − s) / (s t)`, `Y = (1 − t) / (s t)`: a rational patch of bidegree (2, 2), raised to (3, 3) so that every weight is positive. Until now it was refused (`POLES_BOTH_WAYS`). With `s` and `t` the shares of the domain from the pole's borders:

- *The chart* (`Corner`). The point goes to `(s t (s − t), s t)` (its first coordinate negated when one of the two shares is measured backwards, so that it turns the way the parameters do): the L is the origin, `(s − t, s t)` is one to one on the square, and near the origin the chart is a wedge `|x| ≤ y` in which the meridians leave the pole in their order along the L. Its speed: with `τ = s t` and `σ = t − s` a step moves the chart by at most `√3 |(dτ, τ dσ)|`, and the surface by `A dτ + B τ dσ` with `A = (t G + s H) / (s + t)` and `B = (H − G) / (s + t)`, where `S_s = t G` and `S_t = s H` (the surface less the pole vanishes on both borders). `G` and `H` are bounded from the nets of `w² S_α` and `w² S_β` divided by the piece's own parameter where the piece reaches a border (`divided`, and `divided_first` for the first parameter), `H − G` from the net of `w² (t S_t − s S_s)` (products of nets with linear factors, `times_linear`) and `G × H` from the normal's net divided by both. At the corner itself `H − G` and `G × H` vanish as `s + t` does; there a field `X` that vanishes at the corner is written `α X_a + β X_b` (`over_sum`), and `X / (a α + b β)` lies in the hull of `X_a / a` and `X_b / b`. The bound is valid wherever the surface leaves the pole in a fan; on the lune it is 29 times the fastest the chart is seen to move, on a cone with its apex at a corner 4 to 10 times (`freeform.rs` › `the_chart_of_a_surface_with_poles_moves_no_faster_than_its_speed`, which now has the lune with its pole at each of the four corners and the cone).
- *The direction the surface closes around* (`corner_axis`): the mean of the limit normals along the whole L, the rows of the doubly divided normal net on both borders and, at the corner, of the two halves `over_sum` gives.
- *The side of the surface at the pole* (`Pole::normal_towards`, `normal_along`): the meridians leave the pole from both borders, and the nearest of all is taken; a meridian near the end of a border that is the other pole's border is looked at as far from that end as from its own border (nearer, both derivatives are rounding). The nearest point next to the pole (`field.rs`, `foot_from`) is looked for again from the meridian of every pole the search ended next to.
- *The surface intersections.* A surface through the pole makes the walked distance vanish as `s t` at the corner, a saddle just outside the chart around which no cell is simple down to the floor ("tangent or nearly coincident over too large an area"): a freeform surface with a corner pole is measured against rather than walked (`meet.rs` › `chart_rank`), where it is a surface like any other. Nodes of the projector's grid on the two borders are one point and are not compared for curvature.
- *The tessellator* (`CornerPole`): a boundary that comes into the pole goes along the L from the meridian of the point before (its smaller share dropped: near the border `t = 0` the line of constant `s` leaves the pole in the direction `R(s, 0)` of `S = P + s t R`) to that of the point after, round the corner when they are on different borders; the pole is also recognised by position (its projection may come back off the L). A stretch into or out of the corner is subdivided along whichever parameter moves (`finish_boundary`).

*Held to* (`crates/kernel/tests/boolean_corner_pole.rs`): a quarter of a ball (x and y positive) whose lune is that patch against the same booleans of the analytic quarter ball, with blocks, cylinders, balls, cones and tori away from the poles, around the north pole (the corner of the parameters), through it (on a block's face, a block's edge, the side of a ball and a cone) and around the south pole (the patch's ordinary corner), upright and tilted, at 1e-3, 1 and 1e6: within 2.7e-7 in volume and area (held to 1e-6, as the octant), the identities of sets to 1e-7, every result valid, meshed wherever the analytic result meshes, and at scale 1 without a crossing of its own mesh (`body_self_intersections`); the pole at each of the other corners of the patch and with the parameters exchanged, cut through the pole. The twin itself measures as the closed forms (`π r³ / 3`, `2π r²`) to 1e-9 and meshes. *Refused:* a patch that closes into a point on two adjacent borders but leaves it along one border all in one direction (`u v R(v)`: no fan, `IRREGULAR` or `POLE_SHAPE`).

**Coincident freeform faces (2026-10-15, `boolean/hits.rs`, `geom/src/intersect/march.rs` › `lies_on`).** Two refusals, two causes. *An edge that lies on a patch for a while and leaves it where the patch ends* (the rational quarter rod against its analytic twin lifted along the axis: the lifted body's straight edge, from 1 to 4, lies on the twin's patch, which ends at 3): the distance along the edge is zero over the stretch on the patch and then grows, and the root isolation took the stretch for a touch somewhere along it, narrowing in on a distance that is zero all along; the cut it made (z = 1.0278 on an edge from 1 to 4) was a vertex where nothing is, and the stretch along the edge no longer fitted the edge's pieces ("an imprinted curve that does not fit the edges it should coincide with"). Now a stretch of the curve within a quarter of the tolerance of a surface that ends (a freeform one), longer than 64 tolerances, is cut where it comes onto the surface and where it leaves it (the last place within a quarter of the tolerance, by bisection towards the clear stretch beside it), as touches (a ray that runs along a face is still no ray to count by). *A patch on a patch that ends sooner* (the quarter rod against itself lifted): one surface lies on another when 81 samples of it are within the tolerance of the other; samples past the end of a freeform one were among them, so a patch was never found on a patch it overhangs, and the marcher refused the tangency along the whole face ("surfaces that touch along a curve … without a closed form"). Samples whose nearest point of a freeform surface is on its border are now left out, and at least 16 of the 81 must count. *Held to* (`boolean_rational.rs` › `a_rational_face_on_the_surface_it_equals_is_right`): the quarter rod as a rational patch against its analytic twin lifted, against itself lifted, against a patch a fifth larger of itself lifted and the larger patch against itself lifted, upright and tilted, at 1e-3, 1 and 1e6, against the same booleans of the analytic rods to 1e-6, every result valid and meshed.

**Freeform faces that close on themselves (2026-10-14, `boolean/chart.rs`, `geom/src/closure.rs`, `geom/src/intersect`).** A Bézier or NURBS surface whose control net ends on the row it starts with, on clamped knots (`geom::closure::closes`, the test the measure and the tessellator take: a vase wall, a NURBS cylinder, ball or torus as a STEP file brings them, with or without a seam edge), was refused by the boolean wherever a cut crossed its seam ("a piece of a face is not closed off by its boundary"): its chart was the parameter rectangle, in which a loop over the seam is not closed, and the surface intersections walked it as a rectangle too, ending curves at the seam and, past it, on the polynomial the end span continues in (a curve that came back on itself). Two places read such a surface through its parameters, and both now know it closes:

- *The chart* (`chart.rs`, `Layout`). With `s` the share of the way around and `t` of the way across, the way around is an angle `θ = 2π s`: closed one way, the annulus `(1 + t) e^{iθ}`; with a pole at one end, the disc `t e^{iθ}` (or `(1 − t)`), the pole its centre; with a pole at each end (a ball), `z = t e^{iθ} / (1 − t)` puts one pole at the origin and the other at infinity and `w = 1 / (z − z₀)` sends a point `z₀` clear of the loops to infinity instead (the poles and twelve places between them are tried, the one furthest from the loops taken; refused within 1e-3 of the surface's size), so both poles are ordinary points and the face may contain the point left out, as on a sphere; closed both ways (a torus), cut open along a line of constant `t` (or `s`) that the loops do not cross, found as for the analytic torus by following each boundary piece through the nearest point, and `(1 + (t − cut mod 1)) e^{iθ}`. The direction between two images is never computed through a far point: for the ball, `(a_q b_p − a_p b_q) conj(A_p) conj(A_q)` with `a = t e^{iθ}`, `b = 1 − t`, `A = a b₀ − a₀ b`, bounded and continuous through the pole at infinity of `z`. The speed is bounded from the tree's bound on the Jacobian: in the shares, `4π` times it for the annulus and the torus (`r ≤ 2`), `√(1 + 4π²)` and `√(4 + 4π²)` times the wedge's bound (`wedge_sigma`, `ω` the width that vanishes at a pole) for the disc and the ball, where the derivatives of the direction along `s` vanish as `ω` does. Measured, the bounds are 4 to 13 times the fastest the chart is seen to move, across the seam too (`chart.rs` › `the_chart_of_a_closed_surface_moves_no_faster_than_its_speed`). Refused by name: a surface that closes into a point along the same parameter it closes in, or closes both ways and into a point (`CLOSED_POLE`); a torus-like face whose boundary crosses every line both ways (`NO_SEAM_CUT`); a ball-like face whose boundary comes near every point tried (`NO_DROP`).
- *The surface intersections* (`intersect/field.rs`, `march.rs`). Such a surface is a periodic chart, as a cylinder is: the quadtree wraps its cells, and a branch is marched over the seam with its parameters taken around into the domain where the surface is evaluated (`Chart::at`, and the start of the projector's search). A curve around the surface closes, and one across the seam is one curve. A border across the way around that lies on the other surface (a rim on a plate) is reported as a closed curve; one that is a pole gives none.

Besides: a face without loops on a surface closed both ways, or one way with a pole at each end, is the whole surface, as on a sphere or a torus (merge may join pieces into one, `freeform::borderless`); the fixed points looked at inside such a face are taken in the shares of the domain. *Held to* (`crates/kernel/tests/boolean_closed_twins.rs`): a NURBS can (with and without a seam edge, the seam in two places, the turn in u or v), ball, cup and torus, each one face on a rational surface that closes, against the same solid on the analytic cylinder, sphere and torus, with blocks, cylinders, balls, cones and tori that cross the seam, cut through both poles, around the axis and across the tube; the three booleans at 1e-3, 1 and 1e6, in the world's frame and tilted: 594 booleans, all built and valid, within 7.9e-8 of the analytic twin in volume and area (held to 1e-6), the identities of sets to 2.4e-8 (held to 1e-7), and `(A − B) ∪ (A ∩ B)` built and measured as A. Six results through both poles of a ball cut along its seam are not measured by `topo` (named there) and are held to their twins by 4000 sampled points each; 77 results are declined by the tessellator (listed by the test). The vase of `boolean_closed_freeform.rs` (a Bézier profile turned, the seam inside the half, on the cut and outside it) is cut in half at three scales, within 9.1e-9 of half its volume. *Cost:* 0.13 to 0.4 s per boolean (the test profile), as for other rational faces. *Not done:* knots that are not clamped (a closure the net does not show).

**The helmet's findings (2026-10-15, `geom/src/intersect/field.rs`, `march.rs`, `geom/src/pole.rs`, `boolean/section.rs`).** A shell turned from two splines and cut by lofted frustums (the Voronoi helmet; `crates/kernel/tests/boolean_helmet.rs`) found six mistakes, all where two freeform faces meet. Five are in how a freeform surface on the *field* side of the marcher (the surface measured against, not the one walked) was read: a lofted side whose corners are rounded is a bilinear patch, not a plane, so the shell is the field (with a planar tool the shell is walked and none of these happen). The sixth is in the bound below the distance to a freeform face:

- *Curvature at a pole.* The projector estimates the curvature of a freeform surface from how fast the normal turns between neighbouring nodes of its 25 × 25 grid. Nodes on one pole are one point, apart by rounding (1e-14 at a size of 100), each with the limit normal of its own meridian; their difference over that distance read as a curvature of 1e7 per unit length, every cell within two grid steps of the pole was too bent to be simple, and the boolean refused "tangent or nearly coincident over too large an area". Nodes on one pole are no longer compared.
- *The nearest point next to a pole.* Newton's step for the nearest point cannot turn from one meridian to another at a pole (the derivative along its border is zero), and next to it the step along the border is ill-conditioned: from the pole node, or by a step that clamps onto the border, it kept to the meridian it started on and stopped at the pole or on that meridian, for a point a millimetre off the pole a millimetre from the surface. A search that ends within one grid row of a pole is done again from the meridian that leaves the pole towards the point (`Pole::meridian_towards`), first along that meridian, then in both parameters, and the nearer foot is kept.
- *Pieces between the field's borders.* Past a border of a freeform surface the field goes on (the distance to the nearest point, on the border), and the quadtree sees only that: a piece of curve between two borders of the field (a frustum's side cutting a corner off a half of the shell, between the half's meridian and the vent's rim) lay in simple cells whose every edge seed was past the field's edge, and was lost ("imprint lost"). The marcher now also seeds where a border of a freeform field that is an edge (not a seam, not a pole) crosses the walked surface: along each such border, bisection on its parameter wherever the distance to the walked surface could be zero (it changes no faster than the border moves; the finest piece is sixteen tolerances, and at most 4096 pieces per border), and a root that is on the walked surface gives a seed.
- *A pole border is no curve.* A border of the walked patch that lies on the other surface is reported as a curve; a border that is one point (the pole of an octant on an open patch) was reported as a curve from the pole to itself, a few tolerances long in the chart's parameters, which on the other face hid the arc that divides it. It was already left out where the border runs around a closed surface; now everywhere.
- *A loop through a cut place.* An end of a curve next to a place where an edge meets a surface is that place, and the place is found on the curve once. A curve that begins and ends there (the figure eight in which a torus meets the plane it touches at its inner equator, through the octant's pole) had the place at its start only, and the lobe back into the face was dropped. An end whose place was found at the other end of the curve is now that place itself.

- *A bound below the distance that is one* (`boolean/freeform.rs`, `PatchTree::bound_by` and `refine`). Asked up to a threshold (does the distance exceed it?), the bound stopped as soon as the nearest piece looked at so far was under the threshold and returned that piece's distance; a nearer piece not yet looked at could undercut it, so between the threshold (1.001 times what is asked) and what is asked the value was no bound. A ray through a lofted frustum was proven clear of the side it leaves by (its distance there 0, the value returned 1.0006 tolerances), a face of another frustum, apart from it, was taken for inside and left out, and the union of the two had edges used once (the check caught it: "produced an invalid body"; the helmet's frustums from 60 mm, united). The pieces left (the heap's nearest, the boxes of those still to be split) now count. The union of radial frustums that come within a few millimetres of each other towards the centre is no tolerance case: the same mistake.

With the fourth and fifth, the octant against a torus through its pole, tilted, at 1e6 (pinned as refused in `boolean_poles.rs`) is built and agrees with the analytic octant. *Held to:* four cells of the helmet against the shell with a pole and with a vent, the three booleans at 1e-3, 1 and 1e6, valid, the identities of sets to 1e-7, and the common part against the same boolean with the cell's planar twin (its far corners the near ones scaled exactly, so that its sides are planes and the shell is walked instead) to 2e-5 (the twin's sides are 1e-5 mm off the cell's); two frustums apart, whose union is both (to 1e-12), whose intersection is empty and whose difference is the first; through the CLI, each of 70 cells cut from the shell with a pole, 71 from 60 mm and 71 from 100 mm from the shell with a vent, all built (before: 8, 1 and 1 refused), and the helmet's 71 frustums from 60 mm united and taken from the shell, valid (before: an invalid body). Unit tests in `field.rs` for the curvature and the nearest point next to a pole.

**Not done:** freeform faces whose parametrisation stalls anywhere but at a border that is one point; a cusp; a lens between a freeform surface and another; speed (a boolean of a domed block and a block takes about 0.1 s, most of it nearest-point searches); a spatial index over the faces of a body (every face pair is still tested by box and by side); an untouched operand does not come back with its entities in their old order.

### 5.4 Sketch solver (`solve`)

A 2D geometric constraint solver: entities become unknowns, constraints become equations, solved numerically (Newton-type with a rank-revealing step) from the stored previous solution. It reports degrees of freedom, which constraints are redundant or conflicting, and never loops forever (iteration cap → `DidNotConverge`). Dragging (later, with the UI) is the same solve with a soft target. Decomposition into small sub-problems is an optimisation for later; correctness and diagnostics come first.

As built (M5): the unknowns are point coordinates and circle radii; gradients are exact (forward-mode dual numbers); the Jacobian is factorised row by row in the order the constraints were added, so a row that depends on earlier ones marks its constraint as redundant (it holds) or conflicting (it does not). When the whole system does not converge, constraints are added one at a time from the solution of the earlier ones, and one that cannot be added is blamed. That diagnosis is this solver's verdict, not a proof. About 8% of dense random sketches (up to 40 arbitrary constraints) end as "did not converge" instead of a diagnosis (measured at M5). A constraint that has no value where the others put the sketch (an angle on a line that shrank to a point) is conflicting too, with the reason. `solve` repeats its pass from its own result until nothing changes, so `Ok` means that solving again gives the same coordinates, bit for bit, and the same verdict. `regen::settle_sketches` still does not store a solution with a conflict in it: a collapsed line no longer says which way it pointed, and the stored shape is what brings the sketch back on the same side. Steps are minimum-norm with a line search, so free geometry moves as little as possible and mirror choices (which side of a line, inside or outside a circle) follow the starting point. An angle is signed: counter-clockwise from the first line's direction to the second's.

### 5.5 Regeneration (`regen`)

`regen` walks `Document::features` in order and produces, per feature, a status (`Ok`, `Suppressed`, `Failed { reason }`, `Skipped` because an input failed) and the bodies that exist after it.

- **Incremental.** Each feature's result is cached under a key made of its own parameters (after expression evaluation) and the keys of its inputs. Editing feature *k* reuses 0..k-1 untouched and recomputes only the features that depend on what changed. *As built (M6, `regen/src/cache.rs`):* the key is the feature's definition, the values of its expressions, the document's units and tolerance, and for every feature it is made from (directly or through others) the stamp of that feature's result and who had taken its body. A rebuilt result that equals the old one keeps its stamp, so a change that makes no difference stops there. Sketch solves are cached on their own (a sketch solves the same wherever it lies), which also spares the solve that stores a sketch's solution after an edit. The cache lives with the open document; `regenerate_cached` returns exactly what `regenerate` returns, and the test suites hold it to that after every command. Measured (2026-10-13): one edit of one part of forty took 64 ms before the cache and 2 ms after.
- **Long rows (2026-10-15).** The context a feature's result is compared under lists every feature it is made from, directly or through others: in a row of cuts each made from the body the one before left, most of the history. Put together for every feature of every regeneration, that is the square of the length of the history per command (a thousand holes cut one command at a time: 593 s, of which building was 18 s). So the full context is put together only for a feature that may have a new one. The cache keeps, besides each feature's entry, the *trail* of the last regeneration: per feature in history order its `Step` (name and kind, the stamp of its result, the bodies it took). As regeneration goes, a feature that stands at the same place as the last time and whose step differs is marked, with the bodies it takes or took, and so is everything made from a marked feature, directly or through others (`Changed`: reverse edges of `FeatureKind::inputs`, plus every feature after it that is made from all earlier ones; each feature is marked at most once, so the marks of one regeneration cost the length of the history). A feature at the same place, made only from features before it, none of them marked, has the inputs it had: only its definition, the values of its expressions, units, tolerance, rollback and followed names are compared. Anything else (a feature moved, inserted or deleted before it, a reference to a later or missing feature, an id twice in the history) takes the full comparison as before. Either way what is reused is exactly what the full comparison reuses, and the rebuilt counts are the same. The names a fillet or a chamfer gives are followed to the newest body (`follow::feature`) before its context is known, for every feature of every regeneration; on the engine block that was most of the time of a command that only added a circle to a sketch. While no feature has been marked yet, a feature at the same place made only from earlier features follows its names as it did the last time (they depend on the results before it and the names and kinds of earlier features, all as they were), so the followed feature is taken from its entry. `RegenCache::last_run_work` (`Session::last_regeneration_work`) counts the entries of what features are made from that a regeneration looked at; `crates/engine/tests/long_chains.rs` holds adding a hole, setting a variable used at the end of the row and undoing to at most four per feature, the same per feature at 30 and 90 holes (about 2; the full comparison looked at 1425 for 91 features at 30 holes, growing with the square). `crates/engine/examples/regen_rows.rs` times rows of holes and steps files command by command. Measured (release, 2026-10-15, `craft3d-cli run` of the steps file, on a machine other agents loaded): 1000 holes cut one command at a time 593 s before, 29 s after; the engine block (330 steps) 13 to 14 s before, 2.4 s after; the turbofan (248 steps) unchanged at 16 to 23 s, nearly all of it its patterns (the fan's twenty blades 11.7 s) in the kernel; setting the variable of the turbofan's last pattern rebuilds one feature of 156 in 2 ms. `document.inspect` of the session and `craft3d-cli info` of the saved file are the same before and after for the four models measured, and the boolean corpus and the random runs give the same digests.
- **Variables first.** The variable table is evaluated in its stored order; a cycle or a dimension error fails only the features that use the affected variables.
- **Partial success is normal.** A failed feature does not discard the model: bodies up to the failure stay available and renderable, and the failure is data an agent can read (`document.inspect`). *As built (2026-10-14):* there is no `Skipped` status; a feature whose input failed is `Failed` with a reason that names the input, and `regen::root_cause` gives the feature to fix (§4, *A feature that fails*).
- **Background jobs.** Regeneration runs on the job pool from a snapshot and commits its result if the document has not changed in a conflicting way. *As built (2026-10-14):* no job pool; the desktop app runs whole commands, regeneration and meshing included, on one engine thread that owns the session while it works (§8.4), and regeneration reports each feature it rebuilds (`regen::progress::report`; the engine adds `measuring` and `meshing`) to a sink installed on that thread, which the window shows. Nothing reported changes what is built. Not cancellable yet.
- **Never through itself** (*as built 2026-10-15*, `regen/src/selfcheck.rs`). No feature hands on a body that passes through itself, whose shells overlap or lie inside each other the wrong way, or that touches itself; each operation either cannot make one, by the reasoning below, or is checked with the parameters of `document.check {"deep": true}` (`tess::body_self_intersections`, §5.2), and a finding fails the feature with two faces by their names. A body that cannot be meshed to look is refused too, with the tessellator's reason (`Crossing::Unknown`), as it was for lofts and sweeps.

  | Operation | Checked how | Why that is enough |
  |---|---|---|
  | block, cylinder, sphere, cone, torus | not | closed forms of valid sizes (a torus's tube may not reach its axis) |
  | extrude by a distance or through all, revolve | not | profiles do not touch or cross (`detect_profiles`) and nest even-odd; prisms along one direction, and solids of revolution of regions on one side of the axis turned by at most a full turn, do not either |
  | extrude up to a face | near the end caps | it is the prism cut by a tool with the target's surface: prism faces one group, end caps the other |
  | transform, mirror, a pattern whose instances are apart | not | an isometry of a body that does not; instances whose boxes (`Body::bbox`, conservative) are apart by more than the tolerance neither cross nor nest, and for a linear or circular pattern instance *i* and *j* are instance 0 and *j − i* moved |
  | boolean (also the one of extrude or revolve with `cut`, `join`, `intersect`), a pattern whose instances meet | near where the operands meet | every face is a piece of a face of one operand, which was checked or proven when it was made, or merged from several: pieces of one operand are one group, merged faces new |
  | chamfer, fillet, their chains and meets along freeform edges | near the new faces | made by booleans with tools: kept faces are pieces of the body's (one group); blend and corner faces new, and so is a kept face next to one or merged (it may have taken a tool's face in its plane); a blend face without a closed form (`rolling`) is new like any other, so a radius too large for what lies across the body is found |
  | thread (modeled) | near the groove | the groove is a helical sweep no one checked on its own: its faces are new |
  | loft, sweep, sweep along a helix | the whole body | new everywhere |
  | offset face, shell, delete face | the whole body | as before (2026-10-14); not yet made local |
  | move face, draft | near the faces that changed | every entity keeps its number (`kernel::tweak`): a face whose surface or one of whose vertices moved is new, every other face is the source's own, unchanged, one group (`face_ops::not_through_itself_where_changed`; 2026-10-15) |
  | thicken | the whole body | the layer is two offsets and a boolean, as a shell; not yet made local (2026-10-15) |
  | sketch, datum | — | no body |

  *Near what changed* is `tess::body_self_intersections_near`, which takes a role per face, `Known(group)` or `New`. A pair of faces of one known group is never looked at; the zone where pairs can meet is the box of every new face and the common part of the boxes of every two groups (per face, `Body::face_bbox`, grown by the first chordal tolerance and the tolerance); only faces whose box meets the zone are meshed, a shell whose box meets it is meshed whole when the body has several (so that where it lies can be told), the holes of flat faces that are far from the zone are filled before meshing (the face is the same face near everything looked at; a plate with a thousand holes is meshed with the few near the new one), and only triangles within ten chordal tolerances of the zone are searched. With every face new it is the whole check, finding for finding (`crates/testkit/tests/self_intersection.rs`). Measured (2026-10-15, release, a plate 4010 mm long with 400 holes): a new hole's boolean 10 ms, its check 1 to 3 ms (the whole check of that body 190 to 240 ms); the first cut of all 400 holes at once is checked over the whole plate: 140 ms on a boolean of 200 ms while the plate's long straight edges were one chord each and its top face's triangles fans across the plate, which the search cannot prune; 27 to 31 ms since the tessellator cuts such chords (2026-10-15, §6 *Long straight chords of flat faces*).

  *Touching itself* is a finding of its own (`tess::body_touches_itself`, on the B-rep and not the mesh, a sort along x): a vertex of a shell within twice the tolerance of another vertex of that shell, or of an edge of it that it is not an end of. Such a shell meets itself in a point or along a line (two walls whose outlines end in one point, as a hollowed hull whose inner and outer plans ended in one bow point did); `topo::check` passes it, no closed manifold mesh can be made of it, and its faces there do not say which side is inside. Two shells that touch each other (blocks that share an edge, which the random boolean runs make by the hundred) are each closed and are not this.

  *What it costs on a body of freeform faces (2026-10-16).* The impeller of the showcase (40 features, one body of 99 faces, 62 of them lofted, filleted or chamfered freeform faces) took about 90 s to build, and the check near what changed was suspected. Sampled (`eu-stack`, every busy thread) and built at 9a4970f, before the check, at db1d44b, which brought it, and after: the check is 1 to 2 % of the samples of every slow step but the two unions of blades and splitters with the hub, where its meshing is about 15 % (the two groups' boxes overlap nearly everywhere there, so most faces are meshed), and the build before the check was as slow (95 to 119 s of CPU against 96 to 141 s; the machine was loaded by other agents throughout, and the same build varies by half). The time was the booleans' own: every boolean on the finished body built the `PatchTree` of each of its freeform faces again, the shelf of trees being too small for them (§5.3, *Speed*), and `body.material` measured the body's mass and inertia exactly (§4.2). Both fixed; the run is 62 to 77 s of CPU and 32 to 40 s on the clock where it was 96 to 141 s and 53 to 100 s, the rest the unions and the patterns themselves (intersection curves of freeform faces, their classification) and `document.measure`.

---

## 6. Tessellator (`tess`)

`tessellate(&Body, &TessParams, cancel) -> Result<Mesh, TessError>` with `TessParams { chordal_tolerance, angular_tolerance, max_edge_length }`. *As built:* `tessellate_body(&Body, &TessParams)` with `TessParams { chordal_tolerance, max_segments, max_triangles, max_angle }` (no cancel token yet), `tessellate_body_and_edges`, `tessellate_edges` and `tessellate_within_budget`.

- **Edges first, then faces.** Each edge is discretised once; both faces sharing it use the same points, so the mesh of a solid is **watertight by construction**: no cracks, no T-junctions.
- **An edge is as fine as its faces need** (as built 2026-10-13). The points of an edge are first chosen for its curve alone, within the chordal tolerance. A face may need more: where another of its edges comes nearer than the tolerance, the chords of a curved edge cross it and no longer bound the face (a hole close to a rim, a notch that stops just short of an arc); and close to a pole of a sphere a chord is far from the straight segment the triangulation takes it for in parameter space. And a face may be narrower than the tolerance altogether: between a line and a flat arc from the same vertex to the same vertex the arc is one chord and the loop a line there and back, which encloses nothing; such a face asks for every chord of its curved edges. Before it is triangulated, each face names the chords it cannot do with; they are cut in two on the curve, for every face that uses the edge, and the faces concerned are done again. At most 12 rounds, an edge growing to at most four times its points and 32 more; each round is the same for any number of threads. What is left after that is the error it always was (edges that really cross). `tessellate_edges` goes through the same rounds, so its polylines are the borders of the mesh; `tessellate_body_and_edges` gives both for the work of one.
- **A chord the triangles cannot follow** (2026-10-14). A chord within the tolerance of its edge can still be useless to the face: where the surface is curved across the edge far more tightly than the tolerance (the nose of a slender aerofoil, a few millimetres round, along a loft whose tip of 0.55 m follows a section of 1.1 m directly), the chord sags off the surface by more than that radius and lies beside the face, over the next one. A triangle standing on it then faces sideways whatever its third corner, and the refinement, cutting the longest free side each time it fails to face the way the surface does, moved that corner towards the chord until there was no room left between them; the face was refused with a misleading "needs about 2050 segments per side" (`CdtError::TooMany`), at any tolerance the self-intersection check tried, so a loft whose ruled twin built could not be checked and failed. Now, when the refinement of a face can go no further (no room for a point, or 200 rounds), the chords that its stuck triangles stand on, or lie flat against (all three corners within a millionth of their longest side of the chord: cut after cut, the triangle can end one row away from it), are asked for like the chords of the rounds above (`mesh_face`'s `ask`, in `face.rs`), cut in two on the curve, and the faces using the edge are done again; a chord cut in two lies four times nearer to the surface. Failing that (the last round, or no such chord) the error is a `TessError::Face` that names the edge, which the self-intersection check answers with a mesh four times finer, as for any face it cannot mesh. *Within a chord's bulge* (2026-10-14): a chord can also lie beside the surface along it, by its bulge (`chord_bulge`: a chord of the circle where a fillet of a few tenths of a millimetre meets a plane bulges out of the fillet by its sag, in the plane), and a point of the face nearer to it than that lies beyond it in space; every triangle between the two faces backwards, and the refinement, cutting the side nearest the chord each time, walked towards it until there was no room for a point, a hundred-millionth of a millimetre from it (the turbofan's stripes and flange roots, with the angle criterion of the display at every tolerance). Such a chord is asked for too: the nearest one whose bulge, times `NEAR_BULGE`, a stuck triangle lies within; and when the refinement stops at a triangle without room, the other triangles still failing are looked at as well, so that all such chords are cut in one round. *When no chord is to blame*, the error says what happened instead of the old `TooMany` ("needs about 2050 segments per side", a count never made): a triangle smaller than the tolerance that still fails is a `TessError::Face` ("its refinement cannot go on" or "did not settle: a triangle 1e-8 across, far within the tolerance, is still off the surface or faces against it"), which no coarser tolerance cures; one as large as the tolerance means a tolerance finer than the coordinates resolve, `TooFine` without a count (`needed_segments` infinite, "asks for a tolerance finer than its coordinates can resolve"), which the display rule and `tessellate_within_budget` answer with a coarser tolerance (four times). Covered by `crates/engine/tests/display_quality.rs` (the stripe with fillets of 0.25 and 0.5 mm at ten tolerances from 0.01 to 1 mm: closed, the same on one thread and on eight; and, in release, every body of the showcase and of the models in `CRAFT3D_DISPLAY_MODELS`, the turbofan and the engine block, at the display scene's tolerance and at the first picture's with the angle, and at the automatic tolerance and four times finer without it, every solid closed, strictly since 2026-10-15: the showcase hull did not close until its bow got a wall, since its inner and outer walls touched along a line there, two vertices at one point and four triangles on one segment once welded). Only faces that failed before are affected: every mesh that was made is made bit for bit as it was. `tessellate_edges`, which does not triangulate, does not see these chords; `tessellate_body_and_edges` does. Held to by `crates/engine/tests/loft.rs` (`slender_twisted_blade_sections_loft_smoothly`, from an aerofoil and from a circle; `a_smooth_blade_whose_sides_cross_is_refused`: the same tip from a circle of 1 m, which overshoots so that the upper and lower sides cross, is still refused by the two faces that cross, where it was "cannot be meshed" before).
- **No size of its own** (2026-10-13). Nothing in the tessellator is a length: every threshold is a ratio (of the chordal tolerance, of an edge's parameter range, of a face's own extent). A body and its tolerance made *k* times as large give the same mesh *k* times as large: bit for bit when *k* is a power of two, where scaling does not round (`crates/tess/tests/scale.rs`, and for boolean results `crates/testkit/tests/self_intersection.rs`). For other *k* the edges get the same points to rounding; the triangles between them may differ where two triangulations are equally good (four points of a grid on a cylinder lie on one circle, and the last bit chooses the diagonal), so those meshes are the same surface, not the same triangles. A knot of a spline edge is a point of the edge only when it is inside the edge's range by more than 1e-9 of that range: an edge cut from a spline at a knot may start a rounding error before it.
- **Closed, and checked** (2026-10-14). That the mesh of a closed solid is closed is not left to the construction alone: it is checked on every face before its mesh is returned (`body.rs`, `closes_along_edges`). The sides of the face's triangles that lie on the boundary of its region are compared with what the face's loops say must be there: every chord of every edge, once for each use of the edge by a loop and in that direction, point for point and bit for bit; what the mesh has beyond its edges (the two sides of a seam the tessellator cut itself) must be there once each way. A face that fails is a `TessError::Face` that names the edge, the chord and the face on the other side. Faces that pass and share an edge use its chords in opposite directions, so the mesh of a body whose every edge is used once each way is closed without being welded: the sides inside a face are shared by two triangles by the making of the triangulation. The cost is that of sorting the boundary of each face, not its triangles: on the timing fixtures (`crates/tess/tests/timing.rs`, up to 396,270 triangles, best of three rounds on a machine busy with other work) it is not to be told from the noise between runs, where welding the mesh to check the same thing (`Mesh::weld` and `Welded::is_closed`, the table's last column) takes as long as making it: 190 ms for a sphere of 246,512 triangles that takes 150 to 180 ms to tessellate. A sheet is checked the same way: its border is the edges that are used once.
- **A manifold, where the body is thinner than the tolerance** (2026-10-14). The check above finds a mesh that is open; a mesh can also be closed and still no manifold. A rung is a side of a triangle that joins two points of a face's boundary across the face. Two faces that share an edge can both have the rung between the same two points of it: across a neck of a face between two stretches of one edge, with the neighbouring face just behind it, both take the same chord for their surface, and four triangles meet in it (a ball's face and a torus's around the curve they meet in; found on boolean results cut around a pole). `Welded::is_closed` and every program that reads the mesh call that open. Every face reports its rungs; where two faces have the same one, the curved face of the two (of two curved ones the first in face order) gets a point of its own surface in the middle of it and is done again, which takes its mesh off the chord by less than the tolerance. Three rounds, each the same for any number of threads; a rung still shared then is an error at the face. Two plane faces with a side in common overlap in one plane, which is the body's to answer for, and are left. The same can happen on the edges themselves: an arc flatter than the tolerance is one chord, and where a line joins the same two points (a lens-shaped profile 0.05 thick swept at a chord of 0.25, at the joint between two stretches of the sweep, where no face lies between the two edges to ask for more) four faces meet in that chord. A chord of a curved edge that is the chord of another edge as well is therefore cut in two, like the chords a face asks for (`body.rs`, `coincident_chords`).
- **Poles of spline surfaces** (2026-10-14). A face may run into a row of control points that is one point, with an edge that ends there or passes through. Two things are different from a sphere's pole, whose parameters are in closed form. The parameter around the pole of a boundary point *near* the pole is found by the search that projects it, to rounding over how slowly the surface moves with it there, which a few millionths of the surface's size from the pole is no digits at all (and the curves in which a boolean cuts such a face have points that near); a boundary that zigzags in that parameter bounds triangles that stand inside out. So a boundary point where the surface moves a thousand times slower with a parameter than it does elsewhere along the loop is projected again by descent from its neighbour along the loop, on the side away from the pole (`Surface::project_from`), which moves the parameter only as far as the point asks. And between such points lie triangles whose area is within the rounding of their own corners: they face no way that can be told, were cut in two for facing the wrong way until the triangulation gave up (the error read "needs about 2050 segments"), and are now left alone on spline surfaces. Held to by `crates/kernel/tests/boolean_poles.rs`: an octant's patch and a dome cut through and around their poles, eight results that were refused or meshed wrongly before.
- **Closed surfaces** (as built 2026-10-13). A face is to the left of its loops. On a sphere, loops that all run clockwise bound everything outside them: the face is the whole sphere with those loops as holes (a ball with a hole drilled across the sphere's axis), cut open along a meridian that no hole lies on. The same on a torus is not handled yet. A whole closed surface may also be given with its seam as an edge from pole to pole that the one loop walks there and back, as a STEP file has a sphere (2026-10-14): a loop that comes back from a pole the way it went there turns all the way around the pole, and the face is everything about it.
- **The seam of a face that goes around its surface** (as built 2026-10-13). Such a face (a wall between two circles, a zone of a sphere, a cap up to a pole, a band of a torus around its axis or around its tube) is cut open in parameter space along a seam from its lower winding loop, or the lower end of the surface, to the upper one; the two sides of the cut are the same curve in space and get the same points. A straight seam is tried first, at sixteen places, and is what an ordinary face gets. When the other loops are in the way of every one of them (seven blade roots that together cover the circumference of a turbine hub; two rows of holes, the second row half a pitch further), the seam is made of straight pieces from loop to loop: it enters a hole at one of its vertices and goes on from another, and the two stretches of the hole between those vertices become part of the two sides of the cut, one a period further than the other.
