The pieces are found by triangulating the loops, laid out three times side by side, with our own constrained Delaunay triangulation: no edge of it crosses a loop, so every edge between two different loops is a piece a seam can use, and the edges join the lower side to the upper because the face is connected. The seam is the chain with the fewest pieces (breadth first, short edges first); every piece is tested once more against all loops and against the pieces before it and their copies a period away. It costs nothing where a straight seam exists. Covered: cylinder, cone (also from its apex), sphere (zone, cap to a pole, whole sphere with holes), torus bands both ways, as the surface has them and reversed (`crates/tess/tests/winding_seam.rs`, closed-form areas at three sizes; `crates/testkit/tests/winding_seam_solids.rs`, a pipe drilled by the boolean kernel). *A seam along a line of the loop* (2026-10-15): a straight seam that runs along a straight edge of the loop it starts on (a bore with a stepped bottom: half a circle at one depth, a line along the wall, half a circle deeper and a line back at `a = ±π`, where the surface's parameter wraps) passes through the vertex where that edge ends; computed a period on, it can lean a last bit away from it, so that the exact test saw neither a crossing nor a touch, the seam was taken and swallowed the edge, and the face's mesh had no side on its chord: the engine block's bored mounts were refused as "not closed along edge 1068" at every tolerance finer than about 1 mm. A seam that passes within rounding of a loop's vertex (rounding per parameter, so the same at any scale) is refused like one that crosses it (`seam_grazes`; `crates/tess/tests/stepped_bore.rs`, the face as the engine block has it). A face for which no seam is found asks for finer chords first (chords too coarse can close the way between two loops) and is `Unsupported` after that.
- **A face that winds around its axis along a thread, and the triangulation's work budget** (2026-10-14). The wall of a shank with a cut thread is one face whose lower loop winds once around the axis on its way up one edge of the groove and back down the other, sixteen turns for an M10 thread 24 mm long. Three things made such a face sit for over thirteen minutes at the display tolerance, fixed together. *(a) The straight seam was refused by rounding:* its end on the upper loop (which runs clockwise) is computed as "a period back, then so many periods on", which can be a bit away from that loop's own vertex moved by whole periods at once, and the loop's segments that end there then crossed the seam they only touch. A segment's end within 10⁻¹³ (relative, and of the period) of an end of the seam is now taken for that end (`snapped`). The end itself is left as it was computed: made exact, it let a seam through that runs along the side of a window on the surface's own seam within 10⁻¹⁷, which the rounding had refused as collinear (`crates/tess/tests/periodic.rs`). *(b) A seam from the far side:* when neither the sixteen vertices spread along the loop nor the search in pieces (whose layout is a turn either way, too narrow for a loop sixteen turns long) find a seam, it is tried straight up from the highest vertices of the lower loop (down from the lowest of an upper one), where nothing of the loop lies across the way. *(c) Point location that does not cost the square of the points:* a walk from the last point that runs out of steps (on a long, thin region in parameter space, which a thread's land is, a walk crosses a number of triangles that grows with all of them) starts again from a vertex near its point, found in a grid of about one cell per point made the first time it is needed and made again when the points double; the triangle it finds is put through the same choice as the scan of all triangles it replaces (of a point on an edge, the triangle made first), so the triangulation is the same bit for bit. And every triangulation (the face's and the one the search for a seam in pieces lays out) has a work budget: walk steps, triangles looked at, flips and crossed edges together may not exceed 65,536 plus, per point so far, 4,096 plus 64 times the square root of the number of points. Measured on the showcase and on threads down to 0.0003 mm, the most any triangulation used is about 1,200 per point (7 times the root); beyond the budget the face fails with *its triangulation did not converge*, in time of the order of n^1.5 instead of n². That error is not one the display rule retries at coarser tolerances: it retries only a triangle budget exceeded or a patch too fine for the tessellator, each of which ends as soon as it is certain (`crates/engine/src/scene.rs`, a test there). Covered: `crates/tess/tests/thread_land.rs` (a land forty turns long on a cylinder, closed-form area: 141 s and then refused before, 2 s now), `crates/engine/tests/thread_mesh.rs` (the M10 thread at 0.0015 mm and at the display rule), and two tests in `crates/tess/src/cdt.rs`. Still slow, though proportional: a thread's B-spline flanks cost about 20 µs per triangle to refine (the M10 screw with its head: 200,000 triangles at 0.0015 mm in about 3 s, a million at 0.0003 mm in about 20 s).
- **Spline surfaces that close on themselves** (2026-10-14). `craft3d-geom` reports a period for a cylinder, a cone, a sphere and a torus, and none for a Bézier or NURBS surface. The tessellator finds it: a spline surface closes in u (or in v, or in both, as a torus does) when its control net ends there on the row it starts with (the same points to 10⁻⁹ of the net's size, weights in one proportion, so that the two rows are one curve) and its knots are clamped; the period is the width of its domain. From there on such a face is a face on a closed surface like any other: its boundary is followed across the seam, a face that goes around is cut open along a seam whose two sides share their points, a face may reach a row of control points that is one point (a pole), and the seam may be an edge of the body that the loop walks there and back (then the face is an ordinary patch one period wide) or no edge at all. The surface itself is defined over one period only, so parameters beyond it are brought back into it wherever the surface is evaluated. Covered (`crates/tess/tests/closed_freeform.rs`, against closed forms, at three sizes): a full turn of a spline with a seam edge and between two circles, with the surface's seam anywhere and the turn in either parameter, a window across the seam and the wall around it, a NURBS torus with and without its two seams as edges, and a surface that closes to a point at both ends. **Not recognised, and refused:** a surface that closes on knots that are not clamped (the control net does not show it). Its seam is not followed, the boundary jumps from one end of the parameter range to the other, and that is what the face is refused for: a chord of an edge whose straight segment in parameter space strays further from the chord than the chord is long (and than twice the tolerance) is a boundary that is not closed in parameter space, an error at the face (`mesh_face`), on every kind of surface. (The first face on a closed spline surface, on 2026-10-13, came back as a mesh that was not closed, without an error: the points of its seam edge were projected now to one end of the range and now to the other.) A boundary whose extent on a spline surface is less than 10⁻⁹ of the surface's range is rounding of the projection and bounds no region.
- **Interior points keep their distance from the boundary** (2026-10-13). A grid point is left out when it is nearer than 0.3 of a grid step to any constraint, also one that is not an edge of the triangle the point falls in (the fan around a corner of a hole; found by walking the triangles within that distance, 64 at most). A point just above a chord of a hole, between the chord and its arc, makes a triangle that stands inside out, which refinement then never gets right.
- **Faces** are triangulated in parameter space (constrained Delaunay over the trimmed domain) with refinement until the surface deviation is within the chordal tolerance, then mapped to 3D. Planar faces need no refinement (but see *Long straight chords of flat faces* below).
- **The grid a curved face is seeded with** is sized so that a cell of it, cut in two triangles, is within the tolerance of the surface: by the arc for the analytic kinds, and for freeform surfaces by bounds on the second derivatives (Filip, Magedson & Markot 1986: over a right triangle with legs `hu` and `hv` along the parameters the deviation is at most `(Muu hu² + 2 Muv hu hv + Mvv hv²) / 8`). For a Bézier patch the bounds are those of its control net. **For a NURBS surface they are proven too since 2026-10-14** (until then: second differences on a lattice of 8 by 8 samples, times 1.5, which promised nothing): the surface is a rational Bézier patch on each knot span, each patch the face's box reaches is cut down to the part the box covers (across the seam too, for a surface that closes), and that part is bounded by `RationalBezierPatch::derivative_bounds` after four cuts in halves, each across the parameter that brings the bound down most. A spline face also gets a step of its own per parameter, `(Muu + Muv) hu² = (Mvv + Muv) hv² = 4 × tolerance`, which keeps the same bound and never has more cells than one step for both: the wall of an extruded spline, straight along the sweep, is one cell that way. Where one parameter would need more cells than the limit (`TessParams::max_segments`) and the other has room, it is held at the limit and the other made finer. The grid's promise is for its own cells; the triangles along the boundary are not cells of it and grid points too near the boundary are left out, so every triangle is measured afterwards all the same, and the measure at a triangle's centroid and the middles of its free sides remains a sample. *Measured* (2026-10-14, `crates/testkit/tests/mesh_hashes.rs`, 441 meshes: the reference solids straight and askew, boolean results, extruded, lofted and revolved splines, at three sizes and three tolerances): the 333 meshes of bodies without NURBS faces are what they were, bit for bit; the 108 of the twelve bodies with NURBS faces changed. Triangles at a tolerance of 10⁻³ of the size, before and after: a domed block with a NURBS roof 9,750 and 8,416 (now the mesh of its Bézier twin); an extruded leaf of two splines 10,966 and 324; a loft through three leaves 62,672 and 49,580; revolved vases 245,112 and 247,916 (degree 2), 269,380 and 318,048 (degree 3), 227,524 and 279,348 (degree 5), and up to a third more for three quarters of a turn: on rational surfaces the proven bounds are looser than 1.5 times what was sampled. The largest distance from the meshes to their surfaces, on seven points per triangle, is 0.889 of the tolerance before and after (8/9: a third along a boundary chord that sags by the whole tolerance); the sampled grid broke no promise that was measured, it made none. One body of the set is refused as too fine at 10⁻⁵ of its size where it was meshed before (a three-quarter turn of a cubic spline, 1.8 million triangles in one face): the proven grid needs 1,097 cells a side there against a limit of 1,024. The showcase documents: the six with splines changed (the housing from 5,244 to 1,868 triangles at the automatic tolerance, the vase from 17,300 to 20,980), the six without did not, bit for bit.
- **Output** keeps, per triangle, the face it came from (and per polyline, the edge), so the renderers can colour, select and pick by entity, and exporters can group.
- **Parallel per face** (rayon), merged in face order, so the result is identical for any thread count.
- **Our own triangulator.** No external triangulation or meshing library; this is part of "custom tessellator".
- **What a viewer draws: the display quality** (2026-10-13). The engine's scene (`Session::scene_with(SceneQuality)`, §8.5) is sampled at one of three qualities: `Automatic`, the chordal tolerance `view.render`, previews and `file.export` use when none is given (1e-3 of the model's size, the side of a square with the bodies' total area; without bodies, the diagonal of the sketches); `Tolerance(t)`, a tolerance in document units; and `Display { relative, max_triangles }`, what a viewport shows: `relative` times the model's size (2e-4 by default since 2026-10-14, a chord error of a fifth of a pixel on a model that fills a 1020-pixel viewport, with the tessellator's angle criterion at 12 degrees (`max_angle`, `DISPLAY_MAX_ANGLE`), so that a small hole stays round when the developer zooms in on it; 3e-5 without an angle before, see *An angle criterion* below), coarsened until the scene has at most `max_triangles` (a million) triangles and every body is within the tessellator's reach, but never beyond the automatic tolerance, so the display is never coarser than the export (since 2026-10-14 the size is the area of the quick first picture's mesh and the floor that picture's tolerance; see *Coarse first, then sharp* below). The coarsening is a ladder: a build with a budget stops at the first body after which the budget is exceeded, or that is too fine for the tessellator (`TessError::TooFine`), and the next tolerance is coarser by the ratio of the estimated total (the count so far over the share of the model's faces done) to the budget, at least twice and at most 64 times, six attempts at most; the scene says whether it was coarsened. The budget is the scene's, and it stops a body inside the tessellator (2026-10-13): the bodies are tessellated in feature order and each is given what the ones before it left (`TessParams::max_triangles`); the tessellator ends a body with `TessError::OverBudget` at the end of the first round of its faces in which they have more triangles than that, and ends that round as soon as the faces done show it (faces are done in parallel, each adds its triangles to a count, the ones not started are skipped). What it reports, and what the ladder's estimate takes, is the shortest run of faces from the first, in face order, whose triangles exceed the budget, as a single thread finds them: a function of the body and the parameters, not of the number of threads or of which finished first. So a scene within the budget is the scene built without one, bit for bit, and a scene over it is discarded; nothing in between. The granularity was a face until 2026-10-14 (one face that alone exceeded the budget, a fine sphere or the spring, was tessellated in full first); now a face stops too, see *A budget inside a face* below. Export, `view.render` and previews pass no budget. Measured on the plate with 1024 holes, asked for at 1e-6 of its size (647,180 triangles in full; processor time on a machine busy with other builds, relative to that full tessellation in the same run): a budget of 100,000 triangles took 0.79 of it before and takes 0.43 now, one of 30,000 took 0.78 and takes 0.24 (2.0 s against 6.7 s on the clock); the ladder ends at the same tolerance in 9 of 13 budgets on the part of `crates/engine/tests/scene.rs` and at a neighbouring one in the others (finer in 3, coarser in 1), because the estimate is now taken at the face that crossed the budget, not at the end of its body. Measured (release, 2026-10-13, `crates/engine/tests/scene_bench.rs`): a flange with six bolt holes, 820 triangles at the automatic tolerance and 4328 at the display quality, about 10 ms either way; a plate with 1024 holes (1030 faces), 24,588 triangles in 666 ms and 122,892 in 776 ms; the tessellator's cost beyond about 0.6 ms per face is about 1.5 µs per triangle, which is what the budget bounds.
- **Coarse first, then sharp** (2026-10-14). A viewer that keeps drawing while the display scene is built (`Session::set_refine_elsewhere`, the window) is answered for `Display` with a quick first picture until then: the scene at `PREVIEW_RELATIVE_TOLERANCE` (1e-3) of the diagonal of the box around the bodies (`Body::bbox`, from the geometry: nothing is measured), marked `Scene::preview` (`SceneQuality::Preview` asks for it directly). `Session::refinement` hands out the display scene's build with the document state and its regeneration (both `Arc`s), `Refinement::run` builds it on any thread and stops before its next body or attempt once cancelled, and `Session::accept_refined` keeps the result for the state it was built for, which replaces the first picture at the same revision. A host that does not refine elsewhere gets the display scene from `scene_with` as before, built from the same first picture. The display rule's size is the side of a square with the area of the first picture's mesh (within the first picture's tolerance of the exact area, which takes seconds to measure on spline faces), and its coarsest tolerance is the first picture's (the scene is then the first picture, bit for bit). *Measured* (release, a load of 10 on 14 cores, `crates/engine/tests/display_speed.rs`): regeneration, first picture after it, display scene after that, against the old display scene (regeneration, measure and ladder): the spring 0.8 s + 0.25 s (52,684 triangles) + 4.5 s (644,252) against 4.1 to 5.6 s; the screw 3.1 s + 1.1 s + 1.1 s (214,732) against 4.4 to 6.1 s; the plane 1.5 s + 0.08 s + 0.45 s (412,152) against 3.4 s; the villa 0.43 s + 0.04 s + 0.11 s (46,486) against 1.0 s. Where it goes: the spring's display scene is two attempts, the first (1.67 million triangles at 3e-5, over the budget) tessellated in full before it is given up, because the budget stops a body only between rounds of faces and the spring is one face; the screw's first picture costs more than its display scene, because the thread's faces cost the tessellator more at the coarse tolerance than at a finer one. Both are the tessellator's to improve. The display tolerance stayed 3e-5 then: at 1e-4 a pin hole a fiftieth of the model across is visibly faceted when zoomed onto, and only an angle criterion in the tessellator would keep it round at a coarser distance ([`decisions.md`](decisions.md), 2026-10-14, *What a viewport shows*); that criterion came the same day (below).
- **An angle criterion, a budget inside a face, a body kept within a budget** (2026-10-14). *The angle* (`TessParams::max_angle`, radians, `None` by default so export and `view.render` mesh bit for bit as before, which `crates/testkit/tests/mesh_hashes.rs` holds): a chord of an edge may not turn the curve's tangent by more than the angle between its ends (nor between an end and its middle; tangents taken a millionth of the chord inside it, so a kink at a knot is not the chord's), a free side of a triangle may not turn the surface's normal by more than the angle between its ends (normals kept per vertex while the face is refined), and a face asks for a chord of its edges to be cut where the surface's normal turns further along it than the angle allows (a cylinder cut by an oblique plane, whose ellipse turns less than the cylinder's normal along it). A circle gets the larger of its chordal and its angular count; the grid a cylinder, cone, sphere or torus is seeded with is sized by both. Below `ANGLE_FLOOR` (one chordal tolerance) a chord or a side is held to the tolerance alone, so the angle never asks for more than a fine tolerance would, has no length of its own (a body and its tolerance twice as large give the mesh twice as large, bit for bit), and refinement for it ends. A turn within a millionth of the angle is rounding (`turns_beyond`): an arc cut in chords of exactly the angle is not cut again. *A budget inside a face:* a face stops as soon as a floor of its own count exceeds `TessParams::max_triangles`: its region in parameter space is a polygon with holes, so with `i` points inside it has `2i + b + 2h - 2` triangles, of which at most `b` lose their area at a pole, so at least `2i - 2`; every point added (seed or refinement) is inside, and the count is checked as each is added. The count it reports is that floor, a function of the face and the parameters, so a face that alone exceeds the budget (a fine sphere, the spring) is no longer made in full first, and a mesh within the budget is still the very mesh made without one. *A body within a budget:* `tessellate_within_budget` tries coarser tolerances for the whole body (its edges are the edges of two faces, so one tolerance for the body keeps it closed by the same construction as always), by the estimated excess, at least twice and at most 64 times, sixteen attempts and the body's own size at most, and otherwise is the last `OverBudget`. The quick first picture keeps every body within `DISPLAY_MAX_TRIANGLES` this way, and so does the display rule's fall-back at the first picture's tolerance. *The display quality* is now 2e-4 of the size and 12 degrees (`DISPLAY_RELATIVE_TOLERANCE`, `DISPLAY_MAX_ANGLE`; `ui.set` `quality.angle` in degrees, 0 for none; `ui.get` `scene.angle`); the quick first picture has the chordal tolerance alone. *The angle is a wish* (2026-10-14): a body the tessellator cannot mesh with it (any error but a budget exceeded, or a patch too fine that the ladder is about to answer with a coarser tolerance) is drawn without it at the same tolerance, as `view.render` draws it, rather than left out of the scene (`build` in `crates/engine/src/scene.rs`; `a_body_that_cannot_be_meshed_with_the_angle_is_drawn_without_it`). Covered: `crates/tess/tests/display.rs` (the angle on every chord and on every triangle above the floor of the reference solids and trimmed fixtures, closed meshes, volume within the tolerance times the area, the same mesh on 1, 2 and 8 threads and at twice the size, a hole of radius 5 in a plate of 100 at a tolerance of 0.5: 7 chords without the angle, 36 with 10 degrees; one sphere face stopped near the budget; bodies coarsened to a third, a twentieth and 500 triangles of their mesh: within it, closed, the mesh at the tolerance found, the same twice), `crates/ui-egui/tests/gpu_viewport.rs` (the flange's bolt hole: its rim's sag under a third of a pixel, and under one at four times the zoom, on 760 × 679 and 2300 × 1325 viewports: 0.105 and 0.216 px). *Measured* (release, in one run on a machine at a load of 9 to 12 on 14 cores, two rounds each; the old display quality, 3e-5 without an angle, against the new one; the display scene's build after the first picture, regeneration not counted): the spring 5.4 to 6.2 s (644,252 triangles, coarsened, two attempts) against 0.91 to 0.98 s (194,028); the showcase screw 1.2 s (214,690) against 0.30 s (44,292); the M10 screw of `pbolt` 1.3 to 1.7 s (203,006) against 0.36 to 0.78 s (41,882); the aircraft 0.45 to 0.62 s (412,152) against 0.18 s (68,004); the villa 0.14 s (46,486) against 0.17 s (20,888: many small curved faces, where the angle's normals cost more than the triangles saved). The first picture, meshed after regeneration (`crates/engine/tests/display_speed.rs`, same load): 0.33, 0.26, 0.39, 0.14 and 0.06 s, within half a second on all five; what is left before a first picture is regeneration (0.9 to 5.6 s at that load; the screws' thread and booleans). The flange with a pin hole of radius 0.8 zoomed onto (`target/evidence/tess-budget/`, `window_picture`): 4,100 triangles at 3e-5, round; 1,644 at 2e-4 alone, a twelve-gon; 1,860 at 2e-4 and 12 degrees, round.
- **The angle on a thin fillet** (2026-10-15). On a fillet a hundred times thinner than the ring it runs around (the turbofan's stripes and flange roots: a tube of 0.6 mm on a ring of 50 mm) the surface's normal turns slowly over a long way around the axis and fast over a short way across the tube, so the cells of a grid that meet the angle are a hundred times longer than they are wide. The triangulation's plane was scaled to lengths in space (*Faces* above), where such a cell is a sliver; every diagonal of the grid turned the normal by both of its sides at once (about `√(tu² + tv²)`, beyond the angle when each side is at it), and every point the refinement put on one was joined by Delaunay's rule to points rows away, whose sides turned too far again. The turbofan at its display tolerance had 579,314 triangles, 9.1 times its 63,958 without the angle, 501,630 of them on tori (one fillet: 41,077 where about a thousand do). Three changes, all only with the angle (export meshes bit for bit as they were, `mesh_hashes`): *(a) the plane of the grid:* a torus or a sphere is triangulated with each parameter scaled so that a cell of its seed grid is square (`face_boundary`; the reach of a chord's bulge in that plane is scaled with it, `Boundary::stretch`), so a point added stays among its neighbours; *(b) diagonals within the angle:* the grid gives `v` what `u` leaves of the angle, `tv = √(angle² − tu²)`, with `u` held to `angle / √2` where it needs more (`both_ways` in `face.rs`), so the grid passes the angle with no refinement but along the boundary; *(c) the floor across a tube:* a side shorter than the floor is not held to the angle, and a fillet thinner than the tolerance got two cells across its tube, but the grid's diagonals are as long as its sides around the axis, and where those are not below the floor the refinement cut them until they were (a dozen points per cell, and more triangles at a coarser tolerance than at a finer one); there `v` gets the cells the angle asks for whatever the floor (at most a whole turn's worth at `tv`, and the grid's limit): a count of the angle alone, which keeps the tessellator free of a length of its own. *Measured* (release, one thread per face as usual, on a machine shared with other builds; plain is the same tolerance without the angle): the turbofan at 0.1726 mm 129,532 triangles in 0.22 to 0.44 s (579,314 in 0.9 s before; plain 63,958), 2.03 times plain; the engine block at 0.2422 mm 66,998 (2.36 times plain, of which tori 3,119 to 21,663, the rest the angle's finer edges on cones, cylinders and planes; before, one of its bodies could not be meshed at all, see the seam bullet); the stripe of `display_quality.rs` with a fillet of 0.25 mm 2,168 triangles at every tolerance from 0.17 to 1 mm (14,000 at 0.1 before); the showcase screw 36,432 (44,292 before, plain 37,078), the aircraft 68,004 and the spring 194,028 (unchanged: spline faces), the villa 20,938 (20,888). Covered by `crates/tess/tests/thin_torus.rs`: a torus and a pipe elbow of 50 by 0.6 at 0.17, 0.05 and 0.01 mm, every side not below the floor within the angle on the mesh's own normals (at most 12.000 degrees for 12), closed, the volume within the tolerance times the area, the same mesh on one and eight threads, and at most three times the triangles of a grid that meets both criteria unrefined (165,050 triangles for the torus at 0.17 before, 92 such grids; 3,960 now, 2.2). Spline faces were refined as before: a grid plane for them changed nothing on the models (until *The angle on a spline face* below).

- **Long straight chords of flat faces** (2026-10-15). A plane face gets no points inside, and a straight edge is one chord however long, so a plate 4010 mm long with a row of 400 holes along it had, between each long edge and the holes, no point but the edge's two ends: its top and bottom were fans of slivers from the plate's corners across the whole plate (sides of 4010 mm), poor to draw and to export, and the self-intersection check, which prunes by boxes, found every one of them near every other (the first cut of all 400 holes: 140 ms of checking on a boolean of 200 ms, §5.5). Before any face is triangulated (`body.rs`, `cut_long_chords`; `long.rs`), each plane face looks at its straight chords: a piece of a chord in front of which two or more points of the face's boundary lie (projected inside the piece, on the face's side) nearer than an eighth of the piece's length (`LONG_CHORD`) is cut in two and its halves looked at again, down to the chordal tolerance. One point in front makes one triangle, not a fan; the points of the edges before and after the chord in its loop meet it at a corner (a half disc's arc over its diameter, a wedge) and are not counted: a long block, a thin wedge, a half disc are meshed as before. The points are the edge's, for every face that uses it (the edge's own count from then on, `EdgeSamples::cut_long`); a face across a thin wall may ask for its other edge in turn, six rounds at most, each with the faces that use an edge just cut. Points near a chord are found in a grid of about one cell per point of the face, in face order: the same for any number of threads; ratios only, the same cuts at any scale. *Measured* (release, 2026-10-15): the plate's first cut checked in 27 to 31 ms instead of 115 to 147 (its whole check 33 to 68 ms instead of 430 to 860; the mesh alone 11 to 17 ms either way, 7,064 triangles instead of 6,428); a new hole's check near it 2.4 ms instead of 1.3 to 2.1; the engine block 28,928 triangles at its display tolerance without the angle instead of 28,438 (67,576 with it instead of 66,998), the turbofan 64,022 instead of 63,958, the villa 2 more. Of the 440 meshes of `mesh_hashes` 27 changed, each by two triangles (one chord cut once: the domed blocks with a hole 0.3 from the far edge of their 3-long bottom, and the three-quarter vase of degree 2, whose section is narrow at its waist), at every scale alike. Covered by `crates/testkit/tests/long_chords.rs` (a plate with 100 holes: no side longer than eight times the distance from the holes, closed, the volume within the tolerance times the area, the same mesh on one and eight threads and at the three scales; a long block, a half disc and a plate with one hole as before).
- **The angle on a spline face** (2026-10-16). With the display's angle the turbofan's spline faces (its fan blades) had 35,302 triangles where the chordal tolerance alone gives 19,760. Measured against a grid that meets both criteria with nothing refined (whole columns and rows cut in two where a cell fails, `crates/testkit/tests/spline_angle.rs`), one side of a blade had 830 where such a grid has 750 (the curvature's cost), the other 936 where it has 336: a side of an aerofoil turns half a turn around its nose in a few hundredths of its chord, its parameter across runs 45 times slower near the nose than at the root of the side (|∂S/∂u| 102.6 to 2.3), and one scale per parameter fits such a face nowhere near the nose, so the refinement placed the points the angle asks for one by one, joined to points the plane said were near and the surface did not. Now (`angle_warp` in `face.rs`, with the angle on Bézier and NURBS faces only, so export meshes are as they were) each parameter is cut into 32 pieces, the normal sampled at the ends and the middle of each along 9 lines across the face's box, a piece that turns further than a cell may (`angle / √2`, so that a diagonal keeps within the angle) cut in two and looked at again (1,024 pieces at most), and each piece gets the cells its turn asks for, at least the chordal grid's share (the floor as for a thin torus: no cell shorter than it along a parameter whose sides across are below it). The plane along each parameter is the count of cells so far, monotone and piecewise linear: every cell of the graded grid the face is seeded with is a unit square of that plane, as on a torus. A sample, not a bound: the refinement still holds every side to the angle. The warped plane is tried first; where its boundary would cross itself it asks for the chords concerned like any crossing (`chords_to_refine` checks both planes); where its triangles cannot follow a chord they ask for it while the rounds can still cut (`mesh_face`'s `retry`); anything else, and in the last round, falls back to the plane of the scales, as without the warp. *Measured* (release, display tolerance and 12 degrees): the turbofan's spline faces 30,720 (35,302; plain 19,760), the whole turbofan 125,014 (129,532); the aircraft's 65,957 (67,777), the rotor's 75,718 (74,010), the blade's 5,452 (5,416), the vase, helmet, impeller, hose, spring and loft hull as they were. Tried and dropped: the graded grid alone in the old plane (35,302 to 38,520), whole lines of the chordal grid cut where a cell turns too far (39,640), the torus's square plane for splines (38,314, though it gave the impeller 864 instead of 3,190 triangles: its spline faces are over-refined without the angle too, an open item). Covered by `crates/testkit/tests/spline_angle.rs`: a twisted blade lofted through three cambered aerofoils at 0.003 and 0.001, every side not below the floor within the angle on the mesh's own normals, closed, the same mesh on one and eight threads and at 1,024 times the size, and at most 1.5 times the triangles of the unrefined grid (2,880 against 2,132 and 7,998 against 5,728; 4,070 and 8,482 before).
- **Self-intersections of a mesh** (`tess::self_intersections(&Mesh, tolerance, limit)`, as built 2026-10-13): the pairs of triangles that cross or overlap although they are not neighbours. *Whether* two triangles meet is decided exactly: orientation predicates evaluated in `f64` with an error bound and, where that cannot tell, again without rounding as sums of non-overlapping `f64` (`exact.rs`, after Shewchuk 1997; our own code, no dependency), because the triangles of a planar face are coplanar to the last bit or a rounding error from it. Neighbours (bit-identical corners) meet only beyond what they share. *How deep* is a measure in `f64`: how far the surface around one triangle reaches on both sides of the other inside the column over it, the smaller of the two directions, so that contact has depth zero and a tip that pricks a surface has the depth of the prick (the column is a hair wider than its triangle only across an edge beyond which the surface goes on, so that what stands exactly on a seam of the triangulation passes through; where the surface turns away square, as around an edge along which two parts of a body meet, what stands on the edge touches); for triangles in one plane, the width of their common area. Candidates come from a bounding-volume hierarchy, searched on several threads (`std::thread::scope`, as the tessellator does) and merged in triangle order.

Mesh invariants are tested, not hoped for: closed 2-manifold for a solid, consistent outward orientation, no degenerate triangles, deviation from the surface within tolerance, mesh volume converging to the B-rep volume as the tolerance shrinks.

---

## 7. Rendering

Display is the one place with two implementations.

### 7.1 Draw list

`render` and `gpu` consume the same data: meshes and edge polylines from `tess` with entity ids, a camera (orthographic or perspective), lights, a display mode (shaded, shaded with edges, wireframe, hidden-line later) and per-entity colours and selection state. Nothing about display leaks into `kernel` or `tess`.

### 7.2 Backends

- **CPU (`render`).** The reference implementation: a software rasteriser with a depth buffer, flat/smooth shading, edge overlay, and an **id buffer** (which face or edge is under each pixel). Used by tests, the CLI (`craft3d-cli render`), MCP previews, `ui.screenshot`, and as the fallback in the viewport when no GPU is available or asked for (`--renderer cpu`). It favours exactness and readability over speed.
- **GPU (`gpu`).** wgpu (Vulkan, Metal, DX12, OpenGL, WebGPU) pipelines for the same draw list; picking reads the id buffer. The scene pass draws one sample per pixel into targets `ssaa` times larger per axis (an `Rgba8Unorm` colour target, an `Rg32Uint` target with the face id and the bits of the view distance, a `Depth32Float` buffer with near and far fitted to the mesh per frame, its depth reversed: see *Reversed depth* below); a resolve pass takes the rounded integer mean of the colour samples and the first sample's id and distance, exactly as the CPU renderer ends. Positions go to the GPU in `f32` relative to the centre of the mesh; the camera is computed in `f64` and narrowed last. That is the crate's `Offscreen` and `Renderer`, which read the frame back. **The viewport of the desktop app draws with the crate's `Onscreen`** (2026-10-13) on the window's own wgpu device, into a texture egui shows as it is: nothing is read back for display. Its scene pass draws the same shader into a colour target and depth buffer with 4 samples per pixel (MSAA; 1 where the adapter has no multisampling for the formats, and on wgpu's OpenGL backend, which resolved nothing out of a multisampled attachment on the development machine), shading once per pixel at its centre, exactly where the CPU renderer samples for `ssaa = 1`; a second pass draws the lines over the picture (the edges of the bodies, the world axes, the sketches, the sketch points) as screen-space quads inked by the distance of the pixel's centre to the segment (the CPU viewport's `draw_line`), each tested in the fragment shader against the depth buffer with the CPU viewport's rule (a point is behind a body only when the surface under its pixel and the eight around it is nearer, with the slack the GPU frame gets; a hidden edge is left out, a hidden sketch drawn dimmed), and resolves the samples into the shown texture. The GPU tests against the true depth where the CPU viewport tests against its frame's screen-interpolated distance, so the GPU allows a line the depth spread of the nine pixels as well: that keeps the edge in the hollow where two surfaces meet visible, as the CPU's interpolation happens to. A third pass, only when asked, draws face ids and distances at one sample per pixel (for the tests, and for picking later). Three differences are known and documented in the crate: a triangle that crosses the eye plane is clipped where the CPU renderer drops it whole, a triangle with a corner that does not fit in `f32` is not drawn, and a sample exactly on an edge follows the GPU's top-left rule.
- **Reversed depth** (2026-10-14). The depth the GPU stores is 1 at the near plane and falls towards 0 far away, and every depth test keeps the greater (`Greater`, cleared to 0; on a tie the triangle drawn first stays, as before). In perspective it is `near / dist` (no far plane: the clip `z` is the near distance and `w` the view distance), in orthographic `(far - dist) / (far - near)`; the lines and the datum fills turn it back into a distance as `near / z` and `far - z (far - near)`. Why: near and far are fitted to the box around the mesh, and with the eye inside that box (zoomed into a building) the near plane must sit right in front of the eye, at 10⁻⁶ of the far distance. With the depth rising from 0 at the near plane to 1 at the far one, as it did, all the precision of a float near 0 sits at the near plane and a step of the buffer is `2⁻²⁴ · dist² / near` of distance: half a metre at 20 m in a 50 m model, so a screed 4 cm above its slab fought with it in stairs along every edge (measured: 574 face ids wrong outside the band on Vulkan, 833 on OpenGL, 22097 on lavapipe in `tests/depth_precision.rs`, against the CPU renderer). A float holds `near / dist` to the same relative precision at every distance (`2⁻²⁴ · dist`), so the near plane costs nothing further on and the same scene matches the CPU renderer within the tolerances of every other picture.
- **Parity tests** (`crates/gpu/tests/parity.rs`; the derivations are in `crates/gpu/tests/common/mod.rs`). 75 scenes (the Utah teapot, the sixteen reference solids of the test kit tilted at scales 1e-3, 1 and 1e6 and once ten million units from the origin, and hand-made awkward meshes), in perspective and orthographic, at supersampling 1 to 4, on every adapter of the machine. **The documented tolerance** is:
  - *The band.* The GPU's rasteriser snaps projected vertices to a grid of 2⁻⁸ pixel, so the renderers can disagree about a sample only within 2⁻⁸ pixel of a triangle's edge, and that shows only where the surface under the sample changes. The band is every pixel within one pixel of a projected *feature edge* (a face's border, an edge with one triangle, a silhouette edge) or of a change of face id in the CPU frame. It is computed from the mesh, the camera and the CPU frame, never from the GPU frame, and must leave more than half of the picture.
  - *Face ids:* equal outside the band. Inside it at most 1/128 of the band's pixels may differ (the probability that an edge passes within 2⁻⁸ pixel of a pixel's centre).
  - *Distance* outside the band: within `2⁻²⁰ · reach + 2⁻⁷ · G`, where `reach` is the eye's distance to the mesh centre plus the mesh diagonal (sixteen `f32` roundings of 2⁻²⁴ on numbers that large) and `G` is the largest difference to the eight neighbouring pixels in the CPU frame (the gradient, times one grid step, doubled).
  - *Colour* outside the band: at most 1/255 per channel, with no exceptions (both renderers round per sample; a value within the `f32` error of a half-integer rounds the other way), plus `2⁻⁷ · Gc` levels where the CPU picture itself changes by `Gc` between neighbours, which is a second level only next to a jump of 128 or more. The number of pixels that differ by that one level is bounded too, by the sum over the pixels of `2 · (2⁻⁷ · Gc + 0.004)`: a systematic error such as a truncated mean exceeds it. Inside the band at most 1/128 of the pixels may differ by more than a level.

  Measured on the three adapters of the development machine (Intel Vulkan, lavapipe, Intel OpenGL): no id differs outside the band, at most 0.07 % of a scene's pixels outside the band differ by one level and none by two, the largest distance error is 39 % of its tolerance, and at most 0.14 % of a band's pixels differ. The tests **fail** when no adapter is found, with the way to get one (`mesa-vulkan-drivers` gives lavapipe); they do not skip.
  - *The multisampled picture* (`crates/gpu/tests/onscreen.rs`, `compare_msaa` in the same `common/mod.rs`) is compared with the CPU frame at `ssaa = 1`, with the ids and distances of the capture pass, under the same tolerance with two exceptions that follow from multisampling: inside the band the colour is not compared at all (the samples lie on both sides of a feature edge and the resolved colour is their blend: the anti-aliasing; the number of such pixels is measured and must be more than a twentieth of the band), and on a *seam* (within a pixel of an interior triangle edge of one face) a pixel may mix two fragments, each shaded at the pixel centre from its own triangle's interpolated normal, so it is allowed `1 + Gc` levels, a pixel of the CPU picture's own gradient. Measured on the development machine: away from every edge not one pixel of the teapot or the block differs by a level, at most 6 seam pixels of a scene differ by more than a level, and about a tenth of the band is blended. The lines are checked on a block with lines behind, before, on and beside it (dimmed, left out, whole), and `crates/ui-egui/tests/gpu_viewport.rs` draws the viewport's own scene through the shell on a device made as eframe makes the window's and compares the shown picture with the CPU picture (bodies and inked lines) the same way, sampling every line of the overlay to check that the GPU hid, dimmed and drew what the CPU decided: 1812 of 1812 samples agree.
- **See-through on the GPU** (2026-10-14; `crates/gpu/src/peel.rs`, `peel.wgsl`, `peel_samples.wgsl`, `blend.wgsl`). `RenderParams::opacity` below 1 is drawn with two depth layers, as the CPU renderer defines it (*Current state*, *See-through bodies*): the front surface blended over the nearest surface strictly behind it. At opacity 1 (and at an opacity that is not a number) none of it runs: the passes, pipelines and targets are the ones of the opaque picture, and the frame is that picture bit for bit (asserted on every adapter).
  - *The passes.* (1) The front: the opaque scene pass with another fragment entry, whose alpha says whether the sample is seen through (1 for a body's face, 0 for a highlighted face; the target is cleared to alpha 0, so the background is not blended either), and whose depth buffer is kept. (2) The one behind: the same triangles into a colour target and a depth buffer of their own (`Greater` on the reversed depth), each fragment dropped unless it is behind the front. (3) The blend: one triangle over the picture that reads both layers per sample, blends them and writes the mean of the samples (the supersamples of the readback renderer, in place of its resolve pass; the multisamples of the viewport, in place of the hardware resolve), with the frame's ids and distances taken from the front as ever. Then, in the viewport, the lines: straight onto the shown texture at one sample (they are inked by distance, not by coverage), tested against the front's depth buffer as over an opaque picture. Per frame that is one more pass over the triangles and one more over the pixels; the extra targets (a colour layer and a depth buffer behind, and in the viewport a front layer and its keys, all with the samples of the renderer) are made for the first see-through frame of a size.
  - *The comparison of the layers, and its bias: strict, and none.* The front is the nearest fragment at its sample, so every other fragment that covers the sample is at or behind it: all that has to be told apart is the front itself (and what ties with it) from the rest. With one sample per pixel (the readback renderer; the viewport on OpenGL) the second pass reads the front's depth buffer as a texture and keeps a fragment when its own depth is less (further, with the depth reversed). Both passes rasterise the same triangles with the same vertex stage on the same device, so a triangle's depth at a sample is the same number in both and a surface is never its own "behind"; a bias would only hide a surface that is really there. Two surfaces closer together than `Depth32Float` resolves over the frame's depth range (about 10⁻⁷ of it) have equal depths and then nothing is behind the front, where the CPU renderer in `f64` still sees the second: measured on a wall 10⁻⁹ of its width thick, every pixel sees past both sheets on all adapters, and a wall 10⁻⁴ of its width thick shows its other side as on the CPU.
  - *With several samples per pixel* a fragment is shaded once per pixel and the depth test runs per sample on depths the shader does not see, so that comparison is not available: the fragment's own depth is the triangle's depth at the pixel centre. Making the pass run per sample does not help: **measured on Intel's Vulkan driver (Mesa 25.3.6), a per-sample fragment's depth differs from the depth buffer by far more than rounding** (every body pixel showed its own front as "behind"; lavapipe agreed with its depth buffer). The viewport therefore stores, next to the front's colour, a *key* of the triangle in front at each sample: the bits of its depth at the pixel centre, as the four bytes of an `Rgba8Unorm` target (the one format a multisampled viewport needs anyway; a byte goes in and comes out exactly). The second pass computes the same key for its own triangle (same triangle, same pixel, same arithmetic) and drops, through the fragment's sample mask, the samples whose front has that key; the depth test then keeps the nearest of what is left, per sample and exactly. Two triangles with the same corners have the same key, so a face that coincides with the front is not behind it, as on the CPU (asserted). No multisampled depth texture is read by the peeling, only by the lines as before, so the OpenGL limitation of the decisions document changes nothing here: on OpenGL the viewport has one sample and takes the first path.
  - *The blend is the CPU's own.* `opacity · front + (1 − opacity) · behind`, rounded, is a function of two bytes; the renderer tabulates it in `f64` with the CPU renderer's expression (a 256 × 256 `R8Unorm` texture, written when the opacity changes) and the blend pass looks it up per channel. So a sample whose two layers equal the CPU's has the CPU's colour exactly, also where the exact blend lies half-way between two levels (at opacity 0.1 that is a tenth of all pairs of levels) and `f32` would round the other way.
  - *The tolerance for a see-through picture*, derived in `crates/gpu/tests/common/mod.rs`, is the one above with two layers in place of one. Both layers are known from the CPU renderer alone: the front picture is its frame at opacity 1, the picture behind its frame at opacity 0. *The band* also holds every pixel within one pixel of a change of the face behind the front (where two surfaces behind the front pass through each other, which no mesh edge and no id of the front shows; the test finds that face from the definition with a small rasteriser of its own, for the band only). *Colour* outside the band: each layer is within its own `1 + ⌊2⁻⁷ · G⌋`, the exact blends therefore within the larger of the two, and rounding is monotone and commutes with adding an integer, so the blended levels differ by at most `1 + ⌊2⁻⁷ · max(G_front, G_behind)⌋`. *How many* differ at all: the probabilities of the two layers add. Ids and distances are the front's and keep their tolerances. Nothing was loosened: the same per-pixel limit, the same counted bound.
  - *Measured* (three adapters: Intel Vulkan, lavapipe, Intel OpenGL; `parity.rs`, 114 see-through scenes per adapter: the sixteen reference solids at opacities 0.1, 0.5 and 0.9, their three other rounds of scale and distance at one opacity each, the teapot, a plate with a hole and a body inside a hollow body at all three, a third of them with highlighted faces): no id differs outside the band, no colour by two levels, at most 0.11 % of a scene's pixels outside the band by one level (never more than 92 pixels), the largest distance error 23 % of its tolerance, at most 0.15 % of a band's pixels differ. The viewport's picture (`onscreen.rs`, nine scenes per adapter, four samples on Vulkan): not one pixel away from every edge differs by a level, at most one seam pixel by more than a level. `crates/gpu/tests/see_through.rs` holds the scenes where the comparison of the layers bites, for both renderers: one sheet in four views (nowhere its own "behind"), a wall of two sheets 10⁻² and 10⁻⁴ of its width apart, two sheets 10⁻⁹ apart, a face corner for corner on the front, a box seen 2° above its top face, lines over a see-through picture (hidden and dimmed, hidden and left out, in front), hostile opacities and a renderer told it cannot. `crates/ui-egui/tests/gpu_viewport.rs` draws the window's own scene see-through on a device made as eframe makes the window's: 0 of 408 223 pixels away from edges and lines differ, and 1769 of 1769 samples along the lines agree (a hidden, dimmed edge lies on a feature edge of what is behind the front, where a multisampled pixel mixes the faces that meet there: such a sample is compared with the dimmed ink over the range of the CPU picture's 3 × 3 pixels around it).
  - *A finding about the oracle, and the rule that removed it (2026-10-14).* The CPU renderer has a sample that lies exactly on an edge two triangles share inside both (its edge function is inclusive on both sides, so that a closed surface has no pinholes), and their depth keys there agree only up to rounding; where the second came out a hair greater, the surface was its own "behind" and the sample was drawn opaque. A square of two triangles seen head-on at 240 × 180, with pixel centres on its diagonal, had 8 such pixels in the CPU's see-through picture and none in the GPU's (whose rasteriser gives such a sample to one triangle). **The rule:** the triangles that share an edge or a vertex are one surface at a sample that lies exactly on it. The one in front there is the front as ever (nothing about the opaque picture changes: the same triangle wins, by the same comparison); the others are not behind it, and when one of them takes the front over, the old front does not become the one behind. "The same edge" is the coordinates of its two ends in space (of the vertex, for a sample on a vertex), kept per sample of a see-through picture as one number (`render::shared_place`). What changed: see-through pictures, only on such samples (the 8 pixels of that square are now the blend the other 14 392 are; the 30 see-through frames of the reference solids and the teapot that were hashed before and after are bit for bit the same, as are all opaque ones), and `a_surface_is_never_behind_itself` now compares that view like every other, on the CPU too. *Not taken: the top-left rule in the CPU rasteriser* (a sample on an edge belongs to one triangle outright, as on the GPU). It was tried: every existing test stayed green and every hashed picture the same, but by its definition it also drops a sample that lies exactly on the right or bottom edge of a mesh's outline, which changes opaque pictures at samples no two triangles share, and the oracle was not to change there; it remains the difference documented in `crates/gpu/src/lib.rs`. The count of a section's cut-away fragments does use the top-left rule: a count needs each surface once, and at an outline both rules give the same parity.
  - *What is refused.* A device on which the pipelines of the layers cannot be made keeps drawing opaque pictures and answers a see-through one with `GpuError::SeeThrough` and the reason; the viewport then draws that frame with its other renderer (`ui.get` › `render.renderer` says which). `Onscreen::without_see_through` and `Renderer::without_see_through` put a renderer in that state for the tests of the fallback. None of the adapters this was built on is in it.
  - *Cost* (release, Intel integrated graphics, the frame until the GPU is done, `examples/viewport_cost.rs`): at 1020 × 777 with four samples 1.7 ms opaque and 4.7 ms see-through for 12 800 triangles, 12.5 ms and 19.0 ms for 665 000; at 2560 × 1400, 5.0 and 9.1 ms, 15.8 and 25.7 ms. The CPU renderer takes 176 to 227 ms for the same see-through frames at 1020 × 777.
- **Section on the GPU** (2026-10-14; `crates/gpu/src/cut.rs`, `cut.wgsl`, `cut_flat.wgsl`, `cut_samples.wgsl`). `RenderParams::section` is drawn as the CPU renderer defines it (*Current state*, *Section plane*). Without a section none of it runs and none of it is built: the pipelines of a cut-open picture are made for the first one, the passes, entries and targets of a whole picture are the ones they were (`vs_main` and the fragment entries are untouched; the scene's uniform block grew by four vectors), and the frame is bit for bit the one before this was built (30 scenes, opaque and see-through, hashed on the CPU and on three adapters before and after: 120 of 120 equal; and asserted per adapter: the frame after a cut-open one equals a fresh renderer's).
  - *The cut* is a fragment `discard`: the vertex stage of a cut-open picture (`vs_cut`) adds the vertex's distance from the plane as one more interpolated value, and every fragment entry has a twin that drops the fragment by `Cut::clips` (the triangle's own normal, already a flat vertex attribute, says whether a triangle in the plane goes with its body).
  - *The count, and why it is a blend and not a stencil.* The cap needs, per sample, whether the number of fragments left out there is odd. The depth buffer is `Depth32Float` on every backend and has no stencil, and a stencil format beside it would be a second depth-stencil attachment, which no backend has. So the count is a pass of its own over the triangles, before the others and without a depth test, that keeps only the fragments that are cut away and writes them into an `R8Unorm` target whose blend is "one minus what was there" (source factor `OneMinusDst`, destination factor `Zero`): 0 and 1 are exact in a normalised byte, and the blend runs per sample where the target is multisampled. It is one more pass over the triangles per frame, with no shading.
  - *The cap* is one triangle in the plane, large enough to hold the box around the mesh (grown by the margin the cap may lie in, which the depth range of a cut-open frame is fitted to as well), drawn after the triangles of the mesh in every pass that draws them, through the same vertex stage and the same depth test (`Greater` on the reversed depth: a triangle of the mesh wins a tie, as on the CPU). Its fragment is kept where the count is odd and the point is within the box; its colour is the cap's as it is, and its distance in the frame is the distance of the point of the plane (not the screen-interpolated one of a triangle that large). One triangle, not a quad: no diagonal for a sample to lie on.
  - *With one sample per pixel* (the readback renderer, whose supersamples are pixels of a larger target; the viewport on OpenGL) the cap reads the count at its pixel. *With several* (the viewport: 4 on Vulkan) a fragment is shaded once per pixel, so whether it is cut away is decided at the pixel's centre for all of its samples, in the pass that counts and in the pass that draws alike (the same triangle, the same pixel, the same arithmetic: the two never disagree); the count is then per sample of the fragments left out there, and the cap takes the samples where it is odd through its sample mask, so a silhouette keeps its anti-aliasing and the picture has no hole between a wall and the cap. Along the cut itself the decision is per pixel, not per sample: that line is where the outline of the section is drawn as an edge.
  - *Seen through*, the cap is a surface of both layers, as on the CPU: in the front pass it is drawn with alpha 1 (seen through like a body's face) and, with several samples, with its key; in the pass behind the front it is kept where the count is odd *and* it is behind the front, by the comparison of §*See-through on the GPU* (its depth against the front's with one sample; its key against the front's with several: the cap's triangle goes through the same vertex stage in both passes, so it is never its own "behind").
  - *The viewport's lines* are cut on the CPU before they are uploaded (`viewport::Marks::kept`: an edge of a body ends at the plane; `Cut::outline` adds the outline of the cut as edges), and the cap is in the depth buffer they are tested against, so a line behind the cap is hidden like behind any face (asserted on a block: a line inside the body behind the cap is not drawn, a line in the cut half is).
  - *The tolerance for a cut-open picture* (derived in `crates/gpu/tests/common/mod.rs`, *Cut-open pictures*) is the one above, not loosened. The renderers can decide a cut differently only within the `f32` error of a distance from the plane (a few 10⁻⁷ of the mesh's size), which on screen is a strip along the line where the plane crosses the surface: **the band** also holds every pixel within one pixel of the projected outline of the section, which is known from the mesh and the plane alone. A miscount changes the parity only at that outline and at an edge with one triangle (at a shared edge a sample is in exactly one of the two triangles in either renderer; at a silhouette of the cut-away part in both or neither), so outside the band the cap's id is equal, its colour is equal exactly (unshaded: asserted byte for byte, the tolerance of one level is not used) and its distance is within the tolerance of any surface; inside the band the same 1/128 holds.
  - *Measured* (three adapters: Intel Vulkan, lavapipe, Intel OpenGL; `crates/gpu/tests/section.rs`). 322 cut-open scenes per adapter (the sixteen reference solids and two more, each across x, y and z at two offsets and across an oblique plane, opaque and see-through at opacities 0.1, 0.5 and 0.9, every other one with highlighted faces; the same solids at scales 10⁻³ and 10⁶ and ten million units from the origin; a block with a cavity and a plate with a drilled hole, both made by a boolean; the teapot without a cap and with one): no id differs outside the band, no colour by two levels, at most 26 pixels of a scene by one, the largest distance error is 22 % of its tolerance, and inside the band at most 2 ids and 5 colours of a scene differ. The viewport's picture (18 scenes per adapter, four samples on Vulkan): at most 7 pixels away from every edge differ by a level; on seams at most 40 by more than a level, all within the seam's tolerance, in one scene (the teapot with a third of its faces highlighted). *The cap's area* in an orthographic view along the normal, against the closed form of the cross-section (400 × 400 pixels; the allowed error is the pixels the outline passes through): a block across x, y, z and obliquely (6, 8, 12 and 3·√8) within 0.8 %, a cylinder across (π r²) within 0.013 %, a ball at distance d from its centre (π (r² − d²)) within 0.054 %, a hollow block (the frame, 24 − 8) within 0.25 % and through its wall (24) within 0.1 %; the CPU and every adapter count the same number of pixels in all eight.
  - *What is refused.* A device on which the pipelines of a cut-open picture cannot be made keeps drawing whole pictures and answers one with a section with `GpuError::Section` and the reason; the viewport then draws that frame with its other renderer, as for `GpuError::SeeThrough`. `Onscreen::without_section` and `Renderer::without_section` put a renderer in that state for the tests of the fallback. None of the adapters this was built on is in it. A plane whose numbers do not fit `f32` relative to the mesh is `GpuError::OutOfRange`, which the viewport treats the same way.
  - *Cost* (release, Intel integrated graphics while other work ran on the machine, the frame until the GPU is done, `examples/viewport_cost.rs`): at 1020 × 777 with four samples, 12 800 triangles: 3.8 ms whole, 7.2 ms cut open, 8.3 ms see-through, 16.1 ms both; 665 000 triangles: 26.6, 35.6, 48.1 and 58.0 ms; at 2560 × 1400: 34.1, 50.8, 79.2 and 99.5 ms. Moving the plane costs, besides the frame, its outline on the bodies and the lines over the picture made again: 0.2 ms for 12 800 triangles, 11 ms for 665 000. In the window (impeller, 105 000 triangles, 1020 × 754): the drawing thread spends 0.33 ms per frame on the whole picture and 0.35 to 0.47 ms on a cut-open one while the camera turns, and about 2 ms per frame while the plane moves. The CPU renderer takes 112 to 226 ms for the same cut-open frames at 1020 × 777 with four samples (a cut-open frame is cheaper there than a whole one: what is cut away is not shaded).

- **Colours of their own** (2026-10-15; `craft3d_render::FaceColors`, `render_colored`; on the GPU `Renderer::upload_colored`). A face with a colour of its own (a body's appearance, §4.2) is shaded from it instead of `RenderParams::color`, in exactly the same way (light, ambient term; a highlight wins over it); with no colours the picture is the old one bit for bit. On the GPU the colour travels with the mesh as one more vertex word (`VERTEX_BYTES` 44: red, green, blue and a flag byte; flat-interpolated), so a change of colour is a new upload, which a new document state is anyway; every pass (opaque, see-through, cut open) shades with it. Parity: the reference solids with every face, or every other face, in a palette of light, dark, saturated and grey colours, opaque and see-through, highlighted on top (`parity.rs` › `faces_in_colours_of_their_own_match_the_cpu_renderer`, the three adapters) under the tolerance above, not loosened. The metallic and roughness factors are not shaded yet.

- **Datums in the viewport** (2026-10-14; `engine/src/scene.rs` › `SceneDatum`, `ui-egui/src/viewport.rs` › `fill_datum_planes`, `Marks::datums`, `crates/gpu/src/fills.wgsl`). The scene carries every datum that is placed (built, not suppressed, not rolled back; a failed datum has no place and is not drawn, the tree marks it): a plane as the square `center ± u ± v`, centred on the foot of the model box's centre on the plane, half side `DATUM_PLANE_EXTENT` (0.4) of the box's diagonal, along the plane's own axes; an axis as the segment around the foot of the box's centre, `DATUM_AXIS_EXTENT` (0.6) of the diagonal to either side; a point. They are not in the box the camera frames. The window shows them when `UiState::show.datums` (default on) and hands them to both drawing paths with the marks, like the section: the outline of a plane and an axis are lines of the overlay and a point is a dot (kind `LineKind::Datum`, after the axes and before the sketches, the dot after the sketch points), tested against the bodies like a sketch (dimmed behind a body); a plane is also filled, translucent, *under* the lines: per pixel, the ray through the pixel's centre meets the plane inside the square, in front of the eye and not behind a body (the lines' depth test), and the pixel is blended with the datum colour at `Tokens::datum_fill_opacity`, rounded. On the CPU that is `fill_datum_planes` on the frame before the lines (`compose_with`, so the CPU viewport, the readback path and `ui.screenshot` have it); on the GPU it is a pipeline of its own in the lines pass, before the lines (`Onscreen::upload_overlay` with `Fill`s): one triangle over the picture per plane, whose fragment shader casts the same ray at the pixel's centre in camera coordinates and tests the same depth rule as the lines; a fragment covers all samples of its pixel, so a multisampled pixel resolves to the one value. Without datums nothing of it runs and the picture is the one before, bit for bit (asserted on the CPU; on the GPU the existing parity tests run unchanged). The names are written by egui over the viewport on both paths, beside a corner of a plane, the end of an axis, a point; they are not in `ui.screenshot`. A click passes through a datum to the face behind it. *The tolerance* (`crates/ui-egui/tests/gpu_viewport.rs` › `the_direct_path_draws_datums_and_matches_the_oracle`): the bodies, lines and seams as above; away from those, the fill is compared pixel by pixel except where the plane passes through a surface (where the CPU's and the GPU's depth tests can answer differently: between the lowest and highest threshold either rule can have at the pixel, from the CPU frame's 5 × 5 neighbourhood, widened by the error of the GPU's `f32` point at its grazing angle, by the slack, and by how far the CPU frame's screen-interpolated distance lies off the surface on the faces around the pixel, `(b − a)² / (2 (a + b))` over a triangle whose corners lie at distances `a` to `b`); there a fill pixel may differ by one level more than a body pixel, because how a fixed-function blend rounds into an 8-bit target is the implementation's, and such pixels are not counted against the bodies' rounding bound. *Measured* on a plate with a post, a torus and a ball, two planes (one at 35° about a datum axis), the axis and a point, opaque and see-through at 0.5, at 1000 × 700 points: about 149 000 fill pixels compared per picture and 2 994 marginal ones left out; none differs by more than a level anywhere; fill pixels that differ by a level: Intel Vulkan 189 and 276, Intel OpenGL 59 and 60, lavapipe 76 106 and 61 714 (lavapipe's blend rounds the other way at about half of them); along the lines at most 2 of about 3 260 samples differ (the test allows 1 %; where, was not looked into).

### 7.3 What the oracle is for

In PhotoCraft the CPU compositor defines what the image *is*. Here the model's truth is the B-rep and its mass properties, not the picture. The CPU renderer is the oracle for **display** only: it lets agents and the developer see a model without a GPU, and it catches GPU regressions. Geometric correctness is tested geometrically ([`testing.md`](testing.md)).

---

## 8. Engine (`craft3d-engine`)

### 8.1 `Session`: commands in, events out

```rust
pub struct Session { /* docs, registry, job pool, prefs, services */ }

impl Session {
    pub fn new(services: Services) -> Self;                         // platform functions injected
    pub fn dispatch(&self, doc: Option<DocId>, cmd: CommandInvocation) -> Result<JobHandle, CommandError>;
    pub fn registry(&self) -> &CommandRegistry;
    pub fn snapshot(&self, doc: DocId) -> Arc<DocSnapshot>;         // lock-free read: document + regeneration result
    pub fn events(&self) -> EventReceiver;                          // DocChanged, RegenProgress, FeatureFailed, …
}

pub struct CommandInvocation { pub id: CommandId, pub params: serde_json::Value /* validated against the schema */ }
```

### 8.2 `CommandRegistry`

Each command registers `id` (`"feature.extrude"`), `label`, an optional `menu_path` and shortcut (unused in phase 1), a parameter schema with a one-line params doc agents can read, `enabled(&DocSnapshot) -> bool` with a reason when disabled, and `run`. `run` returns a JSON result and records exactly **one** history step.

Command families (ids are settled when each is implemented, and are stable afterwards):

| Family | Examples |
|---|---|
| `file.*` | `file.new`, `file.open`, `file.save`, `file.close`, `file.export` (the format from the file name or `format`: STEP or a mesh format for every body, DXF or SVG for the outline of a `sketch` or of a flat `face`) |
| `edit.*` | `edit.undo`, `edit.redo` |
| `variable.*` | `variable.set`, `variable.delete`, `variable.rename` |
| `sketch.*` | `sketch.create`, `sketch.addLine`, `sketch.addArc`, `sketch.addCircle`, `sketch.constrain`, `sketch.solve` |
| `datum.*` | `datum.plane`, `datum.axis`, `datum.point` (reference geometry, §4.1 › *Reference geometry*), `datum.references` (what a body offers a datum, with the names) |
| `feature.*` | `feature.primitive.box`, `feature.extrude`, `feature.revolve`, `feature.boolean`, `feature.chamfer` and `feature.fillet` (built 2026-10-14, `engine/src/blend_cmds.rs`), `feature.edit`, `feature.suppress`, `feature.delete`, `feature.reorder` |
| `history.*` | `history.rollback`, `history.regenerate` |
| `document.*` | `document.inspect`, `document.measure` (volume, area, centroid, bounding box, distances), `document.check` (validity report) |
| `view.*` | `view.render` (PNG from a named or explicit camera, CPU or GPU) |

**Inspection is a first-class command.** `document.inspect` returns the feature tree with per-feature status, variables with values, sketches with their degrees of freedom, and bodies with face/edge counts, persistent names and mass properties. An agent must be able to verify what it did from JSON alone, and use `view.render` when it needs to look.

### 8.3 Operations and history (`ops`)

Every mutation of a document is an `Op` (`SetVariable`, `AddFeature`, `EditFeature`, `ReorderFeature`, `AddConstraint`, …). A command produces a labelled `Transaction` of ops. Undo history stores transactions; because documents are persistent data, a "before" state costs only what changed. Undo history (session-only, what the user did) is distinct from the **design history** (the feature list, saved in the file).

### 8.4 Runtime model

```text
 Client (UI / CLI / control / MCP) ──dispatch()──► Engine thread (owns mutable docs, applies ops, publishes snapshots)
        ▲                                               │ spawns
        │ events (changed, progress, failed)            ▼
        │                                   Job pool (rayon): regeneration, tessellation, export, autosave
        │                                   read snapshots, return results
 Viewport: render(snapshot) on GPU or CPU
```

Every job takes a `CancelToken`. Services injected into `Session`: file system (native fs / in-memory for tests / browser later), clock, notifier.

*As built (2026-10-14):* the desktop app has the engine thread of the picture, without the snapshots: `apps/craft3d/src/worker.rs` takes the `Session` itself with one task at a time (a request line, a file chosen in a dialog, or only the scene the window shows), runs it, builds the scene at the shell's display quality, and hands the session back; the window draws the scene of the last sync meanwhile and the progress the task reports. One task at a time keeps requests in order. A panic in a task is caught there, autosaved and reported. On wasm (no app yet) the shell keeps its synchronous path: `Shell::ui` with the session, as tests and examples use it. The scenes of every open document are kept, and of the earlier states its undo history holds, within a memory budget (1.5 GiB by default, the least recently shown first out, the active document's never), so `file.activate` or an undo shows one again without meshing it, in well under a millisecond (a scene's meshes are shared by `Arc`); the display scene is built beside the engine thread while the window shows a quick first picture (`apps/craft3d/src/refine.rs`, §6 › *Coarse first, then sharp*), so a command is never queued behind it; a body's area, volume and centroid are measured once per body (`engine/src/mass.rs`, by the body's `Arc`), and its faces are measured on several threads and summed in face order (`topo::mass_properties`), which is what made opening a model with spline faces slow (the display tolerance is a fraction of the model's size, and the size is the square root of its area).

### 8.5 The UI-swap seam

A frontend needs four things from the core: `Session` (commands in, events out), the `CommandRegistry` (metadata), snapshots with view-models (feature tree, variable table, inspector as plain structs), and a renderer it can host (`gpu::Onscreen` on the host's own device into a texture it shows, or `render` into an RGBA buffer; the egui shell today hosts the first as `ui_egui::direct::Direct` when the window lends it the device, and otherwise `ui_egui::backend::BodyRenderer`, which gives it a `Frame` from the GPU read back or from the CPU). Interactive tools (sketching with the mouse, dragging a face) come after phase 1 and follow PhotoCraft's pattern: pointer events in, ops and overlay primitives out, so the control channel can replay them.

**Litmus test:** `craft3d-cli` and the automation server can do everything the GUI does.

---

## 9. Files

Detail in [`file-formats.md`](file-formats.md).

- **Native `.3dcraft`** (`format`): a bundle (ZIP of STORE entries, or a directory with the same layout) with a versioned `manifest.json` holding the document, and content-addressed blobs for imported bodies and optional regeneration caches. Atomic saves, autosave and crash recovery through the same writer.
- **STEP** (`step` + `io`): export of exact B-rep (AP214 geometry and topology) is built; import later. STEP carries the result, not the history.
- **Meshes** (`meshio` + `io`): STL, 3MF and OBJ export from `tess` output at a caller-chosen tolerance.
- **Flat outlines** (`drawing` + `io`, 2026-10-13): DXF and SVG export of a sketch or of a flat face, as true curves at true size, for laser cutting.

---

## 10. Automation

Detail in [`control-protocol.md`](control-protocol.md).

- **CLI** (`craft3d-cli`): `convert`, `info`, `run` (a list of commands against a document), `commands` (the registry), `render`, `serve` (JSON lines on stdio or loopback TCP) and `mcp`.
- **Control channel:** the desktop app listens on loopback with a token; agents send `engine.execute`, `ui.inspect`, `ui.screenshot`. This is also how the developer watches an agent work in a live window.
- **MCP** (`automation`): `doc_*`, `command_list`, `command_run`, `command_batch`, `doc_render_preview`, generated from the registry; file access limited to explicit read and write roots.
- **Scripts:** a recorded session is `Vec<CommandInvocation>`; replaying it rebuilds the model. This is the natural format for agent-written test scenarios.

---

## 11. Testing and quality

Detail in [`testing.md`](testing.md).

| Layer | What |
|---|---|
| Unit / property | per crate; geometry against closed-form values; proptest for predicates, expression evaluation, persistent structures |
| Kernel invariants | every operation result passes `topo::check`; mass properties against analytic values; boolean identities |
| Tessellation | watertightness, orientation, deviation, volume convergence |
| Regeneration | determinism, incremental = full, save → load → regenerate equality, replay |
| Formats | round trips, malformed-input sweeps, fuzz targets, external validators |
| Renderers | CPU golden images; GPU vs CPU parity |
| Engine | command-level tests; `panic_hunt` fuzzing every command |
| Portability | CI: Linux x64, macOS arm64, Windows x64, wasm32 build check |

---

## 12. Cargo workspace sketch

```toml
[workspace]
resolver = "3"
members = ["crates/*", "apps/*", "xtask"]

[workspace.package]
edition = "2024"
license = "MIT"
# rust-version: pinned to the current stable when M0 scaffolds

[workspace.dependencies]             # versions pinned at scaffold time
serde = { version = "1", features = ["derive"] }
serde_json = "1"
thiserror = "2"
rayon = "1"
arc-swap = "1"
smallvec = "1"
blake3 = "1"
bytemuck = { version = "1", features = ["derive"] }
wgpu = "*"                           # one version shared by gpu and ui
# deliberately absent: any geometry kernel, meshing or triangulation library, nalgebra-style maths
# (math is ours and small; revisit only by a recorded decision)

[workspace.lints.rust]
unsafe_code = "forbid"

[profile.release]
lto = "thin"
codegen-units = 1

[profile.dev]
debug = "line-tables-only"

[profile.dev.package."*"]
opt-level = 2                        # geometry code is unusable at opt-level 0
```

Which general-purpose crates the kernel may use (linear algebra, big-number or interval arithmetic for robust predicates) is an open decision ([`decisions.md`](decisions.md)). The default is: write it, it is part of the kernel.

---

Tracked in [`decisions.md`](decisions.md): target app (if any), licence, UI toolkit confirmation, external maths crates, exact vs tolerant predicates, STEP application protocol, use of open-source tools as external test oracles.
